Chapter 1 · Topic 2

Rate Measure

Tangent & NormalCalculusTopic 1 of 3

Explanation

Concept. Rate measure studies how fast one quantity changes with respect to another, usually time \(t\); if \(x = f(t)\) then \(\frac{dx}{dt}\) is the instantaneous rate of change. When several varying quantities are linked by a relation, differentiate the whole relation implicitly with respect to \(t\) and then substitute the given instantaneous values.
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{dy}{dx}\cdot\frac{dx}{dt}, \qquad V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3 \Rightarrow \frac{dV}{dt} = 4\pi r^2\,\frac{dr}{dt} \]
Key points.
  • instantaneous rate of change of \(f\) w.r.t. \(t\) is \(\frac{d}{dt}f(t)\), evaluated at the instant
  • chain-rule bridge: \(\frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{dy}{dx}\,\frac{dx}{dt}\)
  • express all variables in terms of one independent variable before differentiating
  • substitute given instantaneous values only after differentiating the general relation
  • a decreasing quantity has negative rate; a negative answer means "decreasing"
  • for a circle: \(A = \pi r^2\) gives \(\frac{dA}{dt} = 2\pi r\,\frac{dr}{dt}\)
  • area/volume scale with the square/cube of the length, giving the power in front
Example 1.

A spherical balloon is inflated so that its radius grows at \(3\ \text{cm/s}\). Find how fast the volume increases when the radius is \(10\ \text{cm}\).

  1. \(V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3\) and \(\frac{dr}{dt} = 3\)
  2. differentiate both sides w.r.t. \(t\): \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 4\pi r^2\,\frac{dr}{dt}\)
  3. put \(r = 10\): \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 4\pi (100)(3) = 1200\pi\)

Answer: \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 1200\pi\ \text{cm}^3\text{/s}\)

Example 2.

A \(13\ \text{m}\) ladder rests against a vertical wall; its foot is pulled away at \(2\ \text{m/s}\). How fast does the top slide down when the foot is \(5\ \text{m}\) from the wall?

  1. let \(x\) = distance of foot, \(y\) = height of top: \(x^2 + y^2 = 169\)
  2. implicit differentiation: \(2x\frac{dx}{dt} + 2y\frac{dy}{dt} = 0\)
  3. at \(x = 5\), \(y = \sqrt{169-25} = 12\), with \(\frac{dx}{dt} = 2\)
  4. \(2(5)(2) + 2(12)\frac{dy}{dt} = 0 \Rightarrow \frac{dy}{dt} = -\frac{5}{6}\)

Answer: top slides down at \(\frac{5}{6}\ \text{m/s}\) (negative sign means downward)

Common mistakes.
  • substituting numbers into \(V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3\) before differentiating
  • forgetting the chain factor \(\frac{dr}{dt}\) in compound rate problems
  • dropping the negative sign when a quantity is decreasing

Chapter 1 · Topic 3

Error and Approximation using Differentials

Tangent & NormalCalculusTopic 2 of 3

Explanation

Concept. The differential \(dy = f'(x)\,dx\) gives a linear approximation to the true change \(\Delta y = f(x+\Delta x) - f(x)\) when \(\Delta x\) is small, with \(dx = \Delta x\). This power of calculus computes approximate values of functions and propagated errors without the full computation.
Key formula(s). \[ \Delta y \approx dy = f'(x)\,dx, \qquad f(a + h) \approx f(a) + h\,f'(a), \qquad \text{percentage error of } y = x^n \approx n\frac{|\Delta x|}{|x|}\times 100 \]
Key points.
  • write \(dx = \Delta x\) and compute \(dy = f'(x)\,dx\)
  • linear formula: \(f(a+h) \approx f(a) + h f'(a)\)
  • choose \(a\) where \(f(a)\) is easy and \(|h|\) is tiny
  • relative error \(\approx \left|\frac{dy}{y}\right|\); percentage error = relative error \(\times 100\)
  • power law for errors: \(y = x^n \Rightarrow \frac{dy}{y} = n\,\frac{dx}{x}\)
  • absolute errors add in sums; relative errors add in products
  • the approximation works for small \(h\); the error grows with \(\Delta x\) and with the curvature
Example 1.

Using differentials, find the approximate value of \((26.9)^{1/3}\).

  1. take \(f(x) = x^{1/3}\) with \(a = 27\), \(h = -0.1\); \(f(27) = 3\)
  2. \(f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}x^{-2/3}\), so \(f'(27) = \frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{9} = \frac{1}{27}\)
  3. \(f(26.9) \approx f(27) + h f'(27) = 3 - 0.1\times\frac{1}{27}\)
  4. \(3 - 0.0037 = 2.9963\)

Answer: \((26.9)^{1/3} \approx 2.9963\)

Example 2.

The radius of a sphere is measured as \(7\ \text{cm}\) with a possible error of \(0.1\ \text{cm}\). Estimate the maximum error and the percentage error in the volume.

  1. \(V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3\) gives \(dV = 4\pi r^2\,dr = 4\pi(49)(0.1) = 19.6\pi \approx 61.6\)
  2. percentage error \(= \frac{dV}{V}\times 100 = 3\frac{dr}{r}\times 100 = 3\times\frac{0.1}{7}\times 100\)
  3. \(= \frac{30}{7} \approx 4.29\%\)

Answer: error \(\approx 19.6\pi \approx 61.6\ \text{cm}^3\), percentage error \(\approx 4.29\%\)

Common mistakes.
  • treating \(\Delta y = dy\) as exact instead of approximate
  • forgetting the power coefficient: \(y = x^n\) gives \(\frac{dy}{y} = n\frac{dx}{x}\)
  • evaluating \(f'(x)\) after substituting the perturbed value instead of at the reference point

Chapter 1 · Topic 4

Length of Tangent, Normal, Subtangent and Subnormal

Tangent & NormalCalculusTopic 3 of 3

Explanation

Concept. For a curve \(y = f(x)\) at a fixed point, the tangent and normal meet the \(x\)-axis and cut off segments called the subtangent and subnormal; the segment lengths are the tangent and normal lengths. With slope \(m = \frac{dy}{dx} = \tan\theta\) at the point, all four lengths are compact formulas in \(y\) and \(m\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{ST} = \left|\frac{y}{m}\right|,\quad \text{SN} = |ym|,\quad \text{PT} = \left|\frac{y}{m}\sqrt{1+m^2}\right|,\quad \text{PN} = |y\sqrt{1+m^2}| \]
Key points.
  • subtangent \(\text{ST} = |y\cot\theta|\), subnormal \(\text{SN} = |y\tan\theta|\)
  • ST and SN are the projections of PT and PN on the \(x\)-axis
  • \(x\)-intercept of tangent: \(x - \frac{y}{m}\); of normal: \(x + ym\)
  • tangent is perpendicular to normal: slopes multiply to \(-1\)
  • for the parabola \(y^2 = 4ax\): subtangent \(= 2x\) and subnormal \(= 2a\)
  • lengths are always positive — use absolute values
  • if \(m = 0\), the tangent is horizontal so the subtangent is infinite
Example 1.

Find the lengths of tangent, subtangent, normal and subnormal for the curve \(y = 4x - x^2\) at the point \((1,3)\).

  1. \(m = \frac{dy}{dx} = 4 - 2x\); at \(x = 1\): \(m = 2\), \(y = 3\)
  2. \(\text{ST} = \left|\frac{3}{2}\right| = \frac{3}{2}\), \(\text{SN} = |3(2)| = 6\)
  3. \(\text{PT} = \frac{3}{2}\sqrt{1+4} = \frac{3\sqrt{5}}{2}\), \(\text{PN} = 3\sqrt{1+4} = 3\sqrt{5}\)

Answer: ST \(= \frac{3}{2}\), SN \(= 6\), PT \(= \frac{3\sqrt{5}}{2}\), PN \(= 3\sqrt{5}\)

Example 2.

For the parabola \(y^2 = 4ax\), show that the subtangent is twice the abscissa and the subnormal is constant.

  1. \(m = \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2a}{y}\)
  2. \(\text{ST} = \left|\frac{y}{m}\right| = \frac{y^2}{2a} = \frac{4ax}{2a} = 2x\)
  3. \(\text{SN} = |ym| = 2a\)

Answer: subtangent \(= 2x\), subnormal \(= 2a\) (a constant independent of the point)

Common mistakes.
  • confusing subtangent/subnormal (projections) with the tangent/normal lengths
  • omitting the absolute value and reporting negative lengths
  • substituting the point into \(m\) but then using \(x\) of the point as the "subtangent"

Chapter 2 · Topic 2

Sets and Type of Sets

Sets, Numbers & IntervalsAlgebraTopic 1 of 4

Explanation

Concept. A set is a well-defined collection of distinct objects, written either in roster form (elements listed out) or in set-builder form (a describing condition involving a variable). Sets are classified by type: empty, singleton, finite, infinite, equal, equivalent, subset, and power set. Intervals are special subsets of the real line described with round (open) and square (closed) brackets.
Key formula(s). \[ x \in A \iff P(x)\ \text{is true},\quad n(P(A)) = 2^{n(A)},\quad n(A\cup B) = n(A)+n(B)-n(A\cap B),\quad (a,b),\ [a,b],\ [a,b),\ (-\infty ,a) \]
Key points.
  • roster form: \(\{1,2,3\}\); set-builder form: \(\{x\in\mathbb{Z} : x^2 < 10\}\)
  • order and repetition never matter: \(\{1,2\}=\{2,1\}=\{1,1,2\}\)
  • the empty set \(\varnothing\) has cardinal number \(0\) and is a subset of every set
  • \(A\subseteq B\) means every element of \(A\) lies in \(B\); \(A=B\) means both inclusions hold
  • the power set \(P(A)\) of a set with \(n\) elements contains \(2^n\) subsets
  • \([a,b]\) includes both endpoints while \((a,b)\) excludes them; a set can be finite, countable or infinite
  • two finite sets are equivalent when they have the same number of elements
Example 1.

Write \(\{x\in\mathbb{Z} : -2\le x < 4\}\) in roster form and express it as an interval over the reals.

  1. the integers from -2 through 3 satisfy the condition
  2. roster form: \(\{-2,-1,0,1,2,3\}\)
  3. over the reals the same condition gives the interval \([-2,4)\)

Answer: \(\{-2,-1,0,1,2,3\}\); as reals, \([-2,4)\).

Example 2.

If \(A = \{1,2,3\}\) and \(B = \{x\in\mathbb{N} : x^2 - 4x + 3 = 0\}\), compare \(A\) with \(B\) and find \(n(P(A))\).

  1. \(x^2 - 4x + 3 = (x-1)(x-3) = 0\) gives \(x = 1, 3\)
  2. hence \(B = \{1,3\}\)
  3. \(B\subseteq A\) but \(A\neq B\) because \(2\in A\) while \(2\notin B\)
  4. \(n(P(A)) = 2^3 = 8\)

Answer: \(B\subset A\), \(A\neq B\), and \(|P(A)| = 8\).

Common mistakes.
  • confusing the empty set \(\varnothing\) with \(\{0\}\) and with \(\{\varnothing\}\)
  • attempting to write infinite sets like the irrationals in roster form
  • treating \(\{1,2,3\}\) and \(\{3,2,1\}\) as different sets
  • using \(n(A\cup B)=n(A)+n(B)\) when \(A\cap B\neq\varnothing\)

Chapter 2 · Topic 3

Divisibility Test

Sets, Numbers & IntervalsAlgebraTopic 2 of 4

Explanation

Concept. An integer \(n\) is divisible by \(m\) when dividing \(n\) by \(m\) leaves remainder 0. Divisibility tests read only the last digits or the digit sum of \(n\), giving the answer instantly without performing a full division.
Key formula(s). \[ 2:\ \text{last digit even},\quad 3/9:\ \text{digit sum}\equiv 0,\quad 4:\ \text{last two digits},\quad 5:\ \text{last digit }0\text{ or }5,\quad 6:\ \text{by }2\text{ and }3,\quad 8:\ \text{last three digits},\quad 11:\ \text{alternating digit sum}\equiv 0 \]
Key points.
  • 4 divides \(n\) exactly when 4 divides the number formed by the last two digits
  • 8 divides \(n\) exactly when 8 divides the last three digits
  • 3 and 9 use the digit sum because each \(10^k \equiv 1\) modulo 3 and modulo 9
  • 11's rule: alternating sum of the digits (right to left) must be a multiple of 11
  • composite tests combine coprime factors, e.g. 6 means 2 and 3, 45 means 5 and 9
  • a number is divisible by \(2^k\) iff its last \(k\) digits are
Example 1.

Test whether 72648 is divisible by 4, 8 and 9.

  1. by 4: last two digits are 48 and \(48 = 4\times 12\)
  2. by 8: last three digits are 648 and \(648 = 8\times 81\)
  3. by 9: digit sum \(7+2+6+4+8 = 27 = 3\times 9\)

Answer: 72648 is divisible by 4, 8 and 9.

Example 2.

If the five-digit number \(5x41y\) is divisible by 45, find all possible pairs \((x,y)\).

  1. \(45 = 5\times 9\) so both tests must succeed
  2. by 5: \(y = 0\) or \(y = 5\)
  3. by 9: \(5+x+4+1+y = 10+x+y\) must be a multiple of 9
  4. \(y=0\) gives \(x=8\); \(y=5\) gives \(x=3\)

Answer: \((x,y) = (8,0)\) or \((3,5)\).

Common mistakes.
  • checking only the last digit for divisibility by 4 or 8
  • forgetting that divisibility by 6 needs both 2 and 3
  • testing by a non-coprime factor pair: for 12 use 3 and 4, never 2 and 6
  • applying the 11 digit test from the wrong end of the number

Chapter 2 · Topic 4

Number System

Sets, Numbers & IntervalsAlgebraTopic 3 of 4

Explanation

Concept. Real numbers are classified into natural numbers, whole numbers, integers, rationals, irrationals, primes and composites. A number is rational exactly when its decimal expansion terminates or eventually repeats; otherwise it is irrational and cannot be written as \(\frac{p}{q}\).
Key formula(s). \[ \mathbb{N}\subset\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{R},\quad \mathbb{Q}=\left\{\frac{p}{q}:\ p,q\in\mathbb{Z},\ q\neq 0\right\},\quad \mathbb{I}=\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q},\quad 0.\overline{d_1d_2\cdots d_k}=\frac{\text{(repeated block numerator)}}{(10^k-1)\text{-form denominator}} \]
Key points.
  • terminating decimals (e.g. \(0.75\)) and repeating decimals (e.g. \(0.\overline{18}\)) are rational
  • primes are integers \(>1\) with exactly two divisors; \(2\) is the only even prime
  • \(\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3}, \pi, e\) are irrational; every integer is rational but not vice versa
  • between any two distinct reals there are infinitely many rationals and irrationals
  • to convert \(0.\overline{ab}\) to a fraction multiply by 100 and subtract
  • zero is an integer, a whole number and a rational; it is neither positive nor negative
Example 1.

Classify the numbers \(\frac{7}{13},\ 0.1010010001\ldots,\ -\sqrt{4},\ \pi\) as rational or irrational.

  1. \(7/13 = 0.538461\ldots\) is eventually repeating, hence rational
  2. \(0.1010010001\ldots\) has a non-terminating non-repeating expansion, so it is irrational
  3. \(-\sqrt{4} = -2\), an integer, hence rational
  4. \(\pi\) is irrational

Answer: rational: \(\frac{7}{13}\) and \(-\sqrt{4}\); irrational: \(0.1010010001\ldots\) and \(\pi\).

Example 2.

Express \(0.\overline{12} = 0.121212\ldots\) in the form \(\frac{p}{q}\) with \(q\neq 0\).

  1. let \(x = 0.121212\ldots\)
  2. \(100x = 12.121212\ldots\)
  3. subtract: \(99x = 12\)
  4. \(x = \frac{12}{99} = \frac{4}{33}\)

Answer: \(0.\overline{12} = \frac{4}{33}\).

Common mistakes.
  • declaring every repeated decimal irrational, or every non-terminating decimal rational
  • writing \(\frac{p}{q}\) with \(q = 0\) or with \(p,q\) not coprime
  • believing \(\sqrt{4} = \pm 2\) when \(\sqrt{4}\) denotes the principal root 2
  • assuming all odd numbers are prime (9, 15, 21 are composite)

Chapter 2 · Topic 5

Venn Diagram

Sets, Numbers & IntervalsAlgebraTopic 4 of 4

Explanation

Concept. A Venn diagram pictures sets as circles inside a rectangle that represents the universal set, making union, intersection, complement and symmetric difference visible. Counting problems are solved by inclusion-exclusion: count elements once each, subtracting double-counted overlaps and adding back triple overlaps.
Key formula(s). \[ n(A\cup B) = n(A)+n(B)-n(A\cap B),\quad n(A\cup B\cup C) = n(A)+n(B)+n(C)-n(A\cap B)-n(B\cap C)-n(C\cap A)+n(A\cap B\cap C) \]
Key points.
  • \(A\cup B\) is everything in at least one set; \(A\cap B\) is the common part
  • \(A^\prime = U\setminus A\) (complement) is the region outside \(A\) inside the rectangle
  • \(A\setminus B\) (only \(A\)) is counted as \(n(A) - n(A\cap B)\)
  • for "neither" use \(n(U) - n(A\cup B)\)
  • exactly-two regions exclude the triple intersection: \(n(A\cap B) - n(A\cap B\cap C)\)
  • three-set counting works region by region; start with the triple intersection
Example 1.

In a class of 40 students, 25 play cricket, 20 play football and 10 play both. How many play at least one, and how many play neither?

  1. \(n(C\cup F) = n(C) + n(F) - n(C\cap F) = 25 + 20 - 10 = 35\)
  2. playing at least one game: 35
  3. neither: \(40 - 35 = 5\)

Answer: 35 play at least one and 5 play neither.

Example 2.

Among 100 students: 40 in math, 50 in physics, 45 in chemistry; 15 take math and physics, 10 math and chemistry, 20 physics and chemistry, and 5 take all three. How many study exactly two subjects?

  1. math-physics only: \(15 - 5 = 10\)
  2. math-chemistry only: \(10 - 5 = 5\)
  3. physics-chemistry only: \(20 - 5 = 15\)
  4. total exactly two: \(10+5+15 = 30\)

Answer: 30 students study exactly two subjects.

Common mistakes.
  • adding the pairwise intersections when the triple intersection has already been subtracted once
  • dropping the \(+n(A\cap B\cap C)\) term in the three-set inclusion-exclusion formula
  • shading the complement outside the universal set rectangle
  • double counting the overlap when applying \(n(A\cup B) = n(A)+n(B)\)

A logarithm answers the \( question \) to what power must a base be raised to produce a given number? If \( b^{n} = \) x then n \( = \) log_b(x). For example, because \( 2^{5} = 32 \) we say \( log_{2}(32) = 5 \) The logarithm is therefore the inverse of …

Chapter 3 · Topic 2

Graph of Logarithmic and Exponential Functions

LogarithmAlgebraTopic 1 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The exponential function \(a^x\) and the logarithm \(\log_a x\) are inverse functions, so their graphs are mirror images across the line \(y=x\). Their growth or decay behaviour is decided entirely by whether the base \(a\) satisfies \(a>1\) or \(0<a<1\).
Key formula(s). \[ y = a^x \iff x = \log_a y,\qquad \text{exponential: } a>1\Rightarrow\text{increasing},\ 0<a<1\Rightarrow\text{decreasing},\quad \log_a x\ \text{obeys the same monotonicity in } x \]
Key points.
  • \(y = a^x\): domain \(\mathbb{R}\), range \((0,\infty)\), horizontal asymptote \(y=0\), passes through \((0,1)\)
  • \(y = \log_a x\): domain \((0,\infty)\), range \(\mathbb{R}\), vertical asymptote \(x=0\), passes through \((1,0)\)
  • the two curves are symmetric about \(y=x\), reflecting that they invert each other
  • \(a>1\): both increase; \(0<a<1\): both decrease through \((0,1)\) and \((1,0)\)
  • for \(a>1\), \(\log_a x < 0\) when \(0<x<1\) and \(\log_a x > 0\) when \(x>1\)
  • \(a^x\) (with \(a>1\)) eventually grows faster than every polynomial
Example 1.

Compare \(\log_2 x\) and \(\log_3 x\) for \(0<x<1\) without using a calculator.

  1. both logarithms are negative on \((0,1)\)
  2. at \(x=\frac{1}{2}\): \(\log_2\frac{1}{2} = -1\) while \(\log_3\frac{1}{2}\approx -0.63\)
  3. the graph of \(\log_3 x\) lies above \(\log_2 x\) on \((0,1)\)

Answer: \(\log_2 x < \log_3 x\) for \(0<x<1\).

Example 2.

State the domain, range and asymptote of \(f(x)=\log_2(x-3)\) and of \(g(x)=2^x\).

  1. \(f\) requires \(x-3>0\) so domain is \((3,\infty)\)
  2. range of \(f\) is \(\mathbb{R}\) and it has vertical asymptote \(x=3\)
  3. \(g\): domain \(\mathbb{R}\), range \((0,\infty)\), horizontal asymptote \(y=0\)

Answer: \(f\): \(x>3\), range \(\mathbb{R}\), asymptote \(x=3\); \(g\): domain \(\mathbb{R}\), range \((0,\infty)\), asymptote \(y=0\).

Common mistakes.
  • expecting positive logarithm values on \(0<x<1\)
  • forgetting that bases in \((0,1)\) turn growth into decay
  • swapping the asymptotes: log has a vertical asymptote, exponential has a horizontal one
  • thinking \(\log_a 0\) is a number instead of undefined (the curve only approaches \(x=0\))

Chapter 3 · Topic 3

Characteristic and Mantissa

LogarithmAlgebraTopic 2 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The common logarithm \(\log_{10} x\) is split into an integer part called the characteristic and a non-negative decimal part called the mantissa. The mantissa is read from a log table; the characteristic is fixed by the position of the decimal point.
Key formula(s). \[ \log_{10} x = \text{characteristic} + \text{mantissa},\qquad x\ge 1\ \Rightarrow\ c = d-1\ (d = \text{number of digits}),\qquad 0<x<1\ \Rightarrow\ c = -(z+1)\ (z = \text{zeros just after the decimal point}) \]
Key points.
  • the mantissa always lies in \([0,1)\); negative logarithms are written as (negative characteristic) + (positive mantissa)
  • standard form \(x = m\times 10^k\), \(1\le m<10\): characteristic = \(k\)
  • a 4-digit number has characteristic 3, so \(\log_{10} x \in [3,4)\)
  • \(0.0004 = 4\times 10^{-4}\) has characteristic \(-4\) and mantissa \(\log_{10} 4\approx 0.6021\)
  • the same mantissa is shared by numbers differing by powers of 10, e.g. 2, 20, 200, 0.2
  • when written as the French-style \(\overline{4}.6021\), the dot still separates characteristic from mantissa
Example 1.

If \(\log_{10} x = 3.3617\), how many digits does \(x\) have?

  1. the characteristic is the integer part, 3
  2. for \(x\ge 1\), \(d-1 =\) characteristic
  3. \(d = 3+1 = 4\)

Answer: \(x\) has 4 digits.

Example 2.

Find the characteristic of \(\log_{10}(0.0004)\).

  1. \(0.0004 = 4\times 10^{-4}\)
  2. \(\log_{10}(0.0004) = \log_{10} 4 + \log_{10}10^{-4} = 0.6021 - 4\)
  3. \(= -3.3979\), whose integer part is -4

Answer: characteristic \(= -4\) (mantissa \(0.6021\)).

Common mistakes.
  • writing the characteristic of a number less than 1 as positive or zero
  • quoting a negative total like \(-3.3979\) as characteristic \(-3\) (the integer part is -4)
  • forgetting that the mantissa, though it comes from a table, is never negative
  • using "digits minus one" on a number below 1, where the rule is different

Index / Algebra
Chapter 3 · Topic 4

Definition of Logarithm

LogarithmAlgebraTopic 3 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The logarithm is the inverse of exponentiation: \(\log_a x\) is the power to which the base \(a\) must be raised to obtain \(x\), so the statements \(a^b = x\) and \(\log_a x = b\) mean exactly the same thing. It exists only when the base \(a>0\), \(a\neq 1\), and the argument \(x>0\).
Key formula(s). \[ \log_a x = b \iff a^b = x\quad (a>0,\ a\neq 1,\ x>0),\qquad \log_a a = 1,\quad \log_a 1 = 0,\quad a^{\log_a x} = x,\quad \log_a a^b = b \]
Key points.
  • \(\log_a x\) asks: which exponent of \(a\) equals \(x\)? e.g. \(\log_2 32 = 5\)
  • base must exclude 1 (since \(1^b=1\) for all \(b\)) and must not be negative
  • \(\log_a 1 = 0\) and \(\log_a a = 1\) follow directly from \(a^0=1\), \(a^1=a\)
  • \(\log_a a^k = k\) and \(a^{\log_a x} = x\) are the cancellation identities
  • \(\log_a 0\) and logarithms of negative numbers are undefined over the reals
  • fractional and negative exponents are allowed: \(\log_{1/2} 8 = -3\)
Example 1.

Find \(x\) if \(\log_5 x = 3\).

  1. by definition \(\log_5 x = 3 \iff x = 5^3\)
  2. \(5^3 = 125\)

Answer: \(x = 125\).

Example 2.

Evaluate \(\log_{\sqrt{3}} 81\) and solve \(\log_x 81 = 4\).

  1. \((\sqrt{3})^8 = (3^{1/2})^8 = 3^4 = 81\)
  2. hence \(\log_{\sqrt{3}} 81 = 8\)
  3. \(\log_x 81 = 4 \iff x^4 = 81 = 3^4\)
  4. with \(x>0,\ x\neq 1\) we get \(x = 3\)

Answer: \(8\) and \(x = 3\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(\log_a x\) as \(\frac{x}{a}\) or treating \(\log 8 = 4\) when \(2^3 = 8\)
  • using base 1 or a negative base
  • forgetting that the argument must be positive, e.g. \(\log_2(0)\) is not defined
  • believing \(\log_a a = 0\) instead of \(\log_a a = 1\)
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 3 · Topic 5

Logarithmic and Exponential Inequalities

LogarithmAlgebraTopic 4 of 5

Explanation

Concept. Logarithmic and exponential inequalities are solved by the monotonicity of the function involved: raising both sides of a log inequality like \(\log_a f(x) > c\) preserves the sign when \(a>1\) and flips it when \(0<a<1\). The argument of the logarithm must remain positive throughout.
Key formula(s). \[ \log_a x > b \iff \begin{cases} x > a^b & a>1\\ x < a^b & 0<a<1 \end{cases}\quad(\text{also } x>0),\qquad a^x > b \iff \begin{cases} x > \log_a b & a>1\\ x < \log_a b & 0<a<1 \end{cases} \]
Key points.
  • with base \(>1\): \(\log_a f(x) > c \Rightarrow f(x) > a^c\)
  • with base in \((0,1)\): \(\log_a f(x) > c \Rightarrow f(x) < a^c\) (direction flips)
  • always impose the domain condition \(f(x) > 0\) separately
  • for \(a^x\), the base in \((0,1)\) also reverses inequalities upon taking logs
  • comparing two logarithms with the same arguments needs a base check first
  • combining results: intersect the flip-solved inequality with the positivity domain
Example 1.

Solve \(\log_2 x > 3\).

  1. base \(2>1\), so the inequality direction is kept
  2. \(x > 2^3 = 8\)
  3. the automatic domain \(x>0\) is contained in this region

Answer: \(x\in(8,\infty)\).

Example 2.

Solve \(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{x^2-1} \le \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^3\).

  1. base \(\frac{1}{2}\in(0,1)\), so \(u\le v\) swaps to \(u\ge v\) after dropping the base
  2. \(x^2 - 1 \ge 3\)
  3. \(x^2 \ge 4\)

Answer: \(x\le -2\) or \(x\ge 2\).

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting to flip the inequality when \(0<a<1\)
  • dropping the prescription \(f(x)>0\) (or \(x>0\)) required for the argument
  • taking logarithms of a negative quantity
  • comparing \(a^x\) and \(a^y\) without noting which side of 1 the base lies on

Index / Algebra
Chapter 3 · Topic 6

Properties of Logarithm

LogarithmAlgebraTopic 5 of 5

Explanation

Concept. Logarithms obey four core laws that convert products into sums, quotients into differences, powers into multipliers, and bases into reciprocals. A base change expresses any logarithm in a preferred base, which is indispensable when a problem mixes bases.
Key formula(s). \[ \log_a(mn) = \log_a m + \log_a n,\quad \log_a\left(\frac{m}{n}\right) = \log_a m - \log_a n,\quad \log_a m^n = n\log_a m,\quad \log_a b = \frac{1}{\log_b a},\quad \log_a b = \frac{\log_c b}{\log_c a} \]
Key points.
  • product rule: \(\log_a x + \log_a y = \log_a(xy)\)
  • quotient rule: \(\log_a x - \log_a y = \log_a(x/y)\)
  • power rule: \(\log_a x^k = k\log_a x\); also \(\log_{a^k} x = \frac{1}{k}\log_a x\)
  • base swap: \(\log_a b = \frac{1}{\log_b a}\), hence \(\log_a b\cdot \log_b a = 1\)
  • change of base to \(c\): \(\log_a b = \frac{\log_c b}{\log_c a}\)
  • \(\log_a x = \frac{\log x}{\log a}\) when working with common logs
Example 1.

Simplify \(2\log_2 6 - \log_2 9\).

  1. \(2\log_2 6 = \log_2(6^2) = \log_2 36\)
  2. \(\log_2 36 - \log_2 9 = \log_2\frac{36}{9} = \log_2 4\)
  3. \(\log_2 4 = 2\) since \(2^2 = 4\)

Answer: the simplified value is \(2\).

Example 2.

Given \(a^2 + b^2 = 7ab\) with \(a,b>0\), prove \(\log\left(\frac{a+b}{3}\right) = \frac{\log a + \log b}{2}\).

  1. \(a^2 + 2ab + b^2 = 9ab\), so \((a+b)^2 = 9ab\)
  2. \(a+b>0\) gives \(\frac{a+b}{3} = \sqrt{ab}\)
  3. \(\log\frac{a+b}{3} = \log\sqrt{ab} = \frac{1}{2}\log(ab)\)

Answer: \(\log\left(\frac{a+b}{3}\right) = \frac{\log a + \log b}{2}\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(\log(a+b) = \log a + \log b\) (only products factor this way)
  • converting \(\log a - \log b\) into \(\log(a-b)\)
  • confusing \(\log a^n\) with \((\log a)^n\)
  • forgetting the special case \(\log_a b\cdot\log_b a = 1\) in base-change problems

Introduction & Instructions

An inequality compares two expressions using \( \le \) or \( \ge \) and its solution is the set of values that make the statement true — always an interval or a union of intervals on the number line.

Chapter 4 · Topic 2

Irrational Inequalities

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 1 of 6

Explanation

Concept. An irrational inequality contains a radical such as \(\sqrt{f(x)}\); its solution must respect the domain \(f(x)\ge 0\) and the rule that squaring an inequality is legitimate only when both sides are known to be non-negative. Two distinct cases arise depending on the sign of the other side.
Key formula(s). \[ \sqrt{f(x)} < g(x) \iff 0\le f(x) < g(x)^2\ \text{and}\ g(x)>0,\qquad \sqrt{f(x)} > g(x) \iff f(x)\ge 0\ \text{and}\ \big(g(x)<0\ \text{or}\ f(x) > g(x)^2\big) \]
Key points.
  • first impose the radicand condition \(f(x)\ge 0\)
  • if the right side is negative and the radical non-negative, the inequality \(\sqrt{f}>g\) holds automatically
  • squaring is safe when both sides are non-negative; never square unknown signs blindly
  • \(\sqrt{f(x)} < g(x)\) requires \(g(x)>0\) in addition to \(f(x) < g(x)^2\)
  • factor the resulting polynomial and use a sign chart on the number line
  • always verify the final region against the domain
Example 1.

Solve \(\sqrt{x+1} < 2\).

  1. right side is positive, so both sides may be squared
  2. \(x+1 < 4\)
  3. \(x<3\), combined with radicand \(x+1\ge 0\) giving \(x\ge -1\)

Answer: \(x\in[-1,3)\).

Example 2.

Solve \(\sqrt{x+1} > x-3\).

  1. domain: \(x+1\ge 0\) so \(x\ge -1\)
  2. if \(x<3\), the right side is negative and the inequality holds automatically
  3. if \(x\ge 3\), square: \(x+1 > (x-3)^2\) giving \(x^2 - 7x + 8 < 0\)
  4. the quadratic is negative for \(3\le x < \frac{7+\sqrt{17}}{2}\)

Answer: \(x\in\left[-1,\ \frac{7+\sqrt{17}}{2}\right)\).

Common mistakes.
  • blind squaring when the sign of the other side is unknown
  • forgetting the radicand condition \(f(x)\ge 0\)
  • discarding the automatic case \(g(x)<0\) in \(\sqrt{f(x)}>g(x)\)
  • treating \(\sqrt{f(x)}<g(x)\) as equivalent to \(f(x)<g(x)^2\) alone
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 4 · Topic 3

Logarithmic and Exponential Inequalities

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 2 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Logarithmic and exponential inequalities combine two monotonicities: powers increase with their exponent when the base exceeds 1 and decrease when the base lies in \((0,1)\), and logarithms inherit the same sense from their base. Every solution must respect the base restrictions \(a>0,\ a\neq 1\) and the positivity of the log's argument.
Key formula(s). \[ a^x < b \iff \begin{cases} x < \log_a b & a>1\\ x > \log_a b & 0<a<1 \end{cases},\qquad \log_a f(x) > 0 \iff \begin{cases} f(x)>1 & a>1\\ 0<f(x)<1 & 0<a<1 \end{cases} \]
Key points.
  • an exponential \(a^x\) with \(a>1\) is strictly increasing; with \(0<a<1\) strictly decreasing
  • \(a^x > 0\) for every real \(x\), so no sign case ever needs to be split
  • for a log inequality the argument must stay positive and the base must differ from 1
  • a variable base, e.g. \(\log_{g(x)} h(x) > 0\), requires a case split on \(g(x)>1\) vs \(0<g(x)<1\)
  • converting \(a^x = b\) to \(x = \log_a b\) sets the boundary of the interval
Example 1.

Solve \(2^x > 8\).

  1. \(8 = 2^3\)
  2. base 2 is greater than 1, so the direction is preserved
  3. \(x > 3\)

Answer: \(x\in(3,\infty)\).

Example 2.

Solve \(\log_{x-1}(2x-3) > 0\).

  1. domain: \(x-1>0,\ x-1\ne 1,\ 2x-3>0\) so \(x>\frac{3}{2},\ x\ne 2\)
  2. base \(>1\) (\(x>2\)): need \(2x-3 > 1\), giving \(x>2\), already true
  3. base in \((0,1)\) (\(\frac{3}{2}<x<2\)): need \(0<2x-3<1\), giving \(\frac{3}{2}<x<2\)

Answer: \(x\in\left(\frac{3}{2},2\right)\cup(2,\infty)\).

Common mistakes.
  • assuming the base is always greater than 1 and never flipping the inequality
  • forgetting that the base may not equal 1
  • ignoring the positivity of the argument and of the base
  • treating \(a^{f(x)}<a^{g(x)}\) without checking where \(a\) sits relative to 1
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 4 · Topic 4

Modulus Inequalities

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 3 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Modulus (absolute value) measures distance from zero, so \(|x-a|\) is the distance from \(a\). Inequalities like \(|f(x)|<b\) mean \(f(x)\) stays inside the strip \(-b<f(x)<b\), while \(|f(x)|>b\) pushes \(f(x)\) to either side; squaring both sides turns a modulus inequality into a polynomial one when both sides are non-negative.
Key formula(s). \[ |x-a| < b \iff a-b < x < a+b,\qquad |x-a| > b \iff x<a-b\ \text{or}\ x>a+b,\qquad |f(x)|<|g(x)| \iff f(x)^2 < g(x)^2 \]
Key points.
  • \(|x| = x\) for \(x\ge 0\) and \(|x| = -x\) for \(x<0\)
  • \(|f(x)|<b\) requires \(b>0\) and decouples as \(-b<f(x)<b\)
  • \(|f(x)|>b\) with \(b>0\) means \(f(x)>b\) or \(f(x)<-b\); a union of two intervals
  • squaring is valid here because both sides of a modulus inequality are non-negative
  • \(|x-a|\) inequalities read as distance: \(|x-3|<2\) means \(1<x<5\)
  • sign charts resolve \(|f(x)|\) piecewise where \(f\) changes sign
Example 1.

Solve \(|2x-3|\le 5\).

  1. expand: \(-5\le 2x-3\le 5\)
  2. add 3: \(-2\le 2x\le 8\)
  3. divide by 2: \(-1\le x\le 4\)

Answer: \(x\in[-1,4]\).

Example 2.

Solve \(|x| > 2|x-1|\).

  1. both sides are non-negative, so square: \(x^2 > 4(x-1)^2\)
  2. \(x^2 > 4x^2 - 8x + 4\), i.e. \(3x^2 - 8x + 4 < 0\)
  3. \((3x-2)(x-2) < 0\)

Answer: \(x\in\left(\frac{2}{3},2\right)\).

Common mistakes.
  • dropping the modulus when squaring and writing \(|f|<|g|\) as \(f<g\)
  • forgetting that \(|f(x)|<b\) is untangled only when \(b>0\)
  • turning \(|f(x)|>b\) into \(-b<f(x)<b\)
  • losing the union when \(|x-a|>b\) collapses into a single interval
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 4 · Topic 5

Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 4 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality bounds the dot product by the product of the norms: the square of a sum of products never exceeds the product of the sums of squares. Equality holds exactly when the two sequences \((a_i)\) and \((b_i)\) are proportional.
Key formula(s). \[ \left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i b_i\right)^2 \le \left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} b_i^2\right),\qquad \text{equality} \iff a_1:b_1 = a_2:b_2 = \cdots = a_n:b_n \]
Key points.
  • the inequality is valid for all real \(a_i, b_i\), with no sign restrictions
  • in vector form it reads \((\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})^2 \le \|\mathbf{a}\|^2\|\mathbf{b}\|^2\)
  • choosing \(b_i = 1\) yields \(\left(\sum a_i\right)^2 \le n\sum a_i^2\)
  • it is the sharpest bound: equality occurs on the proportional sequences
  • useful for minimising \(x^2+y^2+\cdots\) under a linear constraint
  • also written as \(\left|\sum a_ib_i\right|\le \sqrt{\sum a_i^2}\ \sqrt{\sum b_i^2}\)
Example 1.

If \(x+y=2\), find the minimum of \(x^2+y^2\).

  1. apply C-S to \((x,y)\) and \((1,1)\): \((x^2+y^2)(1^2+1^2)\ge (x\cdot 1+y\cdot 1)^2\)
  2. \(2(x^2+y^2)\ge (x+y)^2 = 4\)
  3. \(x^2+y^2\ge 2\), with equality at \(x=y=1\)

Answer: the minimum value is \(2\).

Example 2.

Prove \((a+b+c)^2 \le 3(a^2+b^2+c^2)\).

  1. take \((a_1,a_2,a_3) = (a,b,c)\) and \((b_1,b_2,b_3) = (1,1,1)\)
  2. C-S: \((a+b+c)^2 \le (a^2+b^2+c^2)(1+1+1)\)

Answer: \((a+b+c)^2 \le 3(a^2+b^2+c^2)\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing the inequality with the direction reversed
  • imposing positivity on the entries, which C-S does not require
  • applying the equality condition to non-proportional tuples
  • confusing \((ab+cd)^2\) with \((a^2+c^2)(b^2+d^2)\) without the inequality sign
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 4 · Topic 6

Jensen's Inequality

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 5 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Jensen's inequality compares the value of a convex function at the average with the average of its values: for convex \(f\), \(f\) of the mean is at most the mean of the \(f\)-values, and for concave \(f\) the inequality reverses. It generalises the midpoint convexity property to \(n\) points and to weighted averages.
Key formula(s). \[ f\ \text{convex:}\quad f\left(\frac{\sum x_i}{n}\right)\le \frac{\sum f(x_i)}{n},\qquad f\ \text{concave:}\quad f\left(\frac{\sum x_i}{n}\right)\ge \frac{\sum f(x_i)}{n},\qquad \text{two-variable form:}\ f\left(\frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\right)\le \frac{f(x_1)+f(x_2)}{2} \]
Key points.
  • a function with \(f''(x)\ge 0\) on an interval is convex there; \(f''(x)\le 0\) means concave
  • convex examples: \(x^2, e^x, |x|\); concave examples on the right domains: \(\sin x, \log x\)
  • equality holds when all \(x_i\) are equal
  • Jensen handles maxima/minima of symmetric expressions in angles and sides
  • in a triangle, \(A+B+C=\pi\) makes \((A+B+C)/3 = \pi/3\) a fixed average
  • weighted Jensen replaces the mean with \(\sum w_i f(x_i)\ge f(\sum w_i x_i)\) for convex \(f\)
Example 1.

For \(x,y>0\) with \(x+y=2\), prove \(x^2+y^2\ge 2\).

  1. \(f(t)=t^2\) is convex, so \(f((x+y)/2)\le (f(x)+f(y))/2\)
  2. \((x+y)/2 = 1\), hence \(1 \le (x^2+y^2)/2\)
  3. equality at \(x=y=1\)

Answer: \(x^2+y^2\ge 2\).

Example 2.

In a triangle, prove \(\sin A+\sin B+\sin C\le \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\).

  1. \(\sin t\) is concave on \((0,\pi)\) and \(A+B+C=\pi\)
  2. Jensen: \(\frac{\sin A+\sin B+\sin C}{3}\le \sin\left(\frac{A+B+C}{3}\right)\)
  3. \(=\sin\frac{\pi}{3}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\)

Answer: \(\sin A+\sin B+\sin C\le \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\), equality at \(A=B=C=60^\circ\).

Common mistakes.
  • using Jensen on a concave function exactly as on a convex one
  • applying weighted and unweighted Jensen forms interchangeably
  • forgetting equality corresponds to all points equal
  • using the inequality outside the interval on which the convexity/concavity holds
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 4 · Topic 7

Inequalities Based on RMS ≥ AM ≥ GM ≥ HM

InequalitiesAlgebraTopic 6 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Among several classical averages of positive numbers, the root-mean-square (RMS), arithmetic mean (AM), geometric mean (GM) and harmonic mean (HM) are always ordered as RMS \(\ge\) AM \(\ge\) GM \(\ge\) HM. All four are equal exactly when every number in the collection is the same.
Key formula(s). \[ \sqrt{\frac{x_1^2+\cdots+x_n^2}{n}}\ \ge\ \frac{x_1+\cdots+x_n}{n}\ \ge\ (x_1x_2\cdots x_n)^{1/n}\ \ge\ \frac{n}{\frac{1}{x_1}+\cdots+\frac{1}{x_n}}\ ,\qquad \text{equality iff } x_1=x_2=\cdots=x_n \]
Key points.
  • only positive (or non-negative) numbers are allowed for GM and HM
  • AM-GM for two numbers: \(\frac{a+b}{2}\ge\sqrt{ab}\)
  • AM-HM for two numbers: \(\frac{a+b}{2}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}\), equivalent to \((a-b)^2\ge 0\)
  • RMS lies above AM because it penalises spread from the mean
  • all means are shift-and-scale compatible in different ways; equality happens at one point only
  • AM-GM gives quick bounds like \(x+\frac{1}{x}\ge 2\) for \(x>0\)
Example 1.

If \(ab=1\) with \(a,b>0\), find the minimum of \(a+b\).

  1. AM \(\ge\) GM gives \(\frac{a+b}{2}\ge \sqrt{ab} = 1\)
  2. \(a+b\ge 2\), equality when \(a=b=1\)

Answer: the minimum is \(2\).

Example 2.

For \(0<x<1\), find the maximum of \(x(1-x)\).

  1. apply AM-GM to \(x\) and \(1-x\)
  2. \(\frac{x+(1-x)}{2}\ge \sqrt{x(1-x)}\)
  3. \(\frac{1}{2}\ge \sqrt{x(1-x)}\) so \(x(1-x)\le \frac{1}{4}\)

Answer: maximum \(\frac{1}{4}\), attained at \(x=\frac{1}{2}\).

Common mistakes.
  • ordering the means backwards, e.g. claiming AM \(\le\) GM
  • applying GM or HM to negative numbers
  • expecting equality in AM-GM without all entries being equal
  • forgetting that RMS is the top of the chain, not the bottom
Back to Index

Introduction & Instructions

A quadratic equation in one variable has the standard form a \( x^{2} + \) b x \( + \) c \( = 0 \) with a \( \ne 0 \) where a, b and c are real constants. The highest power of x is \( 2 \) so the equation has at most two solutions, called its roots; if …

Chapter 5 · Topic 2

Equation of Degree Two in Two Variables

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 1 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A general equation of degree two in two variables has the form \(ax^2+2hxy+by^2+2gx+2fy+c=0\) and, depending on its coefficients, represents a conic, a circle, or a pair of lines. When the quadratic part is homogeneous, it can factorise into two straight lines through the origin.
Key formula(s). \[ ax^2+2hxy+by^2+2gx+2fy+c=0,\qquad \text{homogeneous: } ax^2+2hxy+by^2=0\iff a t^2 + 2h t + b = 0\ \text{with } t=\frac{y}{x},\qquad x^2+y^2=r^2\ \text{is the circle of radius } r \]
Key points.
  • the circle case \(x^2+y^2=r^2\) corresponds to equal coefficients with no \(xy\) term
  • putting \(t=\frac{y}{x}\) reduces a homogeneous quadratic to an ordinary quadratic in \(t\)
  • the pair of lines through the origin are real when the discriminant of that quadratic is non-negative
  • a general second-degree equation factors as \((l_1x+m_1y+n_1)(l_2x+m_2y+n_2)=0\) when it splits
  • the discriminant-like condition for a conic comes from the full \(3\times 3\) coefficient matrix
  • tool: symmetric systems combine \((x+y)^2 = x^2+y^2+2xy\)
Example 1.

Factorise the homogeneous equation \(x^2 - 5xy + 6y^2 = 0\) into linear factors.

  1. \(x^2 - 5xy + 6y^2 = (x-2y)(x-3y)\)
  2. hence \(x-2y=0\) or \(x-3y=0\)
  3. the two lines are \(x=2y\) and \(x=3y\)

Answer: the equation represents the lines \(x=2y\) and \(x=3y\).

Example 2.

Solve the system \(x^2+y^2=25,\ xy=12\) over the reals.

  1. \((x+y)^2 = x^2+y^2+2xy = 25+24 = 49\)
  2. \(x+y=\pm 7\) and \(xy=12\)
  3. \(x+y=7,\ xy=12\) gives \((3,4)\) and \((4,3)\)
  4. \(x+y=-7\) gives \((-3,-4)\) and \((-4,-3)\)

Answer: \((x,y)=(3,4),(4,3),(-3,-4),(-4,-3)\).

Common mistakes.
  • dropping the \(\pm\) branch when taking square roots of \((x+y)^2\)
  • assuming \(x,y\) are positive, losing the negative pairs
  • treating a homogeneous quadratic as a single line through the origin
  • forgetting to check the discriminant before declaring two real lines
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 3

Modulus Inequalities

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 2 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Modulus combined with a quadratic often hides inside algebraic manipulation: since the modulus is non-negative, \(|f(x)|<b\) becomes the two-sided inequality \(-b<f(x)<b\), and a quadratic inside the bars can be factored after making the substitution \(t=|x|\). Equations of the form \(|g(x)|=k\) are split into \(g(x)=k\) and \(g(x)=-k\).
Key formula(s). \[ |ax^2+bx+c| \le k \iff -k \le ax^2+bx+c \le k,\qquad |ax^2+bx+c| = k \iff ax^2+bx+c = \pm k,\qquad x^2 = |x|^2 \]
Key points.
  • \(|F(x)|\le k\) is two simultaneous linear/quadratic inequalities, not one
  • \(|F(x)|\ge k\) splits into \(F(x)\ge k\) or \(F(x)\le -k\), a union
  • for even expressions, \(x^2 - 3|x| + 2\) becomes \(t^2 - 3t + 2\) under \(t=|x|\ge 0\)
  • the substitution keeps only non-negative roots \(t\ge 0\)
  • \(|x^2 - a|\) splits at the two roots of \(x^2-a=0\) on the sign chart
  • check the quadratic's discriminant before trusting its factorisation
Example 1.

Solve \(|x^2-4|\le 5\).

  1. \(|u|\le 5\) gives \(-5\le x^2-4 \le 5\)
  2. left side \(x^2\ge -1\) holds automatically
  3. right side \(x^2\le 9\) gives \(|x|\le 3\)

Answer: \(x\in[-3,3]\).

Example 2.

Solve \(x^2 - 3|x| + 2 < 0\).

  1. put \(t=|x|\ge 0\): then \(t^2 - 3t + 2 < 0\)
  2. \((t-1)(t-2)<0\) so \(1<t<2\)
  3. \(1<|x|<2\)

Answer: \(x\in(-2,-1)\cup(1,2)\).

Common mistakes.
  • replacing \(|x|\) by \(x\) and solving only the positive half
  • keeping negative roots of \(t=|x|\ge 0\) after substitution
  • collapsing \(-k\le F(x)\le k\) into just the upper bound
  • forgetting that \(|F|=k\) means the union \(F=k\) or \(F=-k\)
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 4

Graph of Quadratic Expression

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 3 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The graph of \(y=ax^2+bx+c\) is a parabola opening upward when \(a>0\) and downward when \(a<0\), with vertex at \(x=-\frac{b}{2a}\). The sign of the discriminant fixes the number of real roots, and completing the square locates both the vertex and the range.
Key formula(s). \[ y = a(x-h)^2 + k,\quad h=-\frac{b}{2a},\quad k = \frac{4ac-b^2}{4a} = -\frac{D}{4a},\qquad \text{axis: } x=-\frac{b}{2a},\qquad D=b^2-4ac \]
Key points.
  • \(a>0\): the parabola has a minimum; \(a<0\): it has a maximum
  • the axis of symmetry is the vertical line through the vertex
  • sign of \(f(x)\) outside and inside the roots flips at every simple root
  • \(f(x)>0\) for all \(x\) requires \(a>0\) and \(D<0\)
  • scaling \(x\to x-h\) shifts the zeros by \(h\)
  • \(y\)-intercept is \(f(0)=c\)
Example 1.

Find the vertex and axis of the parabola \(y=x^2-4x+3\).

  1. \(h = -\frac{b}{2a} = \frac{4}{2} = 2\)
  2. \(k = f(2) = 4-8+3 = -1\)
  3. \(a=1>0\), so it opens upward

Answer: vertex \((2,-1)\), axis \(x=2\).

Example 2.

For which \(k\) is \((k-2)x^2 - (k-2)x + 2 \ge 0\) for all real \(x\)?

  1. positivity for all \(x\) needs leading coefficient \(>0\): \(k>2\)
  2. and no sign change: \(D<0\), i.e. \((k-2)^2 - 8(k-2) < 0\)
  3. \((k-2)(k-10)<0\) so \(2<k<10\)

Answer: \(k\in(2,10)\); note the degenerate \(k=2\) also works since it gives the constant 2.

Common mistakes.
  • writing the vertex \(x\)-coordinate as \(-\frac{b}{a}\)
  • miscomputing \(k=\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}\) including its sign
  • declaring \(f(x)>0\) for all \(x\) from \(a>0\) alone without checking \(D\)
  • ignoring the degenerate case where the leading coefficient vanishes
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 5

Relation Between Roots

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 4 of 10

Explanation

Concept. For roots \(\alpha,\beta\) of \(ax^2+bx+c=0\), Vieta's relations express the sum and product of the roots in the coefficients: \(\alpha+\beta=-\frac{b}{a}\) and \(\alpha\beta=\frac{c}{a}\). Symmetric expressions in the roots are evaluated through these two quantities without ever finding the roots.
Key formula(s). \[ \alpha+\beta=-\frac{b}{a},\qquad \alpha\beta=\frac{c}{a},\qquad \alpha^2+\beta^2=(\alpha+\beta)^2-2\alpha\beta=\frac{b^2-2ac}{a^2},\qquad \frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta}=\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha\beta} \]
Key points.
  • only symmetric expressions are reachable purely from \(S=\alpha+\beta\) and \(P=\alpha\beta\)
  • \(\alpha^3+\beta^3 = (\alpha+\beta)^3 - 3\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)\)
  • a quadratic with roots \(\alpha',\beta'\) is \(x^2 - (\alpha'+\beta')x + \alpha'\beta' = 0\)
  • shifted roots, e.g. \(\alpha+t,\ \beta+t\), keep the sum shifted and the product recomputed
  • the sum appears with a minus sign; the product keeps the sign of \(c/a\)
  • replacing half the sum keeps trace of the discriminant: \(\alpha-\beta = \pm\frac{\sqrt{D}}{a}\)
Example 1.

For \(x^2-5x+6=0\), find \(\alpha^2+\beta^2\) and \(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta}\).

  1. \(\alpha+\beta = 5,\ \alpha\beta = 6\)
  2. \(\alpha^2+\beta^2 = 25 - 2\cdot 6 = 13\)
  3. \(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta} = \frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha\beta} = \frac{5}{6}\)

Answer: \(13\) and \(\frac{5}{6}\).

Example 2.

Given roots \(\alpha,\beta\) of \(x^2-7x+12=0\), form the quadratic whose roots are \(\alpha+1\) and \(\beta+1\).

  1. \(\alpha+\beta=7,\ \alpha\beta=12\)
  2. new sum: \(\alpha+1+\beta+1 = 9\)
  3. new product: \(\alpha\beta+\alpha+\beta+1 = 12+7+1 = 20\)

Answer: \(x^2 - 9x + 20 = 0\).

Common mistakes.
  • dropping the negative sign in front of \(b/a\) for the sum
  • writing the product as \(c/a\) with the wrong sign or with \(b\)
  • attempting to evaluate non-symmetric expressions without extra data
  • confusing the sum of roots with the coefficient of \(x\)
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 6

Theory of Equations

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 5 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The theory of equations states that a degree-\(n\) polynomial has exactly \(n\) roots counted with multiplicity, and pairs each coefficient with an elementary symmetric sum of the roots. The remainder and factor theorems provide shortcuts for detecting roots and factorising.
Key formula(s). \[ a_0x^n + a_1x^{n-1} + \cdots + a_n = 0:\quad \sum \alpha_i = -\frac{a_1}{a_0},\quad \sum_{i<j}\alpha_i\alpha_j = \frac{a_2}{a_0},\quad \cdots,\quad \alpha_1\alpha_2\cdots\alpha_n = (-1)^n\frac{a_n}{a_0} \]
Key points.
  • remainder theorem: a polynomial divided by \(x-k\) leaves remainder \(f(k)\)
  • factor theorem: \(x-k\) is a factor exactly when \(f(k)=0\)
  • a cubic \(x^3+px+q\) with roots \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\): \(\alpha\beta+\beta\gamma+\gamma\alpha=0\)
  • product of the roots carries the sign \((-1)^n\)
  • complex roots of a real polynomial occur in conjugate pairs
  • a degree-two-in-two-variables identity from \(x^3 = 1\) uses the non-real cube roots of unity
Example 1.

Show \(x+1\) divides \(x^3+3x^2+x-1\) and factorise the cubic.

  1. \(f(-1) = -1+3-1-1 = 0\), so \(x+1\) is a factor by the factor theorem
  2. divide: \(x^3+3x^2+x-1 = (x+1)(x^2+2x-1)\)

Answer: \((x+1)(x^2+2x-1)\).

Example 2.

If \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\) are the roots of \(x^3-x^2-4x+4=0\), find \(\alpha^2+\beta^2+\gamma^2\).

  1. \(\alpha+\beta+\gamma = 1\), \(\alpha\beta+\beta\gamma+\gamma\alpha = -4\)
  2. \(\alpha^2+\beta^2+\gamma^2 = (\alpha+\beta+\gamma)^2 - 2(\alpha\beta+\beta\gamma+\gamma\alpha)\)
  3. \(= 1 - 2(-4) = 9\)

Answer: \(\alpha^2+\beta^2+\gamma^2 = 9\).

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting the alternating sign \((-1)^n\) on the product of the roots
  • using the quadratic Vieta relations blindly on a cubic
  • overlooking a repeated root when counting multiplicities
  • applying the factor theorem in the wrong direction (factors force zero, not the reverse statement alone)
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 7

Common Roots

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 6 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Two quadratics \(a_1x^2+b_1x+c_1=0\) and \(a_2x^2+b_2x+c_2=0\) possess a common root exactly when a certain eliminant relation holds among their coefficients. If both roots are common, the polynomials are numerically proportional.
Key formula(s). \[ (c_1a_2-c_2a_1)^2 = (a_1b_2-a_2b_1)(b_1c_2-b_2c_1)\quad (\text{one common root}),\qquad \frac{a_1}{a_2} = \frac{b_1}{b_2} = \frac{c_1}{c_2}\quad (\text{both common}) \]
Key points.
  • the common root also satisfies the difference of the two equations
  • the eliminant follows by eliminating the square term between the two equations
  • when one root is visibly known, substitute it into the other quadratic
  • both-common-roots means the second equation is a scalar multiple of the first
  • if the two quadratics are identical up to scale, all roots are shared
  • the case \(a_1b_2 = a_2b_1\) needs separate inspection of the constant scenario
Example 1.

Find \(k\) if \(x^2-kx+4=0\) and \(x^2+3x-4=0\) share a common root.

  1. \(x^2+3x-4 = (x+4)(x-1)\), so its roots are \(-4\) and \(1\)
  2. common root \(1\): \(1-k+4=0\), giving \(k=5\)
  3. common root \(-4\): \(16+4k+4=0\), giving \(k=-5\)

Answer: \(k=5\) or \(k=-5\).

Example 2.

Find \(k\) if \(x^2-kx+9=0\) and \(x^2-5x+6=0\) have a common root.

  1. \(x^2-5x+6 = (x-2)(x-3)\), roots \(2\) and \(3\)
  2. \(x=2\): \(4-2k+9=0\) so \(k=\frac{13}{2}\)
  3. \(x=3\): \(9-3k+9=0\) so \(k=6\)

Answer: \(k=\frac{13}{2}\) or \(k=6\).

Common mistakes.
  • testing only one root of the simpler quadratic
  • confusing "common root" with "equal discriminants"
  • using the proportionality test for both roots when only one root overlaps
  • dividing by expressions that could vanish, e.g. by \(x\) assuming the root is non-zero
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 8

Nature of Roots

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 7 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The nature of the roots of \(ax^2+bx+c=0\) is decided by the discriminant \(D=b^2-4ac\): positive \(D\) gives two distinct real roots, zero gives a repeated real root, and negative \(D\) gives two complex conjugate roots. Whether the real roots are rational or irrational depends on the perfect-square status of \(D\) relative to \(a\).
Key formula(s). \[ x=\frac{-b\pm\sqrt{D}}{2a},\qquad D=b^2-4ac:\quad D>0 \Rightarrow \text{distinct real},\quad D=0 \Rightarrow \text{equal},\quad D<0 \Rightarrow \text{conjugate complex} \]
Key points.
  • rational real factors give a square discriminant; irrational roots appear when \(D>0\) is not an integer square
  • \(D\ge 0\) is needed before discussing products or sums of real roots
  • a quadratic with rational coefficients and a complex root takes both conjugates
  • for equality of both roots, the polynomial is a pure square \(a(x+\frac{b}{2a})^2\)
  • \(D\) is the same for any scalar multiple of the equation
  • an AM-GM check: two real roots imply \(b^2\ge 4ac\)
Example 1.

Without solving, state the nature of the roots of \(3x^2-5x+2=0\).

  1. \(D = (-5)^2 - 4\cdot 3\cdot 2 = 25-24 = 1\)
  2. \(D>0\) and \(1\) is a perfect square
  3. roots are real, distinct and rational

Answer: two distinct rational real roots.

Example 2.

Find \(k\) so that \(x^2-(k+1)x+4=0\) has equal roots.

  1. equal roots need \(D=0\): \((k+1)^2 - 16 = 0\)
  2. \((k+1)^2 = 16\)
  3. \(k+1=\pm 4\)

Answer: \(k=3\) or \(k=-5\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(D=b^2+4ac\)
  • claiming irrational roots whenever \(D>0\), ignoring that a perfect-square \(D\) gives rational roots
  • treating a negative discriminant as "no real roots" without the complex-character conclusion
  • forgetting that if one root is complex in a real-coefficient quadratic, the other is its conjugate
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 9

Newton's Formula

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 8 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Newton's formula generates the power sums \(S_n=\alpha^n+\beta^n\) of the roots of a quadratic recursively from the two symmetric quantities \(S_1=\alpha+\beta\) and \(\alpha\beta\), so high powers are computed quickly through a recurrence instead of explicit expansion.
Key formula(s). \[ S_0=2,\qquad S_1=\alpha+\beta,\qquad S_n=(\alpha+\beta)S_{n-1}-\alpha\beta\,S_{n-2}\quad (n\ge 2),\qquad S_n=\alpha^n+\beta^n \]
Key points.
  • the recurrence mirrors the characteristic equation satisfied by both roots
  • \(S_0=2\), since each of the two roots contributing \(1\) to the zeroth power
  • check small cases: \(S_2=S_1^2-2\alpha\beta\), \(S_3=S_1S_2-\alpha\beta S_1\)
  • the recurrence remains valid when the roots are complex
  • for a cubic, the analogous relation involves three equal symmetric sums
  • sequential computation \(S_1,S_2,S_3,\dots\) renders arbitrarily high powers in linear time
Example 1.

For the roots of \(x^2-3x+2=0\), compute \(\alpha^5+\beta^5\) using Newton's recurrence.

  1. \(S_1=3,\ \alpha\beta=2,\ S_0=2\)
  2. \(S_2=3\cdot 3 - 2\cdot 2 = 5\), \(S_3=3\cdot 5 - 2\cdot 3 = 9\)
  3. \(S_4=3\cdot 9 - 2\cdot 5 = 17\)
  4. \(S_5=3\cdot 17 - 2\cdot 9 = 33\)

Answer: \(\alpha^5+\beta^5=33\), indeed \(1^5+2^5=33\).

Example 2.

For \(x^2-x-1=0\), find \(\alpha^4+\beta^4\).

  1. \(S_1=1,\ \alpha\beta=-1\)
  2. \(S_2=1\cdot 1-(-1)\cdot 2=3\)
  3. \(S_3=1\cdot 3-(-1)\cdot 1=4\), \(S_4=1\cdot 4-(-1)\cdot 3=7\)

Answer: \(\alpha^4+\beta^4=7\).

Common mistakes.
  • initialising \(S_0=1\) instead of \(S_0=2\)
  • misplacing the sign of the \(\alpha\beta\) term in the recurrence
  • advancing the recursion index incorrectly when the roots coincide in a pair case
  • trusting a power sum without a small-case cross-check
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 10

Location of Roots

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 9 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Location of roots problems pin the real roots of \(ax^2+bx+c=0\) relative to a point \(k\) using three conditions: the discriminant, the sign of \(a\cdot f(k)\), and the position of the axis of the parabola. The sign of \(af(k)\) alone decides whether \(k\) lies between the two roots or strictly outside them.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{both roots }> k:\ D\ge 0,\ af(k)>0,\ -\frac{b}{2a}>k,\qquad \text{both roots }< k:\ D\ge 0,\ af(k)>0,\ -\frac{b}{2a}<k,\qquad k\text{ between roots}:\ af(k)<0 \]
Key points.
  • \(af(k)<0\) is necessary and sufficient for \(k\) to separate the two real roots
  • when \(af(k)>0\) both roots sit on one side of \(k\), and the axis position selects which side
  • real roots force \(D\ge 0\) before any bounding argument
  • for a downward parabola the \(a\) factor compensates the flip in sign, so always test \(af(k)\)
  • boundary points that are roots are handled by making the inequalities non-strict
  • small sketches of the parabola avoid sign mistakes in the interplay of conditions
Example 1.

For which \(k\) do the roots of \(x^2-2kx+(k+6)=0\) lie on either side of \(1\)?

  1. \(1\) lies between the roots exactly when \(af(1)<0\)
  2. \(f(1)=1-2k+k+6=7-k\)
  3. \(a=1>0\), hence \(1\cdot(7-k)<0\)

Answer: \(k>7\).

Example 2.

Find \(k\) so that both roots of \(x^2-(k+1)x+4=0\) are greater than \(1\).

  1. \(D\ge 0\): \((k+1)^2\ge 16\), so \(k\le -5\) or \(k\ge 3\)
  2. \(af(1)>0\): \(1-(k+1)+4=4-k>0\), so \(k<4\)
  3. axis \(-\frac{b}{2a}=\frac{k+1}{2}>1\), so \(k>1\)
  4. intersecting the constraints gives \(3\le k<4\)

Answer: \(k\in[3,4)\).

Common mistakes.
  • omitting the discriminant test
  • writing \(f(k)>0\) instead of \(af(k)>0\) and misjudging downward parabolas
  • inverting the axis inequality
  • using the between-roots sign when the required condition is both-roots-on-one-side
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 5 · Topic 11

Range Problems

Quadratic EquationAlgebraTopic 10 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The range of a rational function like \(\frac{ax^2+bx+c}{px^2+qx+r}\) is found by cross-multiplying into a quadratic in \(x\) with coefficients depending on \(y\); real solutions exist exactly when the discriminant satisfies \(D_y\ge 0\). Purely quadratic functions use the completed-square (vertex) form instead.
Key formula(s). \[ y=\frac{ax^2+bx+c}{px^2+qx+r}\ \Rightarrow\ (yp-a)x^2+(yq-b)x+(yr-c)=0,\ \text{solvable in real } x\ \Rightarrow\ D_y\ge 0,\qquad y=ax^2+bx+c:\ \text{range from vertex}\ \left(-\frac{b}{2a},-\frac{D}{4a}\right) \]
Key points.
  • the denominator never vanishes on the natural domain, so cross-multiplication is reversible
  • the condition \(D_y\ge 0\) becomes a quadratic inequality in \(y\), resolved by a sign chart
  • endpoints from the boundary \(D_y=0\) must be tested for attainability
  • if the leading coefficient vanishes at some \(y\), check straight-line behaviour separately
  • estimated range of a quadratic: \(a>0\) gives \([\,y_{\min},\infty)\), \(a<0\) gives \((-\infty,y_{\max}]\)
  • unbounded behaviour at infinity completes the boundary picture
Example 1.

Find the range of \(f(x)=x^2-4x+7\).

  1. \(x^2-4x+7=(x-2)^2+3\)
  2. \((x-2)^2\ge 0\), so \(f(x)\ge 3\)
  3. as \(x\to\pm\infty\), \(f(x)\to\infty\)

Answer: range \([3,\infty)\).

Example 2.

Find the range of \(y=\frac{x^2-1}{x^2+1}\) over the reals.

  1. cross-multiply: \((y-1)x^2+(y+1)=0\)
  2. real \(x\) forces \(D\ge 0\): \(-4(y-1)(y+1)\ge 0\), hence \(-1\le y\le 1\)
  3. \(y=1\) would require \(2=0\), impossible, so 1 is not attained
  4. \(y=-1\) is attained at \(x=0\)

Answer: range \([-1,1)\).

Common mistakes.
  • announcing the closed interval without verifying endpoint attainability
  • forgetting the \(D_y\ge 0\) condition or its direction
  • mixing the vertex formula up when reading the range of \(y=ax^2+bx+c\)
  • treating the range of the numerator as the range of the fraction

Chapter 6 · Topic 2

Arithmetic Geometric Progression

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 1 of 11

Explanation

Concept. An arithmetico-geometric progression is the term-by-term product of an AP \((a,\ a+d,\ a+2d,\dots)\) with a GP \((1,\ r,\ r^2,\dots)\), producing \(a,\ (a+d)r,\ (a+2d)r^2,\ \dots\). Its finite and infinite sums combine the geometric base with a correction term driven by \(d\).
Key formula(s). \[ \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(a+kd)r^k = \frac{a}{1-r}+\frac{dr(1-r^{n-1})}{(1-r)^2}-\frac{(a+(n-1)d)r^n}{1-r},\qquad |r|<1:\ S_\infty=\frac{a}{1-r}+\frac{dr}{(1-r)^2} \]
Key points.
  • multiply the general AP term \(a+(k-1)d\) by \(r^{k-1}\) to form the AGP
  • subtraction of \(r\cdot S_n\) from \(S_n\) collapses the middle into a geometric tail
  • the infinite limit absorbs the last term when \(|r|<1\)
  • workable pattern: \(S_n = \frac{(\text{first geometric term})}{1-r}-\frac{(\text{tail term})r^n}{1-r} + \text{drift correction}\)
  • the drift correction vanishes exactly when \(d=0\), degenerating to an ordinary GP
  • the sum \(S_\infty\) exists only for \(|r|<1\)
Example 1.

Sum to infinity: \(1+\frac{2}{3}+\frac{3}{9}+\frac{4}{27}+\cdots\)

  1. AP part \(a=1,\ d=1\); GP ratio \(r=\frac{1}{3}\)
  2. \(S_\infty=\frac{a}{1-r}+\frac{dr}{(1-r)^2}\)
  3. \(=\frac{1}{2/3}+\frac{1/3}{4/9}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\)

Answer: \(\frac{9}{4}\).

Example 2.

Sum to infinity: \(1+\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{4}+\frac{7}{8}+\cdots\)

  1. AP part \(a=1,\ d=2\); GP ratio \(r=\frac{1}{2}\)
  2. \(S_\infty=\frac{1}{1-1/2}+\frac{2\cdot(1/2)}{(1-1/2)^2}\)
  3. \(=2+\frac{1}{1/4}=2+4=6\)

Answer: the infinite sum is \(6\).

Common mistakes.
  • applying the plain GP sum formula while an AP multiplier remains
  • using the infinite formula for \(|r|\ge 1\)
  • misallocating the \(+1\) offset between \((a+kd)r^k\) and \((a+(k-1)d)r^{k-1}\)
  • dropping the drift term and recording only the base geometric part
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 3

Harmonic Progression

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 2 of 11

Explanation

Concept. A harmonic progression is a sequence whose reciprocals form an arithmetic progression: \(\frac{1}{a},\ \frac{1}{a+d},\ \frac{1}{a+2d},\dots\). There is no elementary closed sum for a finite HP; problems are solved by re-casting every term as a reciprocal of an AP term.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{HP: }\frac{1}{a},\ \frac{1}{a+d},\ \frac{1}{a+2d},\ \dots,\qquad t_n=\frac{1}{a+(n-1)d},\qquad \frac{1}{t_n}-\frac{1}{t_{n-1}}=d \]
Key points.
  • take reciprocals of the HP terms first; they must form an AP
  • detect an HP by checking constant successive differences of reciprocals
  • the \(n\)-th term is the reciprocal of the AP \(n\)-th term
  • there is no standard sum formula for HPs at JEE level
  • differencing reciprocals is the standard HP manipulation
  • insertion of harmonic means is reciprocation of an insertion problem in AP
Example 1.

Find the 10th term of the HP \(\frac{1}{2},\ \frac{1}{5},\ \frac{1}{8},\ \cdots\)

  1. reciprocals \(2,5,8,\dots\) form an AP with \(a=2,\ d=3\)
  2. the AP \(n\)-th term is \(2+3(n-1)\)
  3. \(n=10\): \(2+27=29\)

Answer: \(t_{10}=\frac{1}{29}\).

Example 2.

Which term of the HP \(\frac{1}{4},\ \frac{1}{7},\ \frac{1}{10},\ \dots\) equals \(\frac{1}{31}\)?

  1. reciprocals \(4,7,10,\dots\) are an AP with \(a=4,\ d=3\)
  2. need \(4+3(n-1)=31\)
  3. \(3n+1=31\), so \(n=10\)

Answer: the 10th term equals \(\frac{1}{31}\).

Common mistakes.
  • adding HP terms directly as if they were AP terms
  • applying the AP \(n\)-th term formula to the HP without taking reciprocals
  • writing a sum formula for a finite HP that does not exist
  • treating \(\frac{1}{a+nb}\) as an HP term without checking the reciprocal pattern
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 4

Geometric Progression

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 3 of 11

Explanation

Concept. A geometric progression (GP) is a sequence in which each term after the first is obtained by multiplying by the fixed ratio \(r\): \(a,\ ar,\ ar^2,\ \dots\), with \(a_n=ar^{n-1}\). Its finite sum and convergent infinite sum follow from factorising the \((1-r)\) differences.
Key formula(s). \[ a_n=ar^{n-1},\qquad S_n=a\frac{r^n-1}{r-1}=a\frac{1-r^n}{1-r},\qquad S_\infty=\frac{a}{1-r}\quad(|r|<1),\qquad \text{terms } a,b,c\ \text{in GP} \iff b^2=ac \]
Key points.
  • the ratio is constant: \(r=\frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}\)
  • three numbers in GP are best written \(a,\ ar,\ ar^2\) (or \(a/r,\ a,\ ar\))
  • the finite sum formula works for every \(r\neq 1\); at \(r=1\) the sum is \(na\)
  • an infinite GP converges exactly for \(|r|<1\)
  • the product of terms equidistant from the ends is \(a\cdot a_n\)
  • a GP is increasing when \(r>1\), decreasing when \(0<r<1\), and alternating when \(r<0\)
Example 1.

For the GP \(2,6,18,\dots\), find the 6th term and \(S_6\).

  1. \(a=2,\ r=3\)
  2. \(t_6=2\cdot 3^5=486\)
  3. \(S_6=2\frac{3^6-1}{3-1}=2\cdot\frac{728}{2}=728\)

Answer: \(t_6=486,\ S_6=728\).

Example 2.

In a GP, \(t_2=6\) and \(t_5=48\). Write the GP and \(S_\infty\) if it is finite.

  1. \(ar=6,\ ar^4=48\), so \(r^3=8\), hence \(r=2\)
  2. \(a=3\); the GP is \(3,6,12,24,\dots\)
  3. \(|r|=2>1\), so the infinite sum diverges

Answer: the GP is \(3,6,12,\dots\) with no finite \(S_\infty\).

Common mistakes.
  • indexing the exponent \(r^{n}\) instead of \(r^{n-1}\)
  • using the finite-sum formula at \(r=1\)
  • applying \(S_\infty\) when \(r\) lies outside \((-1,1)\)
  • taking a negative ratio's power sign incorrectly when evaluating signs of terms
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 5

Geometric Mean

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 4 of 11

Explanation

Concept. The geometric mean of two positive numbers \(a,b\) is \(\sqrt{ab}\); it slots the numbers into a GP. Inserting \(n\) geometric means between \(a\) and \(b\) creates a GP of \(n+2\) terms whose ratio is fixed by the endpoints.
Key formula(s). \[ G(a,b)=\sqrt{ab},\qquad a,\ G_1,\dots,G_n,\ b\ \text{in GP:}\ r=\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{1/(n+1)},\ G_k=a r^k,\qquad G_1G_2\cdots G_n=(ab)^{n/2} \]
Key points.
  • one GM between \(a\) and \(b\) is \(\sqrt{ab}\), valid for positive numbers
  • for \(n\) means the common ratio is the \((n+1)\)-th root of \(b/a\)
  • the means interleave between the endpoints when \(a<b\) and \(r>1\)
  • the product identity \(G_1G_2\cdots G_n=(ab)^{n/2}\) is a neat GP symmetry
  • the GM of a GP term-set equals the middle term when the count is odd
  • AM \(\ge\) GM with equality only for equal entries
Example 1.

Insert three geometric means between 2 and 32.

  1. with 3 means the GP has 5 terms, so \(r^4=\frac{32}{2}=16\)
  2. \(r=2\)
  3. means: \(2\cdot 2,\ 2\cdot 4,\ 2\cdot 8\)

Answer: \(4,\ 8,\ 16\).

Example 2.

Prove that the product of the \(n\) geometric means inserted between \(a\) and \(b\) is \((ab)^{n/2}\).

  1. the sequence \(a,\ ar,\ ar^2,\dots,ar^n,\ ar^{n+1}=b\) is a GP
  2. product of the \(n\) means is \(a^n r^{1+2+\cdots+n}=a^n r^{n(n+1)/2}\)
  3. \(r^{n+1}=b/a\), hence \(r^{n(n+1)/2}=(b/a)^{n/2}\)
  4. total product \(=a^n(b/a)^{n/2}=(ab)^{n/2}\)

Answer: the product is \((ab)^{n/2}\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing one GM as \(\frac{a+b}{2}\) (that is the AM)
  • fixing the ratio as \((b/a)^{1/n}\) without counting \(n+1\) gaps
  • allowing \(r^4\) type miscounting when inserting \(n\) means between two endpoints
  • computing the product identity with the wrong power of the endpoints

Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 6

Special Sequences

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 5 of 11

Explanation

Concept. Special sequences are standard finite sums whose closed forms are memorised and composed: the sum of naturals, of natural squares, and of natural cubes, plus telescoping series whose intermediate terms cancel in pairs.
Key formula(s). \[ \sum_{k=1}^n k=\frac{n(n+1)}{2},\qquad \sum_{k=1}^n k^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6},\qquad \sum_{k=1}^n k^3=\left[\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\right]^2,\qquad \sum_{k=1}^n \frac{1}{k(k+1)}=\frac{n}{n+1} \]
Key points.
  • \(\sum k^3\) equals the square of the natural-sum formula
  • a telescoping fraction like \(\frac{1}{k(k+1)}=\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{k+1}\) collapses to its ends
  • linear combinations of the base sums split term by term
  • constants factor out: \(\sum c f(k) = c\sum f(k)\), \(\sum (f(k)+g(k)) = \sum f(k)+\sum g(k)\)
  • verify a formula on \(n=1\) and \(n=2\) before full use
  • index shifts preserve the count of elements in the summation range
Example 1.

Evaluate \(\sum_{k=1}^{15} k^2\).

  1. formula: \(\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}\) with \(n=15\)
  2. \(=\frac{15\cdot 16\cdot 31}{6}\)
  3. \(=5\cdot 8\cdot 31 = 1240\)

Answer: \(1240\).

Example 2.

Evaluate \(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k(k+1)}\).

  1. partial fractions: \(\frac{1}{k(k+1)}=\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{k+1}\)
  2. the sum telescopes: \(\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\cdots+\left(\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n+1}\right)\)
  3. only the first and last terms survive

Answer: \(\frac{n}{n+1}\).

Common mistakes.
  • quoting \(\sum k^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}\) with a wrong middle factor
  • applying the sum-of-cubes formula to a sum of squares
  • mis-carving the telescope so the surviving ends are off by one
  • forgetting the divisor when summing constants, e.g. \(\sum 1 = n\), rendered incorrectly
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 7

Relation Between AM, GM and HM

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 6 of 11

Explanation

Concept. For positive numbers the arithmetic, geometric and harmonic means are always ordered AM \(\ge\) GM \(\ge\) HM, with equality exactly when all the numbers coincide. For two numbers the geometric mean is the geometric mean of the arithmetic and harmonic means.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{AM}\ge\text{GM}\ge\text{HM},\qquad a,b>0:\ \frac{a+b}{2}\ge\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b},\qquad \text{GM}^2=\text{AM}\cdot\text{HM}\quad\text{(two numbers)} \]
Key points.
  • AM-GM emerges from \((\sqrt a-\sqrt b)^2 \ge 0\)
  • AM-HM is the same inequality after taking reciprocals, or direct from \((a-b)^2\ge 0\)
  • equality in every link forces all entries equal
  • GM is sandwiched: \(\text{AM}\cdot\text{HM}=\text{GM}^2\) for exactly two numbers
  • the chain ranks means for optimisation problems (minimising sums, maximising products)
  • \(\frac{a+b}{2}\ge\sqrt{ab}\) is usable for proving bounds in natural-number settings
Example 1.

If the AM of two positive numbers is 15 and their GM is 9, find the numbers and their HM.

  1. \(\frac{a+b}{2}=15\) and \(\sqrt{ab}=9\), so \(a+b=30,\ ab=81\)
  2. the numbers are roots of \(u^2-30u+81=0\)
  3. \((u-3)(u-27)=0\), hence \(3\) and \(27\)
  4. \(\text{HM}=\frac{\text{GM}^2}{\text{AM}}=\frac{81}{15}=\frac{27}{5}\)

Answer: the numbers are 3 and 27, and HM \(=\frac{27}{5}\).

Example 2.

Prove AM \(\ge\) GM \(\ge\) HM for positive \(a,b\).

  1. \(\frac{a+b}{2}-\sqrt{ab}=\frac{(\sqrt a-\sqrt b)^2}{2}\ge 0\)
  2. \(\sqrt{ab}-\frac{2ab}{a+b}=\frac{\sqrt{ab}(\sqrt a-\sqrt b)^2}{a+b}\ge 0\)
  3. both gaps are squares, hence non-negative, with equality exactly at \(a=b\)

Answer: \(\frac{a+b}{2}\ge\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}\), equality iff \(a=b\).

Common mistakes.
  • ordering the means in the wrong direction
  • using the two-number identity \(\text{GM}^2=\text{AM}\cdot\text{HM}\) for more than two numbers
  • forgetting that GM and HM require positive inputs
  • assuming equality is automatic rather than requiring all entries to be equal
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 8

Harmonic Mean

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 7 of 11

Explanation

Concept. The harmonic mean of a list of positive numbers is the reciprocal of the arithmetic mean of their reciprocals; for two numbers it simplifies to \(\frac{2ab}{a+b}\). Inserting harmonic means is the reciprocal operation of inserting arithmetic means.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{HM}=\frac{n}{\frac{1}{a_1}+\cdots+\frac{1}{a_n}},\qquad \text{HM}(a,b)=\frac{2ab}{a+b},\qquad \text{insert } n\ \text{HMs between } a,b:\ \text{reciprocals form an AP with } \frac{1}{b},\frac{1}{a}\ \text{as endpoints} \]
Key points.
  • the HM always lies between the smallest and largest entries of a positive list
  • for two numbers HM \(=\frac{2ab}{a+b}\le\sqrt{ab}\le\frac{a+b}{2}\)
  • inserting \(n\) harmonic means between \(a\) and \(b\): reciprocate \((n+2)\)-term AP data
  • harmonic means are useful for average speeds over equal distances
  • HM of equal numbers equals that number
  • HM is translation-sensitive, scale-invariant under scaling all terms by a constant? no: it scales linearly with the terms
Example 1.

Find the HM of 4 and 12.

  1. \(\text{HM}=\frac{2ab}{a+b}=\frac{2\cdot 4\cdot 12}{4+12}\)
  2. \(=\frac{96}{16}=6\)

Answer: the HM is 6.

Example 2.

Insert two harmonic means between 4 and 12.

  1. reciprocals \(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{12}\) are the endpoints of a 4-term AP
  2. common difference \(d=\frac{1/12-1/4}{3}=-\frac{1}{18}\)
  3. AP terms: \(\frac{1}{4},\ \frac{7}{36},\ \frac{5}{36},\ \frac{1}{12}\)
  4. HMs are the reciprocals of the middle AP terms

Answer: the two harmonic means are \(\frac{36}{7}\) and \(\frac{36}{5}\).

Common mistakes.
  • taking a plain average of the numbers as the HM
  • adding the two reciprocals but forgetting the \(n\) in the numerator
  • inserting HMs without reciprocating the AP construction
  • expecting the HM to exceed the AM

Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 9

Arithmetic Progression

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 8 of 11

Explanation

Concept. An arithmetic progression (AP) is a sequence in which each term after the first is obtained by adding a fixed constant \(d\), the common difference, to the preceding term: \(a,\ a+d,\ a+2d,\ \dots\).
Key formula(s). \[ a_n = a + (n-1)d, \qquad S_n = \frac{n}{2}\big(2a + (n-1)d\big) = \frac{n}{2}(a + l) \]
Key points.
  • first term \(a\), common difference \(d\)
  • nth term: \(a_n = a + (n-1)d\)
  • sum of first \(n\) terms: \(S_n = \frac{n}{2}[2a + (n-1)d]\)
  • three numbers in AP are best written as \(a-d,\ a,\ a+d\)
  • \(S_{n} - S_{n-1} = a_n\), the defining identity linking sums to terms
  • the average of all terms equals the middle term when \(n\) is odd
Example 1.

Find the 20th term of the AP \(5, 9, 13, 17, \dots\)

  1. first term \(a = 5\), common difference \(d = 9-5 = 4\)
  2. \(a_n = a + (n-1)d\) with \(n = 20\) gives \(a_{20} = 5 + 19 \times 4\)
  3. \(a_{20} = 5 + 76 = 81\)

Answer: \(a_{20} = 81\)

Example 2.

The sum of the first \(n\) terms of an AP is \(3n^2 + 5n\). Find its nth term.

  1. \(a_1 = S_1 = 8\), and \(S_2 = 22\) so \(a_2 = 14\); hence \(d = 6\)
  2. \(a_n = 8 + 6(n-1)\)

Answer: \(a_n = 6n + 2\)

Common mistakes.
  • confusing the nth term \(a_n\) with the sum \(S_n\)
  • writing \(a_n = a + nd\) (missing the \(-1\))
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 10

Arithmetic Mean

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 9 of 11

Explanation

Concept. The arithmetic mean of \(n\) numbers is their average; a single AM between \(a\) and \(b\) is \(\frac{a+b}{2}\). Inserting \(n\) arithmetic means between two numbers makes a total of \(n+2\) terms of an AP.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{AM}(a_1,\dots,a_n)=\frac{a_1+\cdots+a_n}{n},\qquad n\ \text{means between }a,b:\ d=\frac{b-a}{n+1},\quad A_k=a+kd \]
Key points.
  • one AM between \(a\) and \(b\): \(\frac{a+b}{2}\)
  • for \(n\) means there are \(n+1\) gaps, so \(d=\frac{b-a}{n+1}\)
  • the \(k\)-th inserted mean is \(A_k=a+kd\)
  • the AM lies exactly midway between the endpoints
  • sum of the \(n\) means equals \(n\cdot\frac{a+b}{2}\)
  • AM of equal numbers equals that common value
Example 1.

Insert four arithmetic means between 2 and 12.

  1. with 4 means there are 5 gaps, so \(d=\frac{12-2}{5}=2\)
  2. the AP is \(2,4,6,8,10,12\)

Answer: the AMs are \(4,6,8,10\).

Example 2.

Prove that the sum of the \(n\) AMs inserted between \(a\) and \(b\) is \(\frac{n(a+b)}{2}\).

  1. \(d=\frac{b-a}{n+1}\), means are \(a+d, a+2d, \dots, a+nd\)
  2. sum \(=\frac{n}{2}\big(a+d+a+nd\big)=\frac{n}{2}(2a+(n+1)d)\)
  3. \((n+1)d=b-a\), so the parenthesis equals \(a+b\)

Answer: sum of AMs \(=\frac{n(a+b)}{2}\).

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(d=\frac{b-a}{n}\) (there are \(n+1\) gaps)
  • counting the endpoints among the means
  • using the two-number midpoint for a list of more than two numbers
  • forgetting that \(n\) means create \(n+2\) total terms
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 11

Cauchy-Schwarz Inequalities

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 10 of 11

Explanation

Concept. The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality bounds the square of a sum of products by the product of the corresponding sums of squares, writing sequences in parallel; in the context of sequences it is the master tool for bounding dot products, sums, and norms.
Key formula(s). \[ \left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i b_i\right)^2 \le \left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^n b_i^2\right),\qquad \text{equality}\iff (a_1,\dots,a_n)\parallel (b_1,\dots,b_n) \]
Key points.
  • the inequality is sign-free: reals allowed on both sequences
  • setting \(b_i=1\) gives \(\left(\sum a_i\right)^2\le n\sum a_i^2\)
  • in vector form: \((\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})^2\le \|\mathbf{a}\|^2\|\mathbf{b}\|^2\)
  • equality when the sequence of ratios \(\frac{a_i}{b_i}\) is constant
  • ideal for minimising \(\sum x_i^2\) subject to one fixed linear form
  • both sides add harmoniously with normalisation wherever unit vectors are used
Example 1.

Minimise \(x^2+y^2+z^2\) subject to \(x+2y+3z=6\).

  1. C-S on \((x,y,z)\) and \((1,2,3)\):
  2. \((x^2+y^2+z^2)(1^2+2^2+3^2)\ge (x+2y+3z)^2=36\)
  3. \(14(x^2+y^2+z^2)\ge 36\)
  4. minimum \(=\frac{36}{14}=\frac{18}{7}\), attained when \((x,y,z)\propto(1,2,3)\)

Answer: minimum \(\frac{18}{7}\).

Example 2.

Prove \(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\ge \frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\right)^2\).

  1. apply C-S with \(b_i=1\) for all \(i\)
  2. \(\left(\sum a_i\cdot 1\right)^2\le \left(\sum a_i^2\right)\left(\sum 1^2\right)\)
  3. \(\left(\sum a_i\right)^2\le n\sum a_i^2\)

Answer: \(\sum a_i^2\ge\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum a_i\right)^2\).

Common mistakes.
  • flipping the inequality's direction
  • imposing unnecessary positivity on the sequence terms
  • mismatching the equality condition to a non-proportional pair of sequences
  • writing \((ab+cd)^2\le (a^2+c^2)(b^2+d^2)\) with wrong vector pairing
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 6 · Topic 12

Inequalities Based on RMS ≥ AM ≥ GM ≥ HM

Sequence & SeriesAlgebraTopic 11 of 11

Explanation

Concept. The RMS-AM-GM-HM ladder applies to sequences of positive reals termwise, and is the source of countless global bounds in sequence and series problems. Equality throughout forces the sequence to be constant.
Key formula(s). \[ \sqrt{\frac{\sum x_i^2}{n}}\ \ge\ \frac{\sum x_i}{n}\ \ge\ \left(\prod x_i\right)^{1/n}\ \ge\ \frac{n}{\sum 1/x_i}\qquad \text{for } x_i>0,\ \text{equality iff all } x_i\ \text{equal} \]
Key points.
  • AM-GM proves bounds like \(x+\frac{1}{x}\ge 2\) for \(x>0\)
  • AM-HM yields \((a+b+c)(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c})\ge 9\)
  • the means fill this chain: RMS \(\ge\) AM \(\ge\) GM \(\ge\) HM
  • GM and HM require all entries positive
  • equality everywhere forces \(x_1=x_2=\cdots=x_n\)
  • the ladder is applied to symmetric expressions of sequences and to minimisation statements
Example 1.

Find the minimum of \(x+\frac{1}{x}\) for \(x>0\).

  1. AM-GM on \(x\) and \(\frac{1}{x}\): \(\frac{x+1/x}{2}\ge \sqrt{x\cdot 1/x}=1\)
  2. \(x+\frac{1}{x}\ge 2\)
  3. equality when \(x=1\)

Answer: minimum 2.

Example 2.

Prove \((a+b+c)\left(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\right)\ge 9\) for \(a,b,c>0\).

  1. AM-HM: \(\frac{a+b+c}{3}\ge \frac{3}{\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}}\)
  2. \((a+b+c)\left(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\right)\ge 9\)
  3. equality when \(a=b=c\)

Answer: \((a+b+c)\left(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\right)\ge 9\).

Common mistakes.
  • placing RMS below AM on the ladder
  • invoking GM or HM on zero or negative entries
  • expecting equality in a very unequal tuple
  • reversing the AM-HM direction and obtaining a weaker or invalid bound
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 7 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

Chapter 7 · Topic 2

Trigonometric Inequations

Trigonometric EquationTrigonometryTopic 1 of 3

Explanation

Concept. A trigonometric inequation asks for all angles for which an inequality such as \(\sin x \le \frac{1}{2}\) or \(\cos 2x > \frac{1}{2}\) holds in a given interval. First locate the boundary angles from the corresponding equation, then decide on the unit circle which arcs satisfy the inequality.
Key formula(s). \[ \sin x \le a\ (x\in[0,2\pi]):\ x\in[0,\sin^{-1}a]\cup[\pi-\sin^{-1}a,2\pi];\qquad \tan x > a:\ x\in\Big(\tan^{-1}a,\ \frac{\pi}{2}\Big) \text{ up to } n\pi \]
Key points.
  • solve the equality boundary first: those angles cut the interval into candidate arcs
  • test one interior angle of each arc; solutions form whole arcs because the functions are continuous
  • on the unit circle, \(\sin x > a\) means the ray is above height \(a\); \(\cos x > a\) means it lies right of line \(x = a\)
  • for \(>\) and \(<\) exclude boundary angles; for \(\ge\) and \(\le\) include them
  • \(\cos x \le a\) on \([0,2\pi]\) gives \(x\in[\cos^{-1}a,\ 2\pi-\cos^{-1}a]\)
  • \(\tan x > a\): add \(n\pi\) to the base interval since tangent has period \(\pi\)
  • compound forms: \(\sin x > \cos x \iff \sqrt{2}\sin\big(x-\frac{\pi}{4}\big) > 0\)
Example 1.

Solve \(\sin x \le \frac{1}{2}\) for \(x\in[0,2\pi]\).

  1. boundary: \(\sin x = \frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow x = \frac{\pi}{6},\ \frac{5\pi}{6}\)
  2. on \([0,\frac{\pi}{6}]\) the sine rises from \(0\) to \(\frac{1}{2}\), so the inequality holds
  3. on \([\frac{\pi}{6},\frac{5\pi}{6}]\), \(\sin x \ge \frac{1}{2}\), the inequality fails
  4. on \([\frac{5\pi}{6},2\pi]\) we again have \(\sin x \le \frac{1}{2}\)

Answer: \(x\in\big[0,\frac{\pi}{6}\big]\cup\big[\frac{5\pi}{6},2\pi\big]\)

Example 2.

Solve \(\cos 2x > \frac{1}{2}\) for \(0 \le x \le \pi\).

  1. put \(t = 2x \in [0,2\pi]\): \(\cos t > \frac{1}{2}\) holds for \(t\in[0,\frac{\pi}{3})\cup(\frac{5\pi}{3},2\pi]\)
  2. so \(2x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{3})\cup(\frac{5\pi}{3},2\pi]\)
  3. divide by 2 to get \(x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{6})\cup(\frac{5\pi}{6},\pi]\)

Answer: \(x\in\big[0,\frac{\pi}{6}\big)\cup\big(\frac{5\pi}{6},\pi\big]\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting that \(\sin x = c\) has two solutions in \([0,2\pi]\), so the solution set splits into two arcs
  • including boundary angles when the inequality is strict
  • writing the base interval but failing to add the period when a general solution is asked
