Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 2

Chord to a Circle

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 1 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A chord of a circle is a segment whose endpoints lie on the circle, and the perpendicular from the centre to a chord bisects it, so the midpoint of a chord is the foot of that perpendicular. For a circle, the chord with a given midpoint and the chord of contact are obtained from \(T = S_1\).
Key formula(s). For \(x^2+y^2 = r^2\): chord of contact from external \((x_1,y_1)\) is \[ xx_1+yy_1 = r^2; \qquad \text{chord with midpoint } (x_1,y_1):\ xx_1+yy_1 = x_1^2+y_1^2 \]
Key points.
  • Chord length \(= 2\sqrt{r^2 - p^2}\), where \(p\) is the perpendicular distance of the centre from the chord.
  • The midpoint of a chord is the foot of the perpendicular from the centre to the chord.
  • The chord of contact is the chord joining the two contact points of tangents drawn from an external point.
  • General form: for \(S = x^2+y^2+2gx+2fy+c\), \(T = xx_1+yy_1+g(x+x_1)+f(y+y_1)+c\).
  • The chord with midpoint \((x_1,y_1)\) satisfies \(T = S_1\), where \(S_1\) is the value of \(S\) at the midpoint.
  • A chord through the centre is a diameter, the longest chord.
Example 1.

Find the length of the chord cut by the line \(3x+4y = 15\) from the circle \(x^2+y^2 = 25\).

  1. centre \((0,0)\), radius 5; distance of the line: \(p = \frac{|15|}{\sqrt{25}} = 3\).
  2. chord length \(= 2\sqrt{r^2 - p^2} = 2\sqrt{25-9}\).
  3. \(= 2\sqrt{16} = 8\).

Answer: chord length \(= 8\)

Example 2.

Find the equation of the chord of \(x^2+y^2 = 25\) whose midpoint is \((3,1)\).

  1. use \(T = S_1\): \(xx_1 + yy_1 = x_1^2 + y_1^2\) with \((x_1,y_1) = (3,1)\).
  2. \(3x + y = 9 + 1 = 10\).

Answer: chord \(3x + y = 10\)

Common mistakes.
  • computing the chord length as \(r-p\) instead of \(2\sqrt{r^2-p^2}\)
  • using \(xx_1+yy_1 = r^2\) for a midpoint chord — the constant must be \(x_1^2+y_1^2\)
  • assuming the midpoint of a chord lies on the circle (it lies inside)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 3

Pole and Polar

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 2 of 8

Explanation

Concept. For a circle, every point has a corresponding line, called its polar, and the point is the pole of that line. For an exterior point the polar is its chord of contact; for a point on the circle the polar is the tangent; for an interior point the polar still exists as a line that does not meet the circle.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{polar of } (x_1,y_1)\ \text{w.r.t. } x^2+y^2 = r^2:\quad xx_1 + yy_1 = r^2 \qquad \text{(in general, } T = 0\text{)} \]
Key points.
  • If \((x_1,y_1)\) lies on the circle, the polar is the tangent at that point.
  • If \((x_1,y_1)\) is outside, the polar is the chord of contact of the two tangents from it.
  • If the point is inside, its polar is a line with no real intersection with the circle.
  • Symmetry: if the polar of \(P\) passes through \(Q\), then the polar of \(Q\) passes through \(P\).
  • Pole of \(lx+my+n = 0\) w.r.t. \(x^2+y^2 = r^2\): \(\left(-\frac{r^2 l}{n},\ -\frac{r^2 m}{n}\right)\).
  • General circle: the polar is \(T = 0\) with \(T = xx_1+yy_1+g(x+x_1)+f(y+y_1)+c\).
Example 1.

Find the polar of the point \((4,3)\) w.r.t. the circle \(x^2+y^2 = 36\).

  1. use \(xx_1 + yy_1 = r^2\) with \((x_1,y_1) = (4,3)\), \(r^2 = 36\).
  2. \(4x + 3y = 36\).

Answer: polar is \(4x + 3y = 36\)

Example 2.

Find the pole of the line \(2x+3y = 6\) w.r.t. the circle \(x^2+y^2 = 4\).

  1. \(l = 2,\ m = 3,\ n = -6,\ r^2 = 4\).
  2. pole \(= \left(-\frac{4\cdot 2}{-6},\ -\frac{4\cdot 3}{-6}\right) = \left(\frac{8}{6},\ \frac{12}{6}\right)\).

Answer: pole \(= \left(\frac{4}{3},\ 2\right)\)

Common mistakes.
  • applying the chord-of-contact polar even when the point is inside the circle
  • flipping the signs in the pole formula \(-r^2 l/n\)
  • saying a point on the circle has a chord of contact (it has only the tangent)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 4

Introduction to Circle

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 3 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A circle is the set of all points at a fixed distance \(r\) from a fixed point; its general equation \(x^2+y^2+2gx+2fy+c = 0\) has centre \((-g,-f)\) and radius \(\sqrt{g^2+f^2-c}\). Equations of a circle are recognized by equal coefficients of \(x^2\) and \(y^2\) and the absence of the \(xy\)-term.
Key formula(s). \[ (x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = r^2, \qquad x^2+y^2+2gx+2fy+c = 0 \Rightarrow \text{centre } (-g,-f),\ r = \sqrt{g^2+f^2-c} \]
Key points.
  • A real circle requires \(g^2+f^2 \ge c\).
  • A circle through the origin has \(c = 0\).
  • Diameter form with endpoints \((x_1,y_1),\ (x_2,y_2)\): \((x-x_1)(x-x_2) + (y-y_1)(y-y_2) = 0\).
  • Position of \(P(x_1,y_1)\): inside if \(S_1 < 0\), on the circle if \(S_1 = 0\), outside if \(S_1 > 0\), where \(S_1\) is the value of the circle equation at \(P\).
  • Three points determine a unique circle; solve for \(g, f, c\).
  • The radius is half the diameter; the centre is the midpoint of any diameter.
Example 1.

Find the centre and radius of the circle \(x^2+y^2-6x+8y = 0\).

  1. \(2g = -6 \Rightarrow g = -3\), \(2f = 8 \Rightarrow f = 4\), \(c = 0\).
  2. centre \(= (-g,-f) = (3,-4)\).
  3. \(r = \sqrt{g^2+f^2-c} = \sqrt{9+16-0} = 5\).

Answer: centre \((3,-4)\), radius \(5\)

Example 2.

Find the equation of the circle having \(A(1,2),\ B(3,6)\) as a diameter.

  1. diameter form: \((x-1)(x-3) + (y-2)(y-6) = 0\).
  2. expand: \(x^2-4x+3 + y^2-8y+12 = 0\).
  3. \(x^2+y^2-4x-8y+15 = 0\), equivalently centre \((2,4)\), radius \(\sqrt{5}\).

Answer: \(x^2+y^2-4x-8y+15 = 0\)

Common mistakes.
  • taking the centre as \((g,f)\) instead of \((-g,-f)\)
  • computing the radius as \(\sqrt{g^2+f^2+c}\) (wrong sign of \(c\))
  • quoting a real radius when \(g^2+f^2 < c\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 5

System of Circles and Orthogonality

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 4 of 8

Explanation

Concept. Two circles cut orthogonally when the tangents at each common point are perpendicular, which is equivalent to \(d^2 = r_1^2+r_2^2\) where \(d\) is the distance between centres. In coefficient form this becomes a neat relation, and the common chord (radical axis) is obtained by subtracting their equations.
Key formula(s). For \(S_1 = x^2+y^2+2g_1x+2f_1y+c_1 = 0\) and \(S_2 = x^2+y^2+2g_2x+2f_2y+c_2 = 0\): \[ \text{orthogonal} \iff 2g_1g_2 + 2f_1f_2 = c_1 + c_2; \qquad \text{radical axis: } S_1 - S_2 = 0 \]
Key points.
  • Orthogonality is equivalent to \(d^2 = r_1^2 + r_2^2\).
  • The radical axis \(S_1 - S_2 = 0\) is a straight line, the common chord when the circles intersect.
  • The radical axis is the locus of points with equal powers w.r.t. the two circles.
  • Concentric circles have no radical axis (the difference degenerates).
  • A point on the radical axis has equal tangent lengths to both circles.
  • Orthogonality makes the tangent of one circle pass through the centre of the other at the contact point.
Example 1.

Verify that \(x^2+y^2+4x+2y-4 = 0\) and \(x^2+y^2-6x-8y-16 = 0\) cut orthogonally.

  1. \((g_1,f_1,c_1) = (2,1,-4)\), \((g_2,f_2,c_2) = (-3,-4,-16)\).
  2. \(2g_1g_2 + 2f_1f_2 = 2(2)(-3) + 2(1)(-4) = -12-8 = -20\).
  3. \(c_1 + c_2 = -4 - 16 = -20\), equal, so the circles are orthogonal.

Answer: the circles cut orthogonally

Example 2.

Find \(k\) so that \(x^2+y^2+2x-2y+k = 0\) cuts \(x^2+y^2+4x-6y+9 = 0\) orthogonally.

  1. \((g_1,f_1,c_1) = (1,-1,k)\), \((g_2,f_2,c_2) = (2,-3,9)\).
  2. \(2(1)(2) + 2(-1)(-3) = k + 9\), i.e. \(4+6 = k+9\).
  3. \(k = 1\).

Answer: \(k = 1\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(g_1g_2 + f_1f_2 = c_1+c_2\) and dropping the factor 2
  • calling the radical axis of concentric circles the common chord
  • confusing "same power points" with "equal tangent lengths" only on the radical axis (they coincide, but both must be stated with care)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 6

Director Circle

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 5 of 8

Explanation

Concept. The director circle of a given circle is the locus of the point of intersection of two mutually perpendicular tangents drawn to the given circle. For a circle of radius \(r\) it is the concentric circle of radius \(\sqrt{2}\,r\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{director circle of } (x-h)^2+(y-k)^2 = r^2:\quad (x-h)^2 + (y-k)^2 = 2r^2 \]
Key points.
  • Every point of the director circle sends perpendicular tangents to the original circle, and conversely.
  • The director circle is concentric with the original circle and has radius \(\sqrt{2}\,r\).
  • Points strictly between the original circle and the director circle do not produce perpendicular tangents.
  • The chord of contact from any point on the director circle subtends a right angle at the centre.
  • For \(r = 0\) the director circle collapses to the given point.
  • A problem asking for the locus of intersection of two perpendicular tangents is always answered by the director circle.
Example 1.

Find the director circle of \(x^2+y^2 = 9\).

  1. \(r^2 = 9\), so the director circle has radius squared \(2r^2 = 18\).
  2. it is concentric with the original circle, centre \((0,0)\).

Answer: director circle \(x^2+y^2 = 18\)

Example 2.

A point moves so that the tangents drawn from it to \(x^2+y^2-4x-6y-12 = 0\) are perpendicular. Find its locus.

  1. \(g = -2,\ f = -3,\ c = -12\), so \(r^2 = 4+9+12 = 25\).
  2. the locus is the director circle \((x-2)^2 + (y-3)^2 = 50\).
  3. expanding: \(x^2+y^2-4x-6y+13 = 50\).
  4. \(x^2+y^2-4x-6y-37 = 0\).

Answer: \(x^2+y^2-4x-6y-37 = 0\)

Common mistakes.
  • putting \(r^2\) on the right instead of \(2r^2\) (missing the \(\sqrt{2}\) factor)
  • writing the director circle as \((x-h)^2+(y-k)^2 = 4r^2\)
  • forgetting that the director circle is concentric, so its centre is the same \((h,k)\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 7

Common Tangent to Two Circles

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 6 of 8

Explanation

Concept. Two circles may have up to four common tangents — two direct (external) ones that do not cross the segment of centres, and two transverse (internal) ones that do. The number of common tangents depends only on the distance \(d\) between centres and the two radii.
Key formula(s). Number of common tangents: \(4\) if \(d > r_1+r_2\), \(3\) if \(d = r_1+r_2\), \(2\) if \(|r_1-r_2| < d < r_1+r_2\), \(1\) if \(d = |r_1-r_2|\), \(0\) if \(d < |r_1-r_2|\).
Key points.
  • Direct (external) tangent length \(= \sqrt{d^2 - (r_1-r_2)^2}\); transverse (internal) tangent length \(= \sqrt{d^2 - (r_1+r_2)^2}\).
  • The intersection of the direct tangents divides the line of centres externally in the ratio \(r_1:r_2\).
  • The intersection of the transverse tangents divides the line of centres internally in the ratio \(r_1:r_2\).
  • Equal circles have parallel direct tangents; their transverse tangents meet at the midpoint of the centres.
  • Two circles touching externally (\(d = r_1+r_2\)) have 3 common tangents.
Example 1.

Find the length of the transverse common tangent between \(x^2+y^2 = 9\) and \((x-13)^2+y^2 = 4\).

  1. centres distance \(d = 13\), radii \(r_1 = 3,\ r_2 = 2\).
  2. transverse length \(= \sqrt{d^2 - (r_1+r_2)^2} = \sqrt{169 - 25}\).
  3. \(= \sqrt{144} = 12\).

Answer: transverse common tangent length \(= 12\)

Example 2.

How many common tangents do \(x^2+y^2 = 25\) and \((x-10)^2+y^2 = 25\) have?

  1. \(d = 10,\ r_1 = r_2 = 5\).
  2. \(d = r_1 + r_2\), so the circles touch externally.
  3. externally touching circles have 3 common tangents.

Answer: 3 common tangents

Common mistakes.
  • swapping the direct length \(\sqrt{d^2-(r_1-r_2)^2}\) and transverse length \(\sqrt{d^2-(r_1+r_2)^2}\)
  • counting 4 tangents for two touching circles
  • forgetting that internally touching circles (\(d = |r_1-r_2|\)) have just 1 common tangent
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 8

Family of Circles

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 7 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A one-parameter family of circles is built by combining two curves: \(S_1+\lambda S_2 = 0\) gives every circle passing through the common points of two circles (a coaxal family), and \(S+\lambda L = 0\) gives circles through the intersections of a circle with a line. One extra condition fixes the member.
Key formula(s). \[ S_1 + \lambda S_2 = 0 \ (\text{members share the common chord}), \qquad S + \lambda L = 0 \ (\text{through circle-line intersections}) \]
Key points.
  • All members of \(S_1 + \lambda S_2 = 0\) share the same radical axis (the common chord).
  • The value \(\lambda = -1\) converts the combination into a line (the radical axis), not a circle.
  • Fixing a point, a centre, or a tangency condition solves for \(\lambda\).
  • A member tangent to a given line is selected by imposing the tangent-discriminant condition.
  • The family is coaxal: the centres of its members lie on a fixed straight line.
  • Circles through two fixed points \(A, B\) form the family with \(AB\) as the common chord.
Example 1.

Find the circle passing through the intersection of \(x^2+y^2-4 = 0\) and \(x^2+y^2-4x-2y = 0\) that also passes through \((1,1)\).

  1. family: \(x^2+y^2-4 + \lambda(x^2+y^2-4x-2y) = 0\).
  2. at \((1,1)\): \(-2 + \lambda(1+1-4-2) = -2 - 4\lambda = 0\).
  3. \(\lambda = -\frac{1}{2}\).
  4. \(x^2+y^2-4 - \frac{1}{2}(x^2+y^2-4x-2y) = 0 \Rightarrow x^2+y^2+4x+2y-8 = 0\).

Answer: \(x^2+y^2+4x+2y-8 = 0\)

Example 2.

Find \(\lambda\) so that a member of the family \(x^2+y^2+2\lambda x + 6\lambda y + 5 = 0\) has radius 5.

  1. centre \((-\lambda, -3\lambda)\), so \(r^2 = \lambda^2 + 9\lambda^2 - 5 = 10\lambda^2 - 5\).
  2. set \(10\lambda^2 - 5 = 25\).
  3. \(10\lambda^2 = 30 \Rightarrow \lambda^2 = 3\).

Answer: \(\lambda = \pm\sqrt{3}\)

Common mistakes.
  • keeping \(\lambda = -1\) in \(S_1+\lambda S_2 = 0\) and still calling the result a circle
  • forgetting the constant term \(+5\) when reading off the radius from \(g,f,c\)
  • substituting a condition into only one of the combined factors
  • taking the centre of \(x^2+y^2+2\lambda x + 6\lambda y + 5 = 0\) as \((\lambda, 3\lambda)\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 18 · Topic 9

Tangent and Normal

CirclesCoordinate GeometryTopic 8 of 8

Explanation

Concept. The tangent to a circle at a point is perpendicular to the radius through the point of contact, and the normal passes through the centre and the point of contact. For \(x^2+y^2 = r^2\) the tangent at \((x_1,y_1)\) is \(xx_1+yy_1 = r^2\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{tangent at }(x_1,y_1) \text{ of } x^2+y^2 = r^2:\ xx_1+yy_1 = r^2; \qquad \text{normal at }(x_1,y_1):\ x_1y - y_1x = 0 \]
Key points.
  • Tangent slope at \((x_1,y_1)\) is \(-\frac{x_1}{y_1}\); the normal slope is \(\frac{y_1}{x_1}\).
  • General circle: the tangent at \((x_1,y_1)\) is \(T = 0\), where \(T = xx_1+yy_1+g(x+x_1)+f(y+y_1)+c\).
  • The line \(y = mx+c\) touches \(x^2+y^2 = r^2\) iff \(c = \pm r\sqrt{1+m^2}\).
  • Length of a tangent from an external point \(P\) equals \(\sqrt{S_1}\), the square root of the power of \(P\).
  • The normal always passes through the centre of the circle.
  • Tangents from an external point are equal in length and make equal angles with the line joining the point to the centre.
Example 1.

Find the tangent to \(x^2+y^2 = 25\) at the point \((3,-4)\).

  1. use \(xx_1 + yy_1 = r^2\) with \((x_1,y_1) = (3,-4)\): \(3x - 4y = 25\).
  2. check: the radius slope is \(-4/3\) and the tangent slope \(3/4\) are negative reciprocals.

Answer: tangent \(3x - 4y = 25\)

Example 2.

Find the length of the tangent drawn from \((7,1)\) to the circle \(x^2+y^2-2x-4y-20 = 0\).

  1. \(g = -1,\ f = -2,\ c = -20\).
  2. \(S_1 = 49 + 1 - 2(7) - 4(1) - 20 = 50 - 14 - 4 - 20 = 12\).
  3. tangent length \(= \sqrt{S_1} = \sqrt{12}\).

Answer: tangent length \(= 2\sqrt{3}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using \(xx_1+yy_1 = r^2\) for a circle that is not centred at the origin (missing the \(g, f, c\) terms)
  • quoting \(\sqrt{S_1}\) for the tangent length but computing \(S_1\) with the wrong signs from \(g, f\)
  • taking the normal slope instead of the tangent slope at the point of contact
Back to Index

Chapter 19 · Topic 2

General Equation of Parabola

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 1 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A parabola is the locus of a point whose distance from a fixed point (the focus) is equal to its distance from a fixed line (the directrix). The standard parabola with vertex at the origin and axis along the x-axis is \(y^2 = 4ax\), and the most general second-degree equation \(ax^2 + 2hxy + by^2 + 2gx + 2fy + c = 0\) represents a parabola exactly when \(h^2 = ab\) and the conic is non-degenerate.
Key formula(s). \[ y^2 = 4ax,\quad y^2 = -4ax,\quad x^2 = 4ay,\quad x^2 = -4ay;\qquad \text{latus rectum } = 4a \]
Key points.
  • \(y^2 = 4ax\) opens right: focus \((a,0)\), directrix \(x = -a\), axis \(y = 0\), vertex \((0,0)\)
  • \(y^2 = -4ax\) opens left: focus \((-a,0)\), directrix \(x = a\)
  • \(x^2 = 4ay\) opens up: focus \((0,a)\), directrix \(y = -a\), axis \(x = 0\)
  • \(x^2 = -4ay\) opens down: focus \((0,-a)\), directrix \(y = a\)
  • latus rectum is the chord through the focus perpendicular to the axis; length \(4a\), endpoints \((a, \pm 2a)\)
  • a curve \(y = Ax^2 + Bx + C\) is a parabola whose axis is \(x = -B/2A\)
  • the focus always lies inside the opening of the parabola, between the vertex and the directrix on the open side
Example 1.

Find the focus, directrix and latus rectum of the parabola \(y^2 = 12x\).

  1. compare with \(y^2 = 4ax\): \(4a = 12 \Rightarrow a = 3\)
  2. focus \((a, 0) = (3, 0)\)
  3. directrix \(x = -a = -3\)
  4. latus rectum \(= 4a = 12\)

Answer: focus \((3,0)\), directrix \(x = -3\), latus rectum \(12\)

Example 2.

Write the parabola \(x^2 = -16y\) in standard form and find its focus and directrix.

  1. standard form is \(x^2 = -4ay\) with \(4a = 16 \Rightarrow a = 4\)
  2. it opens downward with focus \((0, -a) = (0, -4)\)
  3. directrix \(y = a = 4\), vertex at the origin, axis the y-axis

Answer: focus \((0,-4)\), directrix \(y = 4\)

Common mistakes.
  • putting \(a\) equal to the whole coefficient, i.e. \(a = 12\) for \(y^2 = 12x\), instead of \(4a = 12\)
  • swapping focus and directrix signs: for \(y^2 = 4ax\) the focus is \((a,0)\) and the directrix is \(x = -a\)
  • confusing latus rectum length \(4a\) with the focal distance \(a\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 3

Pole and Polar for a Parabola

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 2 of 8

Explanation

Concept. The polar of a point \(P(x_1, y_1)\) with respect to the parabola \(y^2 = 4ax\) is obtained by the half-replacement rule \(y^2 \rightarrow y y_1\), \(x \rightarrow \frac{x + x_1}{2}\), giving \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\). If \(P\) lies on the parabola this polar is exactly the tangent at \(P\), and the point is then called the pole of that line.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{polar of } (x_1, y_1):\quad y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1) \]
Key points.
  • replacement rule: in \(y^2 = 4ax\) replace \(y^2 \rightarrow y y_1\) and \(2x \rightarrow x + x_1\)
  • if \((x_1, y_1)\) is on the parabola, the polar is the tangent \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\)
  • the pole of the line \(lx + my + n = 0\) is \(\left(\frac{n}{l}, -\frac{2am}{l}\right)\)
  • the polar of the focus \((a, 0)\) is the directrix \(x = -a\)
  • the polar of a point on the axis \((x_0, 0)\) is the vertical line \(x = -x_0\)
  • if the pole lies on the directrix \(x = -a\), its polar passes through the focus
Example 1.

Find the polar of the point \(P(2, 3)\) with respect to the parabola \(y^2 = 8x\).

  1. \(4a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 2\), so the polar is \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\)
  2. substituting \(x_1 = 2,\ y_1 = 3\): \(3y = 2 \cdot 2 (x + 2)\)
  3. \(3y = 4x + 8\)

Answer: \(4x - 3y + 8 = 0\)

Example 2.

Find the pole of the line \(x + 3y - 8 = 0\) with respect to \(y^2 = 12x\).

  1. \(4a = 12 \Rightarrow a = 3\); the polar of \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(y_1 y = 6(x + x_1)\), i.e. \(6x - y_1 y + 6x_1 = 0\)
  2. compare with \(x + 3y - 8 = 0\): ratios \(6/1 = -y_1/3 = 6x_1/(-8)\)
  3. from \(6/1 = -y_1/3\): \(y_1 = -18\); from \(6/1 = 6x_1/(-8)\): \(x_1 = -8\)

Answer: pole is \((-8, -18)\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the polar formula only when the point is on the parabola; it is valid for any point
  • comparing the polar with the given line while the constant signs are mismatched
  • forgetting to halve \(x\) via \(\frac{x + x_1}{2}\) when applying the replacement rule
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 4

Focal Distance and Parametric Coordinates

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 3 of 8

Explanation

Concept. Every point of \(y^2 = 4ax\) can be written parametrically as \(P(t) = (at^2, 2at)\), where the parameter \(t\) is the slope of the line from the origin to the point. The focal distance of a point on the parabola, its distance from the focus \((a,0)\), is \(x_1 + a\), which equals \(a(1 + t^2)\) in parametric form.
Key formula(s). \[ P(t) \equiv (at^2, 2at),\qquad \text{focal distance } = x_1 + a = a(1 + t^2) \]
Key points.
  • \((at^2, 2at)\) satisfies \(y^2 = 4ax\) identically since \((2at)^2 = 4a(at^2)\)
  • the slope of the tangent at parameter \(t\) is \(1/t\)
  • focal distance \(SP = x_1 + a = a(1 + t^2)\)
  • endpoints of the latus rectum correspond to \(t = \pm 1\): \((a, \pm 2a)\)
  • a focal chord subtending parameters satisfies \(t_1 t_2 = -1\)
  • the midpoint of the chord joining \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) is \(\left(\frac{a}{2}(t_1^2 + t_2^2), a(t_1 + t_2)\right)\)
Example 1.

A point with parameter \(t = 2\) lies on the parabola \(y^2 = 4x\). Find its coordinates and focal distance.

  1. \(a = 1\), so the point is \((at^2, 2at) = (1 \cdot 4, 2 \cdot 1 \cdot 2)\)
  2. the point is \((4, 4)\)
  3. focal distance \(= x + a = 4 + 1 = 5\)
  4. check: \(a(1 + t^2) = 1(1 + 4) = 5\)

Answer: point \((4,4)\), focal distance \(5\)

Example 2.

The focal distance of a point on the parabola \(y^2 = 16x\) is 20. Find its coordinates.

  1. \(4a = 16 \Rightarrow a = 4\)
  2. focal distance \(= x_1 + a = x_1 + 4 = 20 \Rightarrow x_1 = 16\)
  3. on the parabola: \(y_1^2 = 16 \times 16 \Rightarrow y_1 = \pm 16\)

Answer: points are \((16, 16)\) and \((16, -16)\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing the parametric point as \((2at^2, at)\) instead of \((at^2, 2at)\)
  • taking the focal distance as \(x_1 - a\) or \(a(1 - t^2)\)
  • forgetting that focal chord endpoints use parameters \(t\) and \(-1/t\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 5

Chord of a Parabola

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 4 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A chord of a parabola is the line segment joining two points of the parabola. The chord joining the parametric points \(P(t_1)\) and \(Q(t_2)\) of \(y^2 = 4ax\) has equation \(y(t_1 + t_2) = 2x + 2a t_1 t_2\), and it passes through the focus exactly when \(t_1 t_2 = -1\), in which case it is called a focal chord.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{chord } P(t_1)Q(t_2):\quad y(t_1 + t_2) = 2x + 2a\, t_1 t_2;\qquad \text{focal chord} \iff t_1 t_2 = -1 \]
Key points.
  • the slope of the chord joining \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) is \(\frac{2}{t_1 + t_2}\)
  • for a focal chord, \(t_2 = -1/t_1\), so the endpoints are \((a t_1^2, 2a t_1)\) and \((a/t_1^2, -2a/t_1)\)
  • the length of that focal chord is \(a\left(t_1 + \frac{1}{t_1}\right)^2\)
  • the chord of contact from an external point \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\), the polar of the point
  • the latus rectum is the focal chord perpendicular to the axis; its endpoints are \((a, \pm 2a)\)
  • the chord whose midpoint is \((x_1, y_1)\) satisfies \(T = S_1\), i.e. \(y y_1 - 2a x-2a x_1 = y_1^2 - 4a x_1\)
Example 1.

Find the equation of the chord of \(y^2 = 8x\) joining the points with parameters \(t_1 = 1\) and \(t_2 = 3\).

  1. \(4a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 2\)
  2. chord equation \(y(t_1 + t_2) = 2x + 2a t_1 t_2\) gives \(y(4) = 2x + 2 \cdot 2 \cdot 3\)
  3. \(4y = 2x + 12\)

Answer: \(y = \frac{x}{2} + 3\)

Example 2.

A focal chord of the parabola \(y^2 = 4x\) goes through the parameter point \(t_1 = 2\). Find \(t_2\) and the length of the chord.

  1. focal chord condition: \(t_1 t_2 = -1 \Rightarrow t_2 = -\frac{1}{2}\)
  2. endpoints are \((4, 4)\) and \((1/4, -1)\)
  3. length \(= \sqrt{(4 - 1/4)^2 + (4 + 1)^2} = \sqrt{225/16 + 25} = \sqrt{625/16}\)

Answer: \(t_2 = -1/2\), chord length \(= \frac{25}{4}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using \(t_1 + t_2 = 0\) for a focal chord instead of \(t_1 t_2 = -1\)
  • dropping the factor \(2a\) in the \(2a t_1 t_2\) constant term of the chord equation
  • confusing the chord of contact (polar of an external point) with a chord joining two on-curve points
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 6

Properties of Parabola

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 5 of 8

Explanation

Concept. The parabola \(y^2 = 4ax\) is the locus of points equidistant from focus and directrix, which gives it a rich set of symmetric and reflective properties: it is symmetric about its own axis, reflects rays parallel to the axis into the focus, and has many interplay relations between tangents, chords and the focal triangle.
Key formula(s). \[ SP = \text{distance to directrix } = x_1 + a;\qquad \text{tangents at }t_1, t_2 \text{ meet at } (a t_1 t_2,\ a(t_1 + t_2)) \]
Key points.
  • axis of symmetry is \(y = 0\) for \(y^2 = 4ax\), since replacing \(y\) by \(-y\) leaves the equation unchanged
  • the tangent at the vertex is \(x = 0\), perpendicular to the axis
  • reflection property: a ray travelling parallel to the axis reflects through the focus \((a,0)\)
  • the circle drawn on any focal chord as diameter touches the directrix
  • tangents at the ends of a focal chord are perpendicular (slopes \(1/t\) and \(-t\))
  • the two tangents at \(t_1, t_2\) intersect at the point \((a t_1 t_2, a(t_1 + t_2))\)
  • the feet of the perpendiculars from the focus on the tangents lie on the tangent at the vertex
Example 1.

Show that the tangents at the ends of a focal chord of \(y^2 = 4ax\) are perpendicular.

  1. ends of a focal chord have parameters \(t\) and \(-1/t\)
  2. the slope of the tangent at parameter \(t\) is \(1/t\)
  3. the slope of the tangent at parameter \(-1/t\) is \(-t\)
  4. product of slopes \(= (1/t)(-t) = -1\)

Answer: hence the two tangents are perpendicular

Example 2.

The tangents at \(t_1 = 1\) and \(t_2 = 3\) of \(y^2 = 8x\) meet at T. Find T.

  1. \(4a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 2\)
  2. intersection of tangents is \((a t_1 t_2, a(t_1 + t_2))\)
  3. \(x = 2 \cdot 3 = 6,\ y = 2(1 + 3) = 8\)

Answer: \(T = (6, 8)\)

Common mistakes.
  • taking the y-axis as the axis of \(y^2 = 4ax\)
  • confusing the coordinates \(a t_1 t_2\) and \(a(t_1 + t_2)\) of the tangent-intersection point
  • thinking the tangent at the vertex is the x-axis for the standard form \(y^2 = 4ax\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 7

Introduction to Conic

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 6 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A conic section is the locus of a point whose distance from a fixed point (focus) is a constant ratio \(e\) times its perpendicular distance from a fixed line (directrix). The eccentricity decides the type: parabola \((e = 1)\), ellipse \((0 < e < 1)\), or hyperbola \((e > 1)\).
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{SP}{PM} = e,\qquad S \text{ the focus, } M \text{ the foot on the directrix} \]
Key points.
  • \(e = 1\): parabola, \(0 < e < 1\): ellipse, \(e > 1\): hyperbola, \(e = 0\): circle
  • a conic is the curve of intersection of a plane with a right circular double cone
  • a plane parallel to a generator produces a parabola; a steeper cut meeting both nappes gives a hyperbola with two branches
  • general conic: \(ax^2 + 2hxy + by^2 + 2gx + 2fy + c = 0\), representing a non-degenerate conic when \(\Delta \ne 0\)
  • type test: \(h^2 - ab = 0\) parabola, \(h^2 - ab < 0\) ellipse, \(h^2 - ab > 0\) hyperbola; \(h = 0,\ a = b\) gives a circle
  • degenerate cases \((\Delta = 0)\): a point, a pair of straight lines, or the empty set
  • the focus-directrix definition is the unifying definition for all three non-degenerate conics
Example 1.

If \(SP = 10\) and the perpendicular distance of P from the directrix is 5, identify the conic.

  1. eccentricity \(e = \frac{SP}{PM} = \frac{10}{5} = 2\)
  2. since \(e = 2 > 1\), the conic is a hyperbola

Answer: hyperbola

Example 2.

Classify the conic \(x^2 + 4xy + 4y^2 - 3x + 5 = 0\).

  1. compare with \(ax^2 + 2hxy + by^2 + \dots = 0\): \(a = 1, b = 4\), and \(2h = 4 \Rightarrow h = 2\)
  2. \(h^2 - ab = 4 - (1)(4) = 0\)
  3. hence the conic is a parabola

Answer: parabola

Common mistakes.
  • assigning the wrong eccentricity ranges (e.g. \(e < 1\) for a hyperbola)
  • taking \(h\) as the whole coefficient of \(xy\) instead of half of it
  • ignoring the non-degeneracy condition \(\Delta \ne 0\) when classifying
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 8

Tangent and Normal

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 7 of 8

Explanation

Concept. The tangent to the parabola \(y^2 = 4ax\) at the parametric point \((at^2, 2at)\) is \(ty = x + at^2\), equivalently \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\) in Cartesian form; the normal there is \(y + tx = 2at + at^3\). A line \(y = mx + c\) is a tangent iff \(c = \frac{a}{m}\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{tangent: } ty = x + a t^2,\quad y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1);\qquad \text{normal: } y + tx = 2at + a t^3;\qquad y = mx + \frac{a}{m} \]
Key points.
  • slope of the tangent at parameter \(t\) is \(\frac{1}{t}\), so the normal's slope is \(-t\)
  • the tangent at \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(y y_1 = 2a(x + x_1)\)
  • slope form: \(y = mx + \frac{a}{m}\) touches \(y^2 = 4ax\) for every \(m \ne 0\)
  • tangents at \(t_1, t_2\) meet at \((a t_1 t_2, a(t_1 + t_2))\)
  • from an external point three normals can be drawn; their slope-parameters \(t_1, t_2, t_3\) satisfy \(t_1 + t_2 + t_3 = 0\) and \(t_1 t_2 t_3 = -1\)
  • the foot of the perpendicular from the focus to any tangent lies on the tangent at the vertex
Example 1.

Find the tangent to \(y^2 = 8x\) at the point with parameter \(t = 2\).

  1. \(4a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 2\)
  2. tangent at \(t\): \(ty = x + at^2\), so \(2y = x + 2 \cdot 4\)
  3. \(2y = x + 8\)

Answer: \(x - 2y + 8 = 0\)

Example 2.

Find the tangent to \(y^2 = 8x\) parallel to the line \(x - 2y + 5 = 0\).

  1. slope of the given line \(m = 1/2\)
  2. slope form of tangent: \(y = mx + \frac{a}{m}\) with \(a = 2\)
  3. \(y = \frac{x}{2} + \frac{2}{1/2} = \frac{x}{2} + 4\)

Answer: \(x - 2y + 8 = 0\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing the normal with the wrong sign of slope, e.g. \(y = tx + \dots\) instead of \(y = -tx + \dots\)
  • using \(y = mx + \frac{a}{m}\) for the \(x^2 = 4ay\) family without adapting the formula
  • forgetting that the tangent constant term uses \(a t^2\), not \(t^2\)
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 19 · Topic 9

Transformation

ParabolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 8 of 8

Explanation

Concept. A parabola whose vertex has been shifted to \((h, k)\) has equation \((y - k)^2 = 4a(x - h)\), obtained by translating the origin of the standard form. Quadratic equations of the form \(y = Ax^2 + Bx + C\) are converted to this shifted standard form by completing the square, after which focus, directrix and axis follow from the standard values.
Key formula(s). \[ (y - k)^2 = 4a(x - h):\ \text{vertex }(h,k),\ \text{focus }(h + a, k),\ \text{directrix }x = h - a \]
Key points.
  • shifting the origin to \((h, k)\) replaces \(x\) by \(x - h\) and \(y\) by \(y - k\)
  • \((y - k)^2 = 4a(x - h)\): axis \(y = k\), focus \((h + a, k)\), directrix \(x = h - a\)
  • \((x - h)^2 = 4a(y - k)\): axis \(x = h\), focus \((h, k + a)\), directrix \(y = k - a\)
  • the latus rectum length \(4a\) is unaffected by translation or rotation
  • \(y = Ax^2 + Bx + C\) becomes \((x - h)^2 = 4a(y - k)\) with \(h = -B/(2A)\)
  • rotating the axes can eliminate an \(xy\) term, restoring the standard aligned form
Example 1.

Find the vertex, focus and directrix of \(y^2 - 6y + 8x + 1 = 0\).

  1. complete the square: \(y^2 - 6y = (y - 3)^2 - 9\)
  2. \((y - 3)^2 - 9 + 8x + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow (y - 3)^2 = -8(x - 1)\)
  3. compare \((y - k)^2 = -4a(x - h)\): \(k = 3,\ h = 1,\ 4a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 2\)
  4. focus \((h - a, k) = (-1, 3)\); directrix \(x = h + a = 3\)

Answer: vertex \((1, 3)\), focus \((-1, 3)\), directrix \(x = 3\)

Example 2.

Express \(y = x^2 - 4x + 7\) in shifted standard form and find its vertex.

  1. complete the square: \(y = (x - 2)^2 - 4 + 7 = (x - 2)^2 + 3\)
  2. \((x - 2)^2 = y - 3\), i.e. \((x - h)^2 = 4a(y - k)\) with \(h = 2,\ k = 3\)
  3. \(4a = 1\), so \(a = 1/4\)

Answer: vertex \((2, 3)\)

Common mistakes.
  • mis-handling the sign after completing the square, e.g. writing \((y-3)^2 = 8(x-1)\) instead of \(-8(x-1)\)
  • adding the shift to the wrong coordinate when identifying focus and directrix
  • forgetting that completion leaves \(4a\) as the only coefficient of the linear variable term
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 20 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

An ellipse is formed around two foci; the sum of the distances to the two foci is constant. Standard form \( x^{2}/a^{2} + y^{2}/b^{2} = 1 \) with a \( > \) b. Here a is the semi-major axis and b the semi-minor axis. Foci are at \( (\pm \sqrt (a^{2} - b^{2}) \) …

Chapter 20 · Topic 2

Chord of Ellipse

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 1 of 6

Explanation

Concept. A chord of the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is a line segment joining two points of the ellipse. The chord joining the parametric points with eccentric angles \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) has a compact half-angle form that reduces to the tangent equation when the two angles coincide.
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{x}{a}\cos\frac{\alpha + \beta}{2} + \frac{y}{b}\sin\frac{\alpha + \beta}{2} = \cos\frac{\alpha - \beta}{2} \]
Key points.
  • as \(\alpha \to \beta\), the chord becomes the tangent \(\frac{x\cos\alpha}{a} + \frac{y\sin\alpha}{b} = 1\)
  • the chord of contact from an external point \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\)
  • the chord whose midpoint is \((x_1, y_1)\) has equation \(T = S_1\), i.e. \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = \frac{x_1^2}{a^2} + \frac{y_1^2}{b^2}\)
  • a chord through the centre is a diameter; the midpoints of parallel chords lie on a diameter
  • the latus rectum is the chord through a focus perpendicular to the major axis, of length \(\frac{2b^2}{a}\)
  • chords parallel to the y-axis are bisected by the x-axis by symmetry
Example 1.

Find the chord of \(\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\) joining the parametric points with \(\alpha = 0\) and \(\beta = \frac{\pi}{2}\).

  1. \(a = 3,\ b = 2,\ (\alpha + \beta)/2 = \pi/4,\ (\alpha - \beta)/2 = -\pi/4\)
  2. chord: \(\frac{x}{3}\cos\frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{y}{2}\sin\frac{\pi}{4} = \cos\left(-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\)
  3. multiply by \(\sqrt{2}\): \(\frac{x}{3} + \frac{y}{2} = 1\)

Answer: \(\frac{x}{3} + \frac{y}{2} = 1\)

Example 2.

Find the chord of contact of the point \((2, 1)\) with respect to the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{y^2}{2} = 1\).

  1. chord of contact: \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\) with \(a^2 = 8,\ b^2 = 2\)
  2. \(\frac{2x}{8} + \frac{1 \cdot y}{2} = 1\)
  3. \(\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{2} = 1\)

Answer: \(x + 2y = 4\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting the half-angles \((\alpha+\beta)/2\) and \((\alpha-\beta)/2\) when writing the chord equation
  • swapping the denominators \(a\) and \(b\) while keeping the same angles
  • confusing the chord of contact with the secant chord through a point of the ellipse

Chapter 20 · Topic 3

Common Articles in Ellipse

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 2 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The standard ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) (\(a > b\), major axis along the x-axis) is associated with the auxiliary circle \(x^2 + y^2 = a^2\) described on the major axis, on which the eccentric angle \(\theta\) is measured; the director circle \(x^2 + y^2 = a^2 + b^2\) is the locus of points from which perpendicular tangents can be drawn.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{auxiliary circle } x^2 + y^2 = a^2;\qquad \text{director circle } x^2 + y^2 = a^2 + b^2 \]
Key points.
  • eccentricity \(e = \sqrt{1 - b^2/a^2}\) with \(0 < e < 1\)
  • foci \((\pm ae, 0)\) and directrices \(x = \pm a/e\)
  • the auxiliary circle has radius \(a\); its centre coincides with the ellipse centre
  • the ordinate of the ellipse is \((b/a)\) times the ordinate of the auxiliary circle at the same abscissa
  • the director circle is the largest track from which perpendicular tangents exist; for a circle it degenerates to a point
  • latus rectum of the ellipse is \(\frac{2b^2}{a}\)
  • the parametric point \((a\cos\theta, b\sin\theta)\) uses \(\theta\) as the eccentric angle on the auxiliary circle
Example 1.

For the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\) find the foci and the equations of the directrices.

  1. \(a^2 = 25,\ b^2 = 9,\ e = \sqrt{1 - 9/25} = 4/5\)
  2. foci \((\pm 5 \cdot 4/5, 0) = (\pm 4, 0)\)
  3. directrices \(x = \pm a/e = \pm 5/(4/5) = \pm 25/4\)

Answer: foci \((\pm 4, 0)\), directrices \(x = \pm \frac{25}{4}\)

Example 2.

Find the eccentricity of the ellipse whose auxiliary circle is \(x^2 + y^2 = 16\) and whose semi-minor axis is 3.

  1. auxiliary circle radius \(a = 4\), and \(b = 3\)
  2. \(e = \sqrt{1 - b^2/a^2} = \sqrt{1 - 9/16}\)
  3. \(e = \sqrt{7/16}\)

Answer: \(e = \frac{\sqrt{7}}{4}\)

Common mistakes.
  • drawing the auxiliary circle with radius \(b\) instead of \(a\)
  • using the ellipse eccentricity formula as \(\sqrt{1 + b^2/a^2}\)
  • calling the directrices of the x-major ellipse \(y = \pm a/e\) instead of \(x = \pm a/e\)

Chapter 20 · Topic 4

Tangent and Normal

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 3 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The tangent to the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) at \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\), and in parametric form \(\frac{x\cos\theta}{a} + \frac{y\sin\theta}{b} = 1\); the normal at \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{a^2 x}{x_1} - \frac{b^2 y}{y_1} = a^2 - b^2\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{tangent: } \frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\ \text{or}\ \frac{x\cos\theta}{a} + \frac{y\sin\theta}{b} = 1;\qquad \text{normal: }\frac{a^2 x}{x_1} - \frac{b^2 y}{y_1} = a^2 - b^2 \]
Key points.
  • slope form: the line \(y = mx + c\) touches the ellipse iff \(c^2 = a^2 m^2 + b^2\), giving \(y = mx \pm \sqrt{a^2 m^2 + b^2}\)
  • slope of the tangent at eccentric angle \(\theta\) is \(-\frac{b}{a}\cot\theta\)
  • parametric tangent: \(\frac{x\cos\theta}{a} + \frac{y\sin\theta}{b} = 1\)
  • parametric normal: \(\frac{a x}{\cos\theta} - \frac{b y}{\sin\theta} = a^2 - b^2\)
  • the tangent cuts the coordinate axes at \((a^2/x_1, 0)\) and \((0, b^2/y_1)\)
  • tangents from a point exist for every point outside the ellipse; the chord of contact is the polar
Example 1.

Find the tangent and normal to \(\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\) at the point with eccentric angle \(\theta = \pi/4\).

  1. the point is \((3\cos 45^\circ, 2\sin 45^\circ) = (3/\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2})\)
  2. tangent: \(\frac{x\cos\theta}{3} + \frac{y\sin\theta}{2} = 1 \Rightarrow \frac{x}{3\sqrt{2}} + \frac{y}{2\sqrt{2}} = 1\)
  3. normal: \(\frac{3x}{\cos\theta} - \frac{2y}{\sin\theta} = 3^2 - 2^2 \Rightarrow 3\sqrt{2}x - 2\sqrt{2}y = 5\)

Answer: tangent \(x/(3\sqrt{2}) + y/(2\sqrt{2}) = 1\), normal \(3\sqrt{2}x - 2\sqrt{2}y = 5\)

Example 2.

Find the tangents to \(\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\) having slope \(m = -1\).

  1. condition of tangency: \(c^2 = a^2 m^2 + b^2\)
  2. \(a^2 = 9,\ b^2 = 4,\ m = -1\): \(c^2 = 9 + 4 = 13\)
  3. \(c = \pm\sqrt{13}\)

Answer: \(y = -x \pm \sqrt{13}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the hyperbola normal RHS \((a^2 + b^2)\) instead of \((a^2 - b^2)\)
  • applying the ellipse tangent formula with a minus sign between the two terms
  • solving the slope-form condition with \(c^2 = a^2 m^2 - b^2\) (that is the hyperbola rule)

Chapter 20 · Topic 5

Properties of the Ellipse

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 4 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The ellipse is the locus of a point whose sum of distances from two fixed points, the foci, is the constant \(2a\); this focal property combines with the eccentricity and reflection laws to characterise the ellipse. On the standard ellipse the two focal distances of a point with abscissa \(x_1\) are \(a \pm e x_1\).
Key formula(s). \[ PF_1 + PF_2 = 2a,\qquad e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \]
Key points.
  • the sum of the focal distances of any point of the ellipse is always \(2a\)
  • individual focal distances of a point with abscissa \(x_1\) are \(a - e x_1\) and \(a + e x_1\)
  • reflection property: a ray from one focus reflects off the ellipse toward the other focus
  • the tangent and normal at a point bisect the external and internal angles between the focal radii
  • the ellipse is contained in the rectangle \(|x| \le a,\ |y| \le b\)
  • the area of the ellipse is \(\pi a b\)
  • the eccentric angles of the endpoints of conjugate diameters differ by \(\pi/2\)
Example 1.

Find the focal distances of a point of \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\) whose abscissa is \(x_1 = 1\).

  1. \(a = 5,\ b = 3,\ e = \sqrt{1 - 9/25} = 4/5\)
  2. focal distances are \(a - e x_1\) and \(a + e x_1\)
  3. \(5 - (4/5)(1) = 21/5,\quad 5 + (4/5)(1) = 29/5\)
  4. their sum is \(21/5 + 29/5 = 10 = 2a\), as required

Answer: \(\frac{21}{5}\) and \(\frac{29}{5}\)

Example 2.

The focal distances of a point on an ellipse are 8 and 12. Find the length of the major axis.

  1. by the focal property \(PF_1 + PF_2 = 2a\)
  2. \(2a = 8 + 12 = 20\)

Answer: major axis \(= 20\)

Common mistakes.
  • assuming the two focal distances of a point are always equal to \(a\) each
  • writing the focal distances as \(a \pm e^2 x_1\)
  • using the focal-distance difference \(|PF_1 - PF_2| = 2a\), which is the hyperbola property

Chapter 20 · Topic 6

Pole and Polar

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 5 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The polar of the point \((x_1, y_1)\) with respect to the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is found by replacing \(x^2 \to x x_1\) and \(y^2 \to y y_1\), giving \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\). If the point lies on the ellipse, its polar is the tangent at that point.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{polar of }(x_1, y_1):\quad \frac{x x_1}{a^2} + \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1 \]
Key points.
  • replacement rule applies term by term: \(x^2 \to x x_1\), \(y^2 \to y y_1\), RHS unchanged at 1
  • for a point on the ellipse, the polar is the tangent
  • the pole of the line \(lx + my = 1\) is \((a^2 l, b^2 m)\)
  • the polar of a focus \((\pm ae, 0)\) is the corresponding directrix \(x = \pm a/e\)
  • the polar of the centre \((0, 0)\) is a degenerate line at infinity
  • if the point is strictly inside the ellipse, the polar does not meet the ellipse in real points
Example 1.

Find the polar of the point \((1, 2)\) with respect to the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\).

  1. polar: \(\frac{x \cdot 1}{9} + \frac{y \cdot 2}{4} = 1\)
  2. \(\frac{x}{9} + \frac{y}{2} = 1\)
  3. multiply through by 18

Answer: \(2x + 9y = 18\)

Example 2.

Find the pole of the line \(3x + 5y = 1\) with respect to the ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{16} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\).

  1. polar of \((x_1, y_1)\): \(\frac{x x_1}{16} + \frac{y y_1}{4} = 1\)
  2. compare with \(3x + 5y = 1\): \(\frac{x_1}{16} = 3,\ \frac{y_1}{4} = 5\)
  3. \(x_1 = 48,\ y_1 = 20\)

Answer: pole \((48, 20)\)

Common mistakes.
  • comparing a line written as \(lx + my + n = 0\) with the polar form without first dividing by \(-n\) to reach \(lx + my = 1\)
  • using the hyperbola polar with a minus sign
  • expecting the polar of an interior point to touch the ellipse in visible real points

Chapter 20 · Topic 7

Parameters Related to Ellipse

EllipseCoordinate GeometryTopic 6 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The standard ellipse \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is summarised by its parameters: semi-major axis \(a\), semi-minor axis \(b\), eccentricity \(e = \sqrt{1 - b^2/a^2}\), foci \((\pm ae, 0)\), directrices \(x = \pm a/e\) and latus rectum \(\frac{2b^2}{a}\).
Key formula(s). \[ e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}},\quad \text{foci }(\pm ae, 0),\quad \text{directrices }x = \pm \frac{a}{e},\quad \text{latus rectum }= \frac{2b^2}{a} \]
Key points.
  • the longer of the axes is the major axis; for the x-major form \(a > b\)
  • the distance between the foci is \(2ae\), and between the directrices \(2a/e\)
  • the ends of the latus rectum are \((ae, \pm b^2/a)\)
  • the parametric point is \((a\cos\theta, b\sin\theta)\)
  • the focal distances of the point are \(a \pm e x_1\)
  • the equation of the ellipse given its focus and directrix data follows from \(b^2 = a^2(1 - e^2)\)
Example 1.

For \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\) find the eccentricity, foci and latus rectum.

  1. \(a^2 = 25,\ b^2 = 16,\ e = \sqrt{1 - 16/25} = 3/5\)
  2. foci \((\pm 5 \cdot 3/5, 0) = (\pm 3, 0)\)
  3. latus rectum \(= \frac{2b^2}{a} = \frac{32}{5}\)

Answer: \(e = 3/5\), foci \((\pm 3, 0)\), latus rectum \(32/5\)

Example 2.

The foci of an ellipse are \((\pm 4, 0)\) and its directrices are \(x = \pm 9\). Find the equation of the ellipse.

  1. focus gives \(ae = 4\) and the directrix gives \(a/e = 9\)
  2. multiplying, \(a^2 = 36\), hence \(a = 6\) and \(e = 4/6 = 2/3\)
  3. \(b^2 = a^2(1 - e^2) = 36(1 - 4/9) = 36 \cdot 5/9 = 20\)

Answer: \(\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{20} = 1\)

Common mistakes.
  • taking latus rectum as \(b^2/a\) and dropping the factor 2
  • using the hyperbola relation \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1)\)
  • confusing vertices \((\pm a, 0)\) with foci \((\pm ae, 0)\)

Introduction & Instructions

A hyperbola has two branches where the difference of the distances to the foci is constant. Standard form \( x^{2}/a^{2} - y^{2}/b^{2} = 1 \) with asymptotes y \( = \pm arunx \) and foci at \( (\pm \sqrt (a^{2} + b^{2}), 0) \) Its eccentricity is always greater …

Chapter 21 · Topic 2

Shifted and Conjugate Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 1 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) has a conjugate hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = -1\) that shares the same asymptotes while interchanging the transverse and conjugate axes. A hyperbola whose centre is shifted to \((h, k)\) takes the form \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\).
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1;\qquad \text{conjugate: } \frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1 \]
Key points.
  • the conjugate of \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = -1\), i.e. \(\frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1\)
  • the two hyperbolas share the same asymptotes \(y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x\)
  • eccentricities obey \(\frac{1}{e^2} + \frac{1}{(e')^2} = 1\), where \(e'\) is the conjugate's eccentricity
  • for a shifted hyperbola, centre \((h, k)\), transverse axis parallel to the x-axis, vertices \((h \pm a, k)\)
  • foci of the shifted hyperbola: \((h \pm ae, k)\), directrices \(x = h \pm a/e\)
  • the transverse and conjugate axes exchange roles between a hyperbola and its conjugate
  • completing the squares converts a general equation into the shifted standard form
Example 1.

Find the centre, vertices and axis lengths of \(\frac{(x-2)^2}{9} - \frac{(y+1)^2}{4} = 1\).

  1. centre \((h, k) = (2, -1)\)
  2. \(a^2 = 9,\ b^2 = 4\), so \(a = 3,\ b = 2\)
  3. vertices \((h \pm a, k) = (2 \pm 3, -1)\)
  4. transverse axis \(2a = 6\), conjugate axis \(2b = 4\)

Answer: centre \((2, -1)\), vertices \((5, -1)\) and \((-1, -1)\), axes \(6\) and \(4\)

Example 2.

Write the conjugate hyperbola of \(\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\) and give its transverse axis.

  1. conjugate is \(\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{9} = -1\), i.e. \(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1\)
  2. here the roles swap: new \(a' = 3,\ b' = 4\)
  3. the transverse axis is along the y-axis and has length \(2a' = 6\)

Answer: \(y^2/9 - x^2/16 = 1\), transverse axis length 6 along the y-axis

Common mistakes.
  • shifting the wrong axis when \((x-h)^2\) and \((y-k)^2\) swap signs
  • forgetting that the conjugate hyperbola swaps the transverse and conjugate axes
  • using \(\frac{1}{e^2} - \frac{1}{(e')^2} = 1\) for the eccentricity linkage

Chapter 21 · Topic 3

Common Terms of Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 2 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) has its transverse axis along the x-axis: centre \((0,0)\), vertices \((\pm a,0)\), foci \((\pm ae,0)\), directrices \(x = \pm a/e\), latus rectum \(\frac{2b^2}{a}\) and eccentricity \(e = \sqrt{1 + b^2/a^2} > 1\).
Key formula(s). \[ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}},\quad \text{foci }(\pm ae, 0),\quad \text{directrices }x = \pm \frac{a}{e},\quad \text{latus rectum }= \frac{2b^2}{a} \]
Key points.
  • the curve consists of two disjoint branches opening along \(\pm x\)
  • transverse axis \(= 2a\) (along x-axis), conjugate axis \(= 2b\)
  • vertices \((\pm a, 0)\) are points of the curve closest to the centre
  • parametric point: \((a\sec\theta, b\tan\theta)\)
  • focal property: \(|PF_1 - PF_2| = 2a\)
  • distance between foci \(2ae\), between directrices \(2a/e\)
  • ends of the latus rectum: \((ae, \pm b^2/a)\)
Example 1.

For \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\), find the eccentricity, foci and latus rectum.

  1. \(a^2 = 9,\ b^2 = 16,\ a = 3,\ b = 4\)
  2. \(e = \sqrt{1 + 16/9} = \sqrt{25/9} = 5/3\)
  3. foci \((\pm 3 \cdot 5/3, 0) = (\pm 5, 0)\)
  4. latus rectum \(= 2b^2/a = 32/3\)

Answer: \(e = 5/3\), foci \((\pm 5, 0)\), latus rectum \(32/3\)

Example 2.

The distance between the foci of a hyperbola is 16 and its transverse axis is 12. Find \(b^2\) and the eccentricity.

  1. \(2ae = 16 \Rightarrow ae = 8\); \(2a = 12 \Rightarrow a = 6\)
  2. \(e = 8/6 = 4/3\)
  3. \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1) = 36(16/9 - 1) = 36 \cdot 7/9 = 28\)

Answer: \(b^2 = 28,\ e = 4/3\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the ellipse formula \(e = \sqrt{1 - b^2/a^2}\)
  • taking the vertices as \((\pm ae, 0)\) and foci as \((\pm a, 0)\), i.e. swapping them
  • writing the latus rectum as \(\frac{b^2}{a}\) without the factor 2

Chapter 21 · Topic 4

Asymptotes

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 3 of 10

Explanation

Concept. An asymptote of a hyperbola is a line that the curve approaches indefinitely as the coordinates grow large, meeting it only at infinity. The asymptotes of \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) are \(y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x\), obtained by replacing the constant 1 with 0.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{asymptotes: } y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x,\quad \text{i.e. } \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 0 \]
Key points.
  • the pair of asymptotes is the equation of the hyperbola with the constant 1 replaced by 0
  • the two asymptotes meet at the centre \((0, 0)\) and separate the two branches
  • the asymptotes of the rectangular hyperbola \(xy = c^2\) are the axes \(x = 0,\ y = 0\)
  • a hyperbola and its conjugate share the same asymptotes
  • the shifted hyperbola has shifted asymptotes: \(y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)\)
  • every hyperbola has exactly two asymptotes; a parabola or ellipse in the plane has none in the usual finite sense
  • along an asymptote the hyperbola is never reached, so asymptotes never contain points of the curve
Example 1.

Find the asymptotes of the hyperbola \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\).

  1. divide by 36: \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\), so \(a = 3,\ b = 2\)
  2. asymptotes: \(y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{2}{3}x\)
  3. equivalently \(2x - 3y = 0\) and \(2x + 3y = 0\)

Answer: \(2x - 3y = 0\) and \(2x + 3y = 0\)

Example 2.

The asymptotes of a hyperbola are \(x + 2y - 3 = 0\) and \(x - 2y + 1 = 0\). Find the centre.

  1. the centre is the intersection point of the two asymptotes
  2. add the equations: \(2x - 2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1\)
  3. then \(1 + 2y - 3 = 0 \Rightarrow y = 1\)

Answer: centre \((1, 1)\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing the asymptotes as \(y = \pm \frac{a}{b}x\)
  • thinking the asymptotes are found by setting \(y = 0\) or \(x = 0\) in the hyperbola equation
  • forgetting the shifted form \(y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)\) when the centre is not the origin

Chapter 21 · Topic 5

Tangent and Normal

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 4 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The tangent to the hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) at \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} - \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\), and in parametric form \(\frac{x\sec\theta}{a} - \frac{y\tan\theta}{b} = 1\). The normal at \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{a^2 x}{x_1} + \frac{b^2 y}{y_1} = a^2 + b^2\).
Key formula(s). \[ \text{tangent: } \frac{x x_1}{a^2} - \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\ \text{or}\ \frac{x\sec\theta}{a} - \frac{y\tan\theta}{b} = 1;\qquad \text{normal: } \frac{a^2 x}{x_1} + \frac{b^2 y}{y_1} = a^2 + b^2 \]
Key points.
  • slope form: \(y = mx + c\) touches the hyperbola iff \(c^2 = a^2 m^2 - b^2\), so \(y = mx \pm \sqrt{a^2 m^2 - b^2}\)
  • real tangents in slope form demand \(a^2 m^2 \ge b^2\)
  • slope of the tangent at parameter \(\theta\) is \(\frac{b}{a}\frac{\sec\theta}{\tan\theta} = \frac{b}{a\sin\theta}\)
  • the parametric normal is \(a x \cos\theta + b y \cot\theta = a^2 + b^2\)
  • the tangent at a vertex \((a, 0)\) is the vertical line \(x = a\)
  • tangents at the ends of the latus rectum meet on the corresponding directrix
Example 1.

Find the tangent to \(\frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\) at the point with parameter \(\theta = \pi/4\).

  1. the point is \((a\sec\theta, b\tan\theta) = (2\sqrt{2}, 3)\)
  2. tangent: \(\frac{x\sec\theta}{a} - \frac{y\tan\theta}{b} = 1 \Rightarrow \frac{\sqrt{2}x}{2} - \frac{y}{3} = 1\)
  3. multiply by 6: \(3\sqrt{2}x - 2y = 6\)

Answer: \(3\sqrt{2}x - 2y = 6\)

Example 2.

Find the tangents to \(\frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\) with slope \(m = 2\), if they exist.

  1. slope form: \(y = mx \pm \sqrt{a^2 m^2 - b^2}\)
  2. \(a^2 = 4,\ b^2 = 9,\ m = 2\): \(c^2 = 16 - 9 = 7\)
  3. \(y = 2x \pm \sqrt{7}\)

Answer: \(y = 2x \pm \sqrt{7}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the ellipse tangent with a plus sign between the two terms
  • using the ellipse tangency condition \(c^2 = a^2 m^2 + b^2\)
  • forgetting the reality condition \(a^2 m^2 \ge b^2\) for slope-form tangents

Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 6

Introduction to Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 5 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A hyperbola is the locus of a point whose distance from a fixed point (focus) divided by its distance from a fixed line (directrix) is a constant \(e > 1\); equivalently, it is the locus of points whose difference of distances from two fixed points (foci) is constant \(2a\).
Key formula(s). \[ |PF_1 - PF_2| = 2a,\qquad e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} > 1 \]
Key points.
  • the two branches of \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) open along \(\pm x\)
  • \(e > 1\) distinguishes the hyperbola from the ellipse \((e < 1)\) and the parabola \((e = 1)\)
  • the constant difference \(2a\) is exactly the transverse axis length
  • the curve is symmetric about both the x-axis and the y-axis
  • asymptotes: \(y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x\), approached but never touched
  • a plane meeting both nappes of a double cone cuts out a hyperbola
  • for the standard hyperbola, no real point has \(|x| < a\); the branches start at the vertices
Example 1.

The difference of a point's distances from the foci \((\pm 5, 0)\) is 8. Write the equation of the hyperbola.

  1. definition: \(|PF_1 - PF_2| = 2a = 8 \Rightarrow a = 4\)
  2. foci at \((\pm ae, 0)\): \(ae = 5\), so \(e = 5/4\)
  3. \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1) = 16(25/16 - 1) = 9\)

Answer: \(\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\)

Example 2.

For which real values of \(k\) does \(\frac{x^2}{k+3} - \frac{y^2}{k-2} = 1\) represent a hyperbola in standard position?

  1. we need \(a^2 = k + 3 > 0\) and \(b^2 = k - 2 > 0\)
  2. \(k > -3\) and \(k > 2\)
  3. the stronger condition dominates

Answer: \(k > 2\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing the focal definition with a plus sign (sum), which is the ellipse property
  • drawing the branches touching the asymptotes; they only approach them
  • taking \(e < 1\) for a hyperbola
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 7

Properties of Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 6 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The hyperbola has focal, angular and chord properties mirroring the ellipse: the difference of focal distances is constant \(2a\), and the tangent and normal at any point bisect the angles between the focal radii, so a ray aimed at one focus reflects toward the other. The two branches cluster around the asymptotes.
Key formula(s). \[ |PF_1 - PF_2| = 2a,\qquad \text{focal distances: } e x_1 - a \text{ and } e x_1 + a \text{ (right branch)} \]
Key points.
  • on the right branch the focal distances are \(e x_1 - a\) and \(e x_1 + a\), whose difference is \(2a\)
  • the tangent and the normal at a point bisect the external and internal angles between the focal radii
  • reflection property: light directed toward one focus reflects off the hyperbola toward the other focus
  • the tangents at the ends of a latus rectum meet on the directrix
  • the product of the slopes of the asymptotes is \(-1\) only for the rectangular hyperbola
  • the segment between the two vertices bounds the narrowest gap of the real curve
  • the hyperbola and its conjugate have the same asymptotes and hence the same centre
Example 1.

A point on the right branch of \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\) has abscissa \(x_1 = 6\). Find its focal distances.

  1. \(a = 3,\ b = 4,\ e = \sqrt{1 + 16/9} = 5/3\)
  2. focal distances are \(e x_1 - a\) and \(e x_1 + a\)
  3. \((5/3)(6) - 3 = 7\) and \((5/3)(6) + 3 = 13\)
  4. difference \(= 6 = 2a\), verifying the focal property

Answer: 7 and 13

Example 2.

Verify the focal property at the point \((6, 4\sqrt{3})\) of \(\frac{x^2}{36} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\).

  1. check the point lies on the hyperbola: \(\frac{36}{36} - \frac{48}{16} = 1 - 3 = -2 \ne 1\)
  2. so \((6, 4\sqrt{3})\) is not a point of this hyperbola; recheck membership first
  3. correct y for \(x = 6\): \(\frac{y^2}{16} = \frac{36}{36} - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow y = 0\), giving the vertex \((6, 0)\)
  4. at \((6, 0)\): distances to foci \((\pm 2\sqrt{13}, 0)\) differ by \(12 = 2a\)

Answer: vertex \((6, 0)\); difference \(12 = 2a\), so the property holds

Common mistakes.
  • applying the elliptic focal formula (sum \(= 2a\)) instead of the difference
  • misidentifying focal distances as \(a \pm e x_1\)
  • forgetting to check that a given point actually lies on the hyperbola before using its focal distances
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 8

Parameters Related to Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 7 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The standard hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is governed by the parameters: eccentricity \(e = \sqrt{1 + b^2/a^2}\), foci \((\pm ae, 0)\), directrices \(x = \pm a/e\) and latus rectum \(\frac{2b^2}{a}\), with vertices \((\pm a, 0)\) and asymptote slopes \(\pm b/a\).
Key formula(s). \[ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}},\quad \text{foci }(\pm ae, 0),\quad \text{directrices }x = \pm \frac{a}{e},\quad \text{latus rectum }= \frac{2b^2}{a} \]
Key points.
  • vertices \((\pm a, 0)\) lie on the curve; foci \((\pm ae, 0)\) lie outside it
  • the distance between the foci is \(2ae\); between the directrices it is \(2a/e\)
  • the ends of the latus rectum are \((ae, \pm b^2/a)\)
  • the parametric point \((a\sec\theta, b\tan\theta)\) covers the right branch for \(-\pi/2 < \theta < \pi/2\)
  • focal distances on the right branch are \(e x_1 \pm a\)
  • the eccentricity satisfies \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1)\)
Example 1.

Find \(e\), the foci and the directrices of \(\frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{12} = 1\).

  1. \(a^2 = 4,\ b^2 = 12,\ a = 2\)
  2. \(e = \sqrt{1 + 12/4} = \sqrt{4} = 2\)
  3. foci \((\pm 2 \cdot 2, 0) = (\pm 4, 0)\); directrices \(x = \pm 2/2 = \pm 1\)

Answer: \(e = 2\), foci \((\pm 4, 0)\), directrices \(x = \pm 1\)

Example 2.

Find the equation of the hyperbola with foci \((\pm 5, 0)\) and latus rectum \(32/3\).

  1. \(ae = 5\) and latus rectum \(2b^2/a = 32/3\)
  2. \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1) = a^2 e^2 - a^2 = 25 - a^2\), and also \(b^2 = 16a/3\)
  3. \(\frac{16a}{3} = 25 - a^2 \Rightarrow 3a^2 + 16a - 75 = 0 \Rightarrow a = 3\)
  4. \(b^2 = 25 - 9 = 16\)

Answer: \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\)

Common mistakes.
  • taking the foci as \((\pm a, 0)\); those are the vertices
  • using \(b^2 = a^2(1 - e^2)\) (the ellipse relation) instead of \(b^2 = a^2(e^2 - 1)\)
  • writing the latus rectum as \(b^2/a\) without the factor 2
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 9

Pole and Polar for a Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 8 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The polar of the point \((x_1, y_1)\) with respect to the hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) is obtained by the replacement rule \(x^2 \to x x_1\), \(y^2 \to y y_1\), giving \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} - \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\). If the point lies on the hyperbola, its polar is the tangent at that point.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{polar of }(x_1, y_1):\quad \frac{x x_1}{a^2} - \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1 \]
Key points.
  • for a point on the hyperbola, the polar reduces to the tangent
  • the pole of the line \(lx + my = 1\) is \((a^2 l, -b^2 m)\)
  • the polar of a focus \((\pm ae, 0)\) is the directrix \(x = \pm a/e\)
  • the polar of a point on an asymptote is parallel to that asymptote
  • if the point lies strictly between the branches, its polar does not meet the hyperbola in real points
  • the pole-polar duality: if one point lies on the polar of another, the second lies on the polar of the first
Example 1.

Find the polar of the point \((-3, 4)\) with respect to \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\).

  1. polar: \(\frac{x(-3)}{9} - \frac{y(4)}{16} = 1\)
  2. \(-\frac{x}{3} - \frac{y}{4} = 1\)
  3. multiply through by \(-12\)

Answer: \(4x + 3y = -12\)

Example 2.

Find the pole of the line \(2x - 3y = 1\) with respect to \(\frac{x^2}{4} - y^2 = 1\).

  1. here \(a^2 = 4,\ b^2 = 1\)
  2. the pole of \(lx + my = 1\) is \((a^2 l, -b^2 m)\)
  3. pole \(= (4 \cdot 2, -1 \cdot (-3)) = (8, 3)\)

Answer: pole \((8, 3)\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the ellipse polar with a plus sign
  • solving the pole problem from a line written as \(lx + my + n = 0\) without first normalising to \(lx + my = 1\)
  • expecting a real chord of contact when the point has no real tangents to the hyperbola

Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 10

Rectangular Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 9 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A rectangular hyperbola is a hyperbola whose asymptotes are perpendicular; the simplest form is \(xy = c^2\), whose asymptotes are the coordinate axes. Its parametric point is \((ct, c/t)\), its eccentricity is \(\sqrt{2}\), and its tangent at parameter \(t\) is \(x + t^2 y = 2ct\).
Key formula(s). \[ xy = c^2,\quad P(t) = \left(ct, \frac{c}{t}\right),\quad \text{tangent: } x + t^2 y = 2ct,\quad e = \sqrt{2} \]
Key points.
  • the eccentricity of every rectangular hyperbola is \(e = \sqrt{2}\)
  • the parametric point \((ct, c/t)\) satisfies \(xy = c^2\) identically
  • the tangent at \((ct, c/t)\) is \(x + t^2 y = 2ct\), of slope \(-1/t^2\)
  • the normal is \(t y = t^3 x - c(t^4 - 1)\), of slope \(t^2\)
  • the chord joining parameters \(t_1, t_2\) is \(x + t_1 t_2 y = c(t_1 + t_2)\)
  • the tangent cuts the axes at points whose product of intercepts is constant \(4c^2\), enclosing area \(2c^2\)
  • the standard-aligned rectangular hyperbola is \(x^2 - y^2 = a^2\), with asymptotes \(y = \pm x\)
Example 1.

Find the tangent to \(xy = 9\) at the point \((6, 3/2)\).

  1. \(c^2 = 9 \Rightarrow c = 3\); the point \((6, 3/2) = (3 \cdot 2, 3/2)\) corresponds to \(t = 2\)
  2. tangent: \(x + t^2 y = 2ct \Rightarrow x + 4y = 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 2\)
  3. \(x + 4y = 12\)

Answer: \(x + 4y = 12\)

Example 2.

Find the normal to the rectangular hyperbola \(xy = c^2\) at the point with \(t = 1\).

  1. the point is \((c, c)\)
  2. slope of tangent \(= -1/t^2 = -1\), so the normal's slope is \(1\)
  3. normal through \((c, c)\): \(y - c = 1(x - c)\)

Answer: \(y = x\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the tangent of \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) verbatim on \(xy = c^2\)
  • writing the normal slope as \(-t^2\) instead of \(t^2\)
  • forgetting that \(e = \sqrt{2}\) characterises the rectangular hyperbola only
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 21 · Topic 11

Chord Joining Two Points of Hyperbola

HyperbolaCoordinate GeometryTopic 10 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The chord joining the parametric points \((a\sec\alpha, b\tan\alpha)\) and \((a\sec\beta, b\tan\beta)\) of the hyperbola \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) has equation \(\frac{x}{a}\cos\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2} - \frac{y}{b}\sin\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2} = \cos\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}\). For the rectangular hyperbola \(xy = c^2\), the chord joining parameters \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) is \(x + t_1 t_2 y = c(t_1 + t_2)\).
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{x}{a}\cos\frac{\alpha - \beta}{2} - \frac{y}{b}\sin\frac{\alpha + \beta}{2} = \cos\frac{\alpha + \beta}{2};\qquad xy = c^2:\ x + t_1 t_2 y = c(t_1 + t_2) \]
Key points.
  • as \(\alpha \to \beta\), the chord becomes the tangent \(\frac{x\sec\alpha}{a} - \frac{y\tan\alpha}{b} = 1\)
  • the chord of contact of an external point \((x_1, y_1)\) is \(\frac{x x_1}{a^2} - \frac{y y_1}{b^2} = 1\)
  • the chord joining \(t_1, t_2\) on \(xy = c^2\) has slope \(-1/(t_1 t_2)\)
  • the chord with a given midpoint \((x_1, y_1)\) of the standard hyperbola satisfies \(T = S_1\)
  • a line \(y = mx + c\) cuts the hyperbola in a chord; substituting gives a quadratic whose roots give the two intersection points
  • for a chord to be real, the quadratic discriminant must be non-negative
Example 1.

Find the chord of \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\) joining the parametric points with \(\alpha = 0\) and \(\beta = \pi/3\).

  1. \((\alpha - \beta)/2 = -\pi/6\), \((\alpha + \beta)/2 = \pi/6\)
  2. chord: \(\frac{x}{3}\cos\left(-\frac{\pi}{6}\right) - \frac{y}{2}\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = \cos\frac{\pi}{6}\)
  3. \(\frac{\sqrt{3}x}{6} - \frac{y}{4} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\), multiply by 12

Answer: \(2\sqrt{3}x - 3y = 6\sqrt{3}\)

Example 2.

Find the chord of the rectangular hyperbola \(xy = 4\) joining the points with parameters \(t_1 = 1\) and \(t_2 = 2\).

  1. \(c^2 = 4 \Rightarrow c = 2\)
  2. chord: \(x + t_1 t_2 y = c(t_1 + t_2)\)
  3. \(x + 2y = 2(1 + 2) = 6\)

Answer: \(x + 2y = 6\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the ellipse chord formula (with a plus sign) on the hyperbola
  • swapping \(\alpha - \beta\) into the sine term and \(\alpha + \beta\) into the cosine term
  • on \(xy = c^2\), dropping the scale factor \(c\) from the chord constant

Chapter 22 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

A vector has magnitude and direction, for example A \( = (a_{1}, a_{2}, a_{3}) \) in space. Dot product \( A\cdot B = a_{1}b_{1} + a_{2}b_{2} + a_{3}b_{3} \) is a scalar. The cross product has magnitude a b sin \( \theta \) and points perpendicular to both vectors.

Chapter 22 · Topic 2

Test of Collinearity

Vector AlgebraVectorsTopic 1 of 11

Explanation

Concept. Three points \(A, B, C\) with position vectors \(\vec{a}, \vec{b}, \vec{c}\) are collinear iff the vectors \(\vec{AB} = \vec{b}-\vec{a}\) and \(\vec{AC} = \vec{c}-\vec{a}\) are parallel, i.e. one is a scalar multiple of the other.
Key formula(s). \[ \vec{b}-\vec{a} = \lambda(\vec{c}-\vec{a}) \iff \frac{x_2-x_1}{x_3-x_1} = \frac{y_2-y_1}{y_3-y_1} = \frac{z_2-z_1}{z_3-z_1} \]
Key points.
  • points \(A, B, C\) are collinear iff \(\vec{AB}\) and \(\vec{AC}\) are parallel
  • parallel-test: \(\vec{AB} = \lambda\vec{AC}\) for some real \(\lambda\)
  • classworker-free coordinate test: \((x_2-x_1,\ y_2-y_1,\ z_2-z_1) = \lambda(x_3-x_1,\ y_3-y_1,\ z_3-z_1)\)
  • the ratio test works only when all denominators are non-zero
  • two vectors are always collinear; collinearity of points involves three or more points
  • collinear points form a triangle of zero area
  • area of \(\triangle ABC = \frac{1}{2}|\vec{AB}\times\vec{AC}| = 0 \iff A, B, C\) collinear
  • a point on the line through \(\vec{a}, \vec{b}\) writes as \(\vec{c} = (1-t)\vec{a} + t\vec{b}\)
Example 1.

Show that the points \(A(2,3,4),\ B(4,3,6),\ C(6,3,8)\) are collinear.

  1. \(\vec{AB} = (2,0,2)\) and \(\vec{AC} = (4,0,4)\)
  2. clearly \(\vec{AC} = 2\vec{AB}\), so the two vectors are parallel
  3. both pass through the common point \(A\), so all three points lie on one line

Answer: \(A, B, C\) are collinear

Example 2.

For what value of \(k\) are the points \(A(1,2,3),\ B(4,5,6),\ C(7,8,k)\) collinear?

  1. \(\vec{AB} = (3,3,3)\) and \(\vec{AC} = (6,6,k-3)\)
  2. collinearity needs \(\vec{AC} = \lambda\vec{AB}\), so \(6 = 3\lambda\), giving \(\lambda = 2\)
  3. hence \(k-3 = 2 \times 3 = 6\)

Answer: \(k = 9\)

Common mistakes.
  • concluding collinearity because two vectors are parallel even when they do not share a common point
  • comparing only two coordinates and ignoring the third
  • reversing the order of ratios in the proportional condition
Back to Index
Index / Vectors
Chapter 22 · Topic 3

Vector Equations

Vector AlgebraVectorsTopic 2 of 11

Explanation

Concept. A vector equation is an equation whose unknown is a vector; it is solved by comparing coefficients of the basis vectors, or by dotting/crossing with convenient vectors to kill unwanted terms.
Key formula(s). \[ x\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = m\vec{a} + n\vec{b} \iff (x-m)\vec{a} + (y-n)\vec{b} = \vec{0} \Rightarrow x = m,\ y = n \ \text{if } \vec{a}, \vec{b} \text{ are independent} \]
Key points.
  • coefficients may be equated only when the vectors involved are linearly independent
  • the equation \(\alpha\vec{a} + \beta\vec{b} = \vec{0}\) with independent \(\vec{a}, \vec{b}\) forces \(\alpha = \beta = 0\)
  • dot both sides with the vector \(\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\) to eliminate both \(\vec{a}\) and \(\vec{b}\) terms at once
  • \(\vec{x}\times\vec{a} = \vec{b}\) has infinitely many solutions: \(\vec{x} = \frac{\vec{b}\times\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|^2} + t\vec{a}\)
  • \(\vec{x}\cdot\vec{a} = p\) is a plane; a single scalar equation never pins down \(\vec{x}\) uniquely
  • consistency check: \(\vec{b}\) must satisfy \(\vec{b}\cdot\vec{a} = 0\) in \(\vec{x}\times\vec{a} = \vec{b}\)
  • two scalar conditions (\(\vec{x}\cdot\vec{a},\ \vec{x}\cdot\vec{b}\) independent) determine \(\vec{x}\) in the plane
  • the triple-product identity \(\vec{a}\times(\vec{b}\times\vec{c}) = (\vec{a}\cdot\vec{c})\vec{b} - (\vec{a}\cdot\vec{b})\vec{c}\) is the main tool for \(\vec{x}\times\vec{a} = \vec{b}\)
Example 1.

Solve \(\vec{x}\) in \(x\hat{i} + (2x+y)\hat{j} + 3\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 3\hat{k}\).

  1. equate coefficients of \(\hat{i}\): \(x = 2\)
  2. equate coefficients of \(\hat{j}\): \(2x + y = -1\), so \(4 + y = -1\)
  3. the \(\hat{k}\) components agree: \(3 = 3\), so the system is consistent

Answer: \(\vec{x} = 2\hat{i} - 5\hat{j}\)

Example 2.

If \(\vec{x}\cdot\vec{a} = p\) and \(\vec{x}\times\vec{a} = \vec{b}\), express \(\vec{x}\) in terms of \(\vec{a}, \vec{b}, p\).

  1. cross the second equation on the left with \(\vec{a}\): \(\vec{a}\times(\vec{x}\times\vec{a}) = \vec{a}\times\vec{b}\)
  2. expand by the triple-product identity: \((\vec{a}\cdot\vec{a})\vec{x} - (\vec{a}\cdot\vec{x})\vec{a} = \vec{a}\times\vec{b}\)
  3. use \(\vec{x}\cdot\vec{a} = p\) and \(|\vec{a}|^2 = a^2\): \(a^2\vec{x} - p\vec{a} = \vec{a}\times\vec{b}\)
  4. rearrange: \(\vec{x} = \frac{p\vec{a} + \vec{a}\times\vec{b}}{a^2}\)

Answer: \(\vec{x} = \dfrac{p\vec{a} + \vec{a}\times\vec{b}}{|\vec{a}|^2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • equating coefficients of linearly dependent vectors and producing extra fake solutions
  • treating \(\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\) as \(\vec{b}\times\vec{a}\) while rearranging (missing a minus sign)
  • assuming a dot-product equation determines \(\vec{x}\) uniquely
  • sign slips in \(\vec{a}\times(\vec{b}\times\vec{c}) = (\vec{a}\cdot\vec{c})\vec{b} - (\vec{a}\cdot\vec{b})\vec{c}\)
Back to Index
Index / Vectors
Chapter 22 · Topic 4

Introduction to Vectors

Vector AlgebraVectorsTopic 3 of 11

Explanation

Concept. A vector is a quantity possessing both magnitude and direction, drawn as a directed segment \(\vec{AB}\) with initial point \(A\) and terminal point \(B\). The position vector of a point \(P\) is \(\vec{OP} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}\), locating \(P\) with respect to a fixed origin.
Key formula(s). \[ |\vec{a}| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}, \qquad \hat{a} = \frac{\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|}, \qquad \vec{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a} \]
Key points.
  • magnitude is never negative: \(|\vec{a}| \ge 0\), with \(|\vec{a}| = 0\) only for \(\vec{a} = \vec{0}\)
  • the unit vector in the direction of \(\vec{a}\) is \(\hat{a} = \vec{a}/|\vec{a}|\)
  • \(\vec{AB}\) is the position vector of \(B\) minus the position vector of \(A\)
  • a zero vector has magnitude \(0\) and no definite direction
  • two vectors are equal iff they have equal magnitude and the same direction
  • vectors are free: a parallel translation does not change a vector
  • \(\vec{a}\) and \(k\vec{a}\) are parallel for \(k > 0\), anti-parallel for \(k < 0\)
  • every vector in space: \(\vec{a} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}\) with \(|x|, |y|, |z| \le |\vec{a}|\)
Example 1.

Find the unit vector in the direction of \(\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}\).

  1. \(|\vec{a}| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9} = 3\)
  2. \(\hat{a} = \frac{\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|} = \frac{1}{3}(2\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k})\)

Answer: \(\hat{a} = \frac{2}{3}\hat{i} - \frac{1}{3}\hat{j} + \frac{2}{3}\hat{k}\)

Example 2.

The position vectors of \(A\) and \(B\) are \(3\hat{i}-\hat{j}+2\hat{k}\) and \(\hat{i}+2\hat{j}-\hat{k}\). Find \(\vec{AB}\) and its magnitude.

  1. \(\vec{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a} = (\hat{i}+2\hat{j}-\hat{k}) - (3\hat{i}-\hat{j}+2\hat{k})\)
  2. \(\vec{AB} = (1-3)\hat{i} + (2+1)\hat{j} + (-1-2)\hat{k} = -2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}\)
  3. \(|\vec{AB}| = \sqrt{4 + 9 + 9} = \sqrt{22}\)

Answer: \(\vec{AB} = -2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 3\hat{k},\quad |\vec{AB}| = \sqrt{22}\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(\vec{AB} = \vec{a} + \vec{b}\) instead of \(\vec{b} - \vec{a}\)
  • forgetting the square root when computing the magnitude
  • sign errors while subtracting components of the position vectors
  • writing \(\hat{a} = \vec{a}\,|\vec{a}|\) instead of \(\vec{a}/|\vec{a}|\)
