Chapter 25 · Topic 2

Definition

RelationsAlgebraTopic 1 of 2

Explanation

Concept. A relation from set \(A\) to set \(B\) is a subset of the cartesian product \(A\times B\); the domain is the set of first coordinates and the range the set of second coordinates of its ordered pairs.
Key formula(s). \[ R \subseteq A\times B,\qquad \text{domain}(R) = \{a : (a,b)\in R\},\qquad \text{range}(R) = \{b : (a,b)\in R\} \]
Key points.
  • \(A\times B\) has \(|A||B|\) ordered pairs
  • total number of relations from \(A\) to \(B\): \(2^{|A||B|}\)
  • total relations on a set \(A\): \(2^{|A|^2}\)
  • the empty set and \(A\times B\) itself are always relations
  • inverse relation: \(R^{-1} = \{(b,a) : (a,b)\in R\}\)
Example 1.

For \(A = \{1,2\}\) and \(B = \{a,b\}\), how many relations from \(A\) to \(B\) exist?

  1. \(A\times B\) contains \(2\times 2 = 4\) ordered pairs
  2. every subset of \(A\times B\) is a relation
  3. count \(= 2^4\)

Answer: 16 relations

Example 2.

If \(R = \{(x,y) : y = 2x,\ x \in A\}\) with \(A = \{1,2,3\}\), find \(R\), its domain and its range.

  1. \(R = \{(1,2),(2,4),(3,6)\}\)
  2. the first coordinates give the domain
  3. the second coordinates give the range

Answer: \(R = \{(1,2),(2,4),(3,6)\}\); domain \(\{1,2,3\}\), range \(\{2,4,6\}\)

Common mistakes.
  • swapping the roles of domain and range
  • forgetting that the empty set is a legitimate relation
  • counting \(|A||B|\) instead of \(2^{|A||B|}\) relations
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 25 · Topic 3

Types of Relation and Explanation of All

RelationsAlgebraTopic 2 of 2

Explanation

Concept. A relation is reflexive when \((a,a)\in R\) for every \(a\), symmetric when \((a,b)\in R\Rightarrow (b,a)\in R\), and transitive when \((a,b),(b,c)\in R\Rightarrow (a,c)\in R\); a relation holding all three is an equivalence relation whose classes partition the set.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{reflexive: }(a,a)\in R\ \forall a,\qquad \text{symmetric: }(a,b)\in R \Rightarrow (b,a)\in R,\qquad \text{transitive: }(a,b),(b,c)\in R \Rightarrow (a,c)\in R \]
Key points.
  • antisymmetric: \((a,b),(b,a)\in R \Rightarrow a = b\) (compatible only with \(a\le b\) orders)
  • equivalence classes form a partition into disjoint non-empty blocks
  • congruence modulo \(m\), \(a \equiv b \pmod m\), is the model equivalence relation
  • reflexive relations on an \(n\)-element set number \(2^{\,n^2 - n}\)
  • divisibility is reflexive and transitive but generally not symmetric
  • \(R\circ R\), transitivity uses each middle element exactly once
Example 1.

Prove that \(a\,R\,b \iff 3\mid(a-b)\) is an equivalence relation on the integers.

  1. reflexive: \(a-a = 0\) is divisible by 3
  2. symmetric: if \(3 \mid (a-b)\) then \(3\mid (b-a)\)
  3. transitive: \(3\mid (a-b)\) and \(3\mid (b-c)\) force \(3\mid (a-c)\)

Answer: hence an equivalence relation; classes \(\{3k\},\{3k+1\},\{3k+2\}\)

Example 2.

Is \(R = \{(1,1),(2,2),(1,2),(2,1)\}\) on \(\{1,2\}\) an equivalence relation?

  1. reflexivity: both \((1,1)\) and \((2,2)\) are present
  2. symmetry: \((1,2)\) and \((2,1)\) are both present
  3. transitivity: \((1,2),(2,1)\Rightarrow(1,1)\) and \((2,1),(1,2)\Rightarrow(2,2)\), both present

Answer: yes, an equivalence relation with the single class \(\{1,2\}\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting that transitivity also applies when one element repeats, e.g. \((a,b),(b,a)\Rightarrow(a,a)\)
  • calling a reflexive-plus-transitive relation an equivalence relation
  • confusing antisymmetric with "not symmetric"
Back to Index
Index / Algebra

Introduction & Instructions

Chapter 26 · Topic 2

Factorization Theorem

DeterminantsAlgebraTopic 1 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The factorization theorem finds linear factors of a determinant in a variable: substituting \(x = a\) that makes two rows identical (or a row vanish) shows \((x-a)\) is a factor, with degree dictating how the factors combine.
Key formula(s). \[ \Delta(a) = 0 \Rightarrow (x-a)\ \text{is a factor};\qquad \Delta = k\,(x-a_1)^{m_1}(x-a_2)^{m_2}\cdots \text{ with right degree} \]
Key points.
  • two rows (or columns) equal forces the determinant to vanish
  • the degree in \(x\) is at most the number of rows containing \(x\)
  • match the total degree with the product of found factors
  • after factorization fix the constant by the leading coefficient of one expansion
  • rows proportional or one row identically zero also give vanishing
Example 1.

Factorize \(\Delta = \begin{vmatrix}x&a&a\\a&x&a\\a&a&x\end{vmatrix}\).

  1. put \(x = a\): all three rows are identical, so \(x-a\) is a factor
  2. put \(x = -2a\): each row sum is zero, so the rows are dependent, giving factor \(x + 2a\)
  3. degree in \(x\) is at most 3, so \(\Delta = (x-a)^2(x+2a)\) up to a constant
  4. the coefficient of \(x^3\) is 1 in both, so no extra constant

Answer: \(\Delta = (x-a)^2(x+2a)\)

Example 2.

The determinant \(\begin{vmatrix}1&1&1\\a&b&c\\a^3&b^3&c^3\end{vmatrix}\) has which linear factors in the variables \(a,b,c\)?

  1. set \(a = b\): the second and third columns become identical, so \((a-b)\) is a factor
  2. similarly \((b-c)\) and \((c-a)\) are factors
  3. the product \((a-b)(b-c)(c-a)\) is skew-symmetric and has degree 3, leaving one factor of total degree 1
  4. testing the symmetric part gives the factor \(a+b+c\)

Answer: divisible by \((a-b)(b-c)(c-a)(a+b+c)\)

Common mistakes.
  • claiming a factor without verifying the substituted rows actually vanish
  • producing factors past the polynomial's degree in the variable
  • forgetting to fit the leading constant after writing the factor product

Chapter 26 · Topic 3

Multiplication of Two Determinants

DeterminantsAlgebraTopic 2 of 5

Explanation

Concept. Determinants of the same order multiply like matrices: \(|A||B| = |AB|\), where the entries of \(AB\) are row-by-column dot products, so products and sums of determinants collapse into one expansion.
Key formula(s). \[ |A||B| = |AB| = \left|\sum_{k} a_{ik}b_{kj}\right|,\qquad |AB| = |BA| = |A||B| \text{ for determinants of one order} \]
Key points.
  • entry \((i,j)\) of the product is the dot product of row \(i\) of the left factor with column \(j\) of the right factor
  • a factor written with transposed blocks is brought back by transposing before multiplying
  • \(|AB|=0\) iff at least one of \(|A|,|B|\) is zero
  • the rule also factorizes structured determinants into products
  • determinants of different orders cannot combine directly
Example 1.

Compute \(|A||B|\) for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\), \(B = \begin{pmatrix}5&6\\7&8\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(|A| = 1\cdot 4 - 2\cdot 3 = -2\)
  2. \(|B| = 5\cdot 8 - 6\cdot 7 = -2\)
  3. \(|A||B| = (-2)(-2)\)

Answer: 4

Example 2.

Verify \(|A||B| = |AB|\) for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&3\end{pmatrix}\), \(B = \begin{pmatrix}4&1\\2&1\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(|A| = 3\), \(|B| = 4 - 2 = 2\)
  2. \(AB = \begin{pmatrix}1\cdot4+2\cdot2&1\cdot1+2\cdot1\\0+3\cdot2&0+3\cdot1\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}8&3\\6&3\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. \(|AB| = 8\cdot 3 - 3\cdot 6 = 24 - 18 = 6\)
  4. \(3 \times 2 = 6\) confirms the identity

Answer: \(|AB| = 6 = |A||B|\)

Common mistakes.
  • multiplying determinants entry-wise instead of row-by-column
  • writing \(|A+B| = |A| + |B|\) (generally false)
  • forgetting to transpose a factor that was written as a transpose

Chapter 26 · Topic 4

Properties of Determinants

DeterminantsAlgebraTopic 3 of 5

Explanation

Concept. Determinant properties: transpose preserves value, a row swap changes the sign, adding a multiple of one row to another changes nothing, and any row of zeros (or two proportional rows) forces the value to zero.
Key formula(s). \[ |A^T| = |A|;\quad R_i \leftrightarrow R_j \Rightarrow \Delta \rightarrow -\Delta;\qquad R_i \rightarrow R_i + kR_j \text{ leaves } \Delta \text{ unchanged};\qquad \text{two equal rows} \Rightarrow \Delta = 0 \]
Key points.
  • operations that cost nothing: \(R_i \rightarrow R_i + kR_j\) and any common factor pulled out
  • a row of zeros, or two identical/proportional rows, kills the determinant
  • scaling one row by \(k\) multiplies the determinant by \(k\)
  • splitting a row with two terms splits the whole determinant into two
  • create zeros in a row first, then expand along it
Example 1.

Evaluate \(\begin{vmatrix}1&2&3\\4&5&6\\7&8&9\end{vmatrix}\) using properties.

  1. \(R_2 \rightarrow R_2 - R_1\) gives row 2 as \((3,3,3)\)
  2. \(R_3 \rightarrow R_3 - R_1\) gives row 3 as \((6,6,6)\)
  3. rows 2 and 3 are proportional, so the determinant is zero

Answer: 0

Example 2.

Show that \(\begin{vmatrix}1&1&1\\a&b&c\\a^2&b^2&c^2\end{vmatrix} = (b-a)(c-a)(c-b)\).

  1. subtract column 1 from columns 2 and 3
  2. factor \((b-a)\) from column 2 and \((c-a)\) from column 3
  3. the determinant becomes \((b-a)(c-a)\begin{vmatrix}1&0&0\\a&1&1\\a^2&b+a&c+a\end{vmatrix}\)
  4. expanding along row 1: \((b-a)(c-a)\big[(c+a)-(b+a)\big]\)

Answer: \((b-a)(c-a)(c-b)\)

Common mistakes.
  • thinking \(R_i \rightarrow kR_j + R_i\) carries a sign change (it does not)
  • scaling a row by \(k\) and forgetting to factor \(k\) out of the result
  • assuming two spaced identical values mean the determinant is unchanged rather than zero

Chapter 26 · Topic 5

Cramer's Rule

DeterminantsAlgebraTopic 4 of 5

Explanation

Concept. Cramer's rule solves \(AX = B\) for a square coefficient matrix with \(|A|\neq 0\) by writing \(x_i = \frac{\Delta_i}{\Delta}\), where \(\Delta_i\) replaces column \(i\) of \(A\) by the constants \(B\).
Key formula(s). \[ x_i = \frac{\Delta_i}{\Delta},\qquad \Delta = |A|,\qquad \Delta_i = |A\ \text{with column } i\text{ replaced by } B|; \quad \text{valid only if } \Delta \neq 0 \]
Key points.
  • a unique solution exists exactly when \(\Delta \neq 0\) (non-singular system)
  • make the sign pattern \(+,-,+,-\dots\) follow the cofactor expansion when building \(\Delta_i\)
  • if \(\Delta = 0\), use rank or proportionality to test consistency (none or infinitely many)
  • homogeneous systems \(AX=0\): \(\Delta \neq 0\) forces only the zero solution
  • Cramer's rule is most efficient for small \(n\) with simple numbers
Example 1.

Solve \(2x + 3y = 8,\ x - y = -1\) by Cramer's rule.

  1. \(\Delta = \begin{vmatrix}2&3\\1&-1\end{vmatrix} = -2 - 3 = -5\)
  2. \(\Delta_x = \begin{vmatrix}8&3\\-1&-1\end{vmatrix} = -8 + 3 = -5\), \(\Delta_y = \begin{vmatrix}2&8\\1&-1\end{vmatrix} = -2 - 8 = -10\)
  3. \(x = \frac{-5}{-5} = 1\), \(y = \frac{-10}{-5} = 2\)

Answer: \(x = 1,\ y = 2\)

Example 2.

Does \(x + y = 3,\ 2x + 2y = 6\) have a unique solution?

  1. \(\Delta = \begin{vmatrix}1&1\\2&2\end{vmatrix} = 0\)
  2. \(\Delta_x = \begin{vmatrix}3&1\\6&2\end{vmatrix} = 0,\ \Delta_y = \begin{vmatrix}1&3\\2&6\end{vmatrix} = 0\)
  3. with the equator equations proportional, the system is consistent and dependent
  4. so there are infinitely many solutions, \(y = 3 - x\)

Answer: no unique solution; infinitely many \((x,\ 3-x)\)

Common mistakes.
  • dividing by a zero determinant
  • substituting the constants into the wrong column when forming \(\Delta_i\)
  • declaring "no solution" whenever \(\Delta = 0\) without a consistency check

Chapter 26 · Topic 6

Cofactor and Minor of Determinants

DeterminantsAlgebraTopic 5 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The minor \(M_{ij}\) of entry \(a_{ij}\) is the determinant remaining after deleting row \(i\) and column \(j\); the cofactor \(C_{ij} = (-1)^{i+j}M_{ij}\). Expansion along row \(i\) writes \(\Delta = \sum_j a_{ij}C_{ij}\).
Key formula(s). \[ C_{ij} = (-1)^{i+j}M_{ij},\qquad \Delta = \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_{ij}C_{ij},\qquad \sum_{j} a_{ij}C_{kj} = \begin{cases}\Delta, & k = i\\ 0, & k \neq i\end{cases} \]
Key points.
  • the sign pattern is a chessboard: \(\begin{matrix}+&-&+\\-&+&-\\+&-&+\end{matrix}\)
  • expand along the row or column with the most zeros
  • cofactors assemble the adjoint matrix for the inverse computation
  • different rows' cofactors annihilate the expansion: \(\sum_j a_{ij}C_{kj} = 0\) for \(i\neq k\)
  • minors of a \(3\times 3\) are \(2\times 2\) determinants
Example 1.

For \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2&3\\4&5&6\\7&8&9\end{pmatrix}\) find the cofactor \(C_{23}\).

  1. \(M_{23} = \begin{vmatrix}1&2\\7&8\end{vmatrix} = 8 - 14 = -6\)
  2. \(C_{23} = (-1)^{2+3}M_{23}\)
  3. \(= -(-6) = 6\)

Answer: \(C_{23} = 6\)

Example 2.

Expand \(\Delta = \begin{vmatrix}1&0&2\\3&4&5\\0&6&7\end{vmatrix}\) along the first row.

  1. \(\Delta = 1\cdot\begin{vmatrix}4&5\\6&7\end{vmatrix} - 0\cdot\begin{vmatrix}3&5\\0&7\end{vmatrix} + 2\cdot\begin{vmatrix}3&4\\0&6\end{vmatrix}\)
  2. \(= (28-30) + 2(18-0)\)
  3. \(= -2 + 36\)

Answer: 34

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting the \((-1)^{i+j}\) factor in the cofactor sign
  • using the original entry in place of the minor determinant during the expansion
  • confusing \(M_{ij}\) with \(C_{ij}\) when assembling the adjoint

Chapter 27 · Topic 2

Matrix Multiplication

MatricesAlgebraTopic 1 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The product \(AB\) of two matrices is defined when the columns of \(A\) equal the rows of \(B\); entry \((i,j)\) of \(AB\) is the dot product of row \(i\) of \(A\) with column \(j\) of \(B\). Multiplication is associative and distributive but not commutative.
Key formula(s). \[ (AB)_{ij} = \sum_{k=1}^{m} a_{ik}b_{kj},\qquad A_{m\times n}\,B_{n\times p} = (AB)_{m\times p} \]
Key points.
  • \(AB \neq BA\) in general; the order of factors cannot be switched
  • \((AB)C = A(BC)\) and \(A(B+C) = AB + AC\)
  • \(AB = AC\) does not imply \(B = C\)
  • \(AB = 0\) is possible with neither \(A\) nor \(B\) being the zero matrix
  • identity matrix: \(AI = IA = A\)
  • scalar rule: \((kA)(lB) = kl\,AB\)
Example 1.

For \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\), \(B = \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\) compute \(AB\).

  1. \((AB)_{11} = 1\cdot0 + 2\cdot1 = 2\), \((AB)_{12} = 1\cdot1 + 2\cdot0 = 1\)
  2. \((AB)_{21} = 3\cdot0 + 4\cdot1 = 4\), \((AB)_{22} = 3\cdot1 + 4\cdot0 = 3\)
  3. so \(AB = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\4&3\end{pmatrix}\), while \(BA = \begin{pmatrix}3&4\\1&2\end{pmatrix}\)
  4. hence \(AB \neq BA\)

Answer: \(AB = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\4&3\end{pmatrix},\ BA = \begin{pmatrix}3&4\\1&2\end{pmatrix},\ AB\neq BA\)

Example 2.

Confirm that multiplying by the identity is harmless for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(AI = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. \((AI)_{11} = 1\cdot1 + 2\cdot0 = 1\), and similarly every column picks out the matching column of \(A\)
  3. hence \(AI = A\)

Answer: \(AI = A\), as required

Common mistakes.
  • multiplying matrices whose inner dimensions do not match
  • assuming commutativity \(AB = BA\)
  • dividing/cancelling a matrix from \(AB = AC\)

Chapter 27 · Topic 3

Transpose of Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 2 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The transpose \(A^T\) of matrix \(A\) interchanges rows and columns, \((A^T)_{ij} = A_{ji}\). Transposing reverses the order of products, preserves sums and determinant, and is its own inverse operation.
Key formula(s). \[ (A^T)_{ij} = A_{ji},\qquad (AB)^T = B^TA^T,\qquad (A^T)^T = A,\qquad |A^T| = |A| \]
Key points.
  • the transpose of a row vector is a column vector
  • \((ABC)^T = C^TB^TA^T\) — every factor is reversed
  • \((kA)^T = kA^T\) and \((A+B)^T = A^T + B^T\)
  • a square matrix equal to its transpose is symmetric; equal to its negative transpose is skew-symmetric
  • \((A^T)^{-1} = (A^{-1})^T\) whenever \(A\) is invertible
Example 1.

If \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&1&0\\3&-1&4\end{pmatrix}\), find \(A^T\).

  1. swap rows and columns so the first column of \(A^T\) is row 1 of \(A\)
  2. \((A^T)_{ij} = A_{ji}\) gives a \(3\times 2\) matrix
  3. \(A^T = \begin{pmatrix}2&3\\1&-1\\0&4\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(A^T = \begin{pmatrix}2&3\\1&-1\\0&4\end{pmatrix}\)

Example 2.

Verify \((AB)^T = B^TA^T\) for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&3\end{pmatrix}\), \(B = \begin{pmatrix}4&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(AB = \begin{pmatrix}4+2&1+0\\0+3&0+0\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}6&1\\3&0\end{pmatrix}\), so \((AB)^T = \begin{pmatrix}6&3\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. \(B^TA^T = \begin{pmatrix}4&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\2&3\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}4+2&0+3\\1+0&0+0\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. \(= \begin{pmatrix}6&3\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \((AB)^T = B^TA^T\), both \(\begin{pmatrix}6&3\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \((AB)^T = A^TB^T\) with factors in the original order
  • transposing a sum of matrices into a scrambled order
  • using the transpose rule on non-conforming dimensions

Chapter 27 · Topic 4

Characteristic Equation

MatricesAlgebraTopic 3 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The characteristic equation of a square matrix \(A\) is \(|A - \lambda I| = 0\), whose roots are the eigenvalues; the Cayley–Hamilton theorem says \(A\) itself satisfies this equation, e.g. for a \(2\times 2\) matrix \(A^2 - (\text{tr }A)A + |A|I = 0\).
Key formula(s). \[ |A - \lambda I| = 0\quad (\text{or } |\lambda I - A| = 0);\qquad A^2 - (\text{tr }A)A + |A|\,I = 0\quad (2\times 2) \]
Key points.
  • the sum of the eigenvalues is the trace, the product is the determinant
  • Cayley–Hamilton reduces high powers of \(A\) to lower powers
  • eigenvalues of a diagonal matrix are its diagonal entries
  • eigenvalues are invariant under similarity \(P^{-1}AP\)
  • a matrix that satisfies its equation can be inverted via the polynomial when \(|A|\neq 0\)
Example 1.

Find the eigenvalues of \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(|A - \lambda I| = \begin{vmatrix}2-\lambda&1\\1&2-\lambda\end{vmatrix} = 0\)
  2. \((2-\lambda)^2 - 1 = 0\)
  3. \(2-\lambda = \pm 1\)

Answer: eigenvalues \(1\) and \(3\)

Example 2.

Verify Cayley–Hamilton for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\) and compute \(A^2\).

  1. \(\text{tr }A = 5\), \(|A| = 4 - 6 = -2\), so the characteristic polynomial is \(\lambda^2 - 5\lambda - 2\)
  2. Cayley–Hamilton: \(A^2 - 5A - 2I = 0\), hence \(A^2 = 5A + 2I\)
  3. \(A^2 = 5\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix} + 2\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(A^2 = \begin{pmatrix}7&10\\15&22\end{pmatrix}\)

Common mistakes.
  • applying the \(2\times 2\) trace/determinant form verbatim to bigger matrices
  • confusing the scalar roots of \(|A - \lambda I| = 0\) with the matrix \(A\) in Cayley–Hamilton
  • flipping the sign convention \((\lambda I - A)\) inconsistently

Chapter 27 · Topic 5

Symmetric and Skew Symmetric Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 4 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A square matrix is symmetric if \(A^T = A\) and skew-symmetric if \(A^T = -A\); every square matrix splits uniquely into a symmetric part plus a skew-symmetric part.
Key formula(s). \[ A^T = A\ (\text{symmetric}),\qquad A^T = -A\ (\text{skew-symmetric}),\qquad A = \frac{A + A^T}{2} + \frac{A - A^T}{2} \]
Key points.
  • the diagonal of a skew-symmetric matrix is all zeros
  • a skew-symmetric matrix of odd order has determinant zero
  • \(A^TA\) is always symmetric; \(A - A^T\) is always skew-symmetric
  • the symmetric/skew-symmetric decomposition is the honest split of any square matrix
  • \(AB = (AB)^T\) detects symmetric products when \(A,B\) commute... use only when both symmetric
Example 1.

Express \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&5\\1&3\end{pmatrix}\) as a sum of its symmetric and skew-symmetric parts.

  1. \(A^T = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\5&3\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. symmetric part \(\frac{A+A^T}{2} = \begin{pmatrix}2&3\\3&3\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. skew part \(\frac{A-A^T}{2} = \begin{pmatrix}0&2\\-2&0\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&3\\3&3\end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix}0&2\\-2&0\end{pmatrix}\)

Example 2.

Prove that \(A - A^T\) is skew-symmetric for any square matrix \(A\).

  1. \((A - A^T)^T = A^T - (A^T)^T\)
  2. \(= A^T - A = -(A - A^T)\)
  3. by definition \(M^T = -M\) makes \(M = A - A^T\) skew-symmetric

Answer: \((A - A^T)^T = -(A - A^T)\), so \(A - A^T\) is skew-symmetric

Common mistakes.
  • believing a skew-symmetric matrix can carry non-zero diagonal entries
  • assuming the product of two symmetric matrices is always symmetric
  • dropping the \(\frac12\) factor in the symmetric/skew decomposition

Chapter 27 · Topic 6

Adjoint of a Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 5 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The adjoint (adjugate) of a square matrix is the transpose of its cofactor matrix, \(\operatorname{adj}A = [C_{ij}]^T\), and satisfies \(A\,\operatorname{adj}A = \operatorname{adj}A \cdot A = |A|I\), linking it directly to the inverse.
Key formula(s). \[ \operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}C_{11}&C_{21}&\cdots\\C_{12}&C_{22}&\cdots\\\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{pmatrix},\qquad A\,\operatorname{adj}A = \operatorname{adj}A\cdot A = |A|\,I \]
Key points.
  • for a \(2\times 2\) matrix swap the diagonal entries and negate the off-diagonal entries
  • build adjoints of larger matrices by computing every cofactor then transposing
  • \(|\operatorname{adj}A| = |A|^{n-1}\) for an \(n\times n\) matrix
  • \(\operatorname{adj}(AB) = \operatorname{adj}B\,\operatorname{adj}A\) for invertible \(A,B\) (order reversal)
  • \(\operatorname{adj}(\operatorname{adj}A) = |A|^{n-2}A\) for \(n \ge 2\)
Example 1.

Find the adjoint of \(A = \begin{pmatrix}4&6\\3&2\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. cofactors: \(C_{11} = 2,\ C_{12} = -3,\ C_{21} = -6,\ C_{22} = 4\)
  2. take the transpose of the cofactor matrix
  3. \(\operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}2&-6\\-3&4\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(\operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}2&-6\\-3&4\end{pmatrix}\)

Example 2.

Verify \(A\,\operatorname{adj}A = |A|I\) for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(|A| = 4 - 6 = -2\), and \(\operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}4&-2\\-3&1\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. \(A\,\operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}4&-2\\-3&1\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}4-6&-2+2\\12-12&-6+4\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. \(= \begin{pmatrix}-2&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix} = -2I\)

Answer: \(A\,\operatorname{adj}A = |A|I\), verified

Common mistakes.
  • omitting the transpose when forming the adjoint from cofactors
  • writing \(\operatorname{adj}(AB) = \operatorname{adj}A\,\operatorname{adj}B\) instead of the reversed order
  • using the \(2\times 2\) swap-and-negate shortcut on matrices of order above 2

Chapter 27 · Topic 7

System of Equations

MatricesAlgebraTopic 6 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A system \(AX = B\) of \(n\) equations in \(n\) unknowns has a unique solution exactly when \(|A|\neq 0\); if \(|A| = 0\) the system is consistent (infinitely many solutions) precisely when the ranks of \(A\) and its augmented matrix agree, otherwise it has none.
Key formula(s). \[ |A| \neq 0 \Rightarrow \text{unique solution};\qquad |A| = 0 \Rightarrow \text{consistent iff } \operatorname{rank}A = \operatorname{rank}[A\,|\,B] \]
Key points.
  • homogeneous \(AX = 0\) always has the zero solution; non-trivial solutions exist iff \(|A| = 0\)
  • consistent distinct parallel lines give no solution; coincident lines give infinitely many
  • inconsistent 2-variable systems satisfy \(\Delta = 0\) but \(\Delta_x\) or \(\Delta_y \neq 0\)
  • augment the coefficient matrix and compare ranks for arbitrary systems
  • a singular system can never boast a unique solution, whatever the constants
Example 1.

For what value of \(k\) does \(x - 2y = 1,\ 3x - 6y = 3k\) have infinitely many solutions?

  1. the second equation is 3 times the first when the constants match: \(3k = 3\)
  2. then both equations represent the same line
  3. \(k = 1\)

Answer: \(k = 1\)

Example 2.

Solve \(x + y + z = 6,\ x - y + z = 2,\ 2x - 3z = -1\).

  1. subtracting the first two equations: \(2y = 4 \Rightarrow y = 2\)
  2. hence \(x + z = 4\), combined with \(2x - 3z = -1\)
  3. substituting \(x = 4 - z\): \(8 - 2z - 3z = -1 \Rightarrow z = \frac95\)
  4. then \(x = 4 - \frac95 = \frac{11}{5}\)

Answer: \(x = \frac{11}{5},\ y = 2,\ z = \frac95\) (unique since \(|A| = 10 \neq 0\))

Common mistakes.
  • announcing "no solution" for a singular system without testing consistency
  • forgetting that a homogeneous system always admits the trivial solution
  • comparing ranks of the wrong submatrices of the augmented array

Chapter 27 · Topic 8

Definition and Algebra of Matrices

MatricesAlgebraTopic 7 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A matrix is a rectangular array of numbers with \(m\) rows and \(n\) columns; matrices of one order add entrywise, scalar multiplication scales every entry, and two matrices are equal only when their orders and all corresponding entries agree.
Key formula(s). \[ (A+B)_{ij} = a_{ij} + b_{ij},\qquad (kA)_{ij} = k\,a_{ij},\qquad A = B \iff a_{ij} = b_{ij}\ \forall\ i,j \]
Key points.
  • the zero matrix is the additive identity for matrix addition
  • addition is commutative and associative componentwise
  • \(-A\) means every entry negated, giving \(A + (-A) = 0\)
  • only matrices of matching orders may be added
  • \(k(A+B) = kA + kB\) for every scalar \(k\)
Example 1.

If \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\0&3\end{pmatrix}\) and \(B = \begin{pmatrix}4&-1\\2&2\end{pmatrix}\), find \(2A - B\).

  1. \(2A = \begin{pmatrix}4&2\\0&6\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. subtract entrywise: \(2A - B = \begin{pmatrix}4-4&2-(-1)\\0-2&6-2\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. simplify each component

Answer: \(\begin{pmatrix}0&3\\-2&4\end{pmatrix}\)

Example 2.

Solve for \(x, y\): \(\begin{pmatrix}x+y&1\\3&x-y\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}5&1\\3&1\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. equality forces \(x + y = 5\) and \(x - y = 1\) from the corner entries
  2. adding the two equations gives \(2x = 6\)
  3. subtracting them gives \(2y = 4\)

Answer: \(x = 3,\ y = 2\)

Common mistakes.
  • adding matrices of different orders
  • declaring matrix equality after checking only some entries
  • inferring \(B = C\) from \(AB = AC\) (matrices are not cancellations)
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 27 · Topic 9

Inverse of Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 8 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A square matrix with \(|A|\neq 0\) is invertible (non-singular) with inverse \(A^{-1} = \frac{\operatorname{adj}A}{|A|}\), satisfying \(AA^{-1} = A^{-1}A = I\); the \(2\times 2\) formula swaps the diagonal, negates the other diagonal and divides by the determinant.
Key formula(s). \[ A^{-1} = \frac{\operatorname{adj}A}{|A|}\quad (|A|\neq 0),\qquad \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}^{-1} = \frac{1}{ad-bc}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix} \]
Key points.
  • \(A\) is invertible iff \(|A|\neq 0\) (non-singular)
  • inverse of a product reverses order: \((AB)^{-1} = B^{-1}A^{-1}\)
  • \((A^{-1})^{-1} = A\), \((A^T)^{-1} = (A^{-1})^T\), \((kA)^{-1} = \frac1k A^{-1}\)
  • \(|A^{-1}| = \frac{1}{|A|}\)
  • row reduction \([\ A\ |\ I\ ] \rightarrow [\ I\ |\ A^{-1}\ ]\) computes it without cofactors
Example 1.

Find \(A^{-1}\) for \(A = \begin{pmatrix}2&3\\1&4\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(|A| = 8 - 3 = 5 \neq 0\), so \(A^{-1}\) exists
  2. \(\operatorname{adj}A = \begin{pmatrix}4&-3\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. \(A^{-1} = \frac15\begin{pmatrix}4&-3\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(A^{-1} = \tfrac15\begin{pmatrix}4&-3\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}\)

Example 2.

Show that \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&4\end{pmatrix}\) possesses no inverse.

  1. \(|A| = 1\cdot 4 - 2\cdot 2\)
  2. \(= 4 - 4 = 0\)
  3. since the determinant is zero the matrix is singular

Answer: \(A\) has no inverse

Common mistakes.
  • dividing by a zero determinant
  • assembling the inverse from a wrongly transposed adjoint
  • writing \((AB)^{-1} = A^{-1}B^{-1}\) instead of the reversed order
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 27 · Topic 10

Special Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 9 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Special matrices are named by algebraic conditions: idempotent \((A^2 = A)\), involutory \((A^2 = I)\), nilpotent \((A^k = 0)\), periodic \((A^{k+1} = A)\), together with the structural families diagonal, scalar, triangular and null matrices.
Key formula(s). \[ A^2 = A\ (\text{idempotent}),\qquad A^2 = I\ (\text{involutory}),\qquad A^k = O\ (\text{nilpotent}),\qquad A^{k+1} = A\ (\text{periodic}) \]
Key points.
  • a diagonal matrix has only its main diagonal possibly non-zero
  • the identity \(I\) is a scalar matrix with all diagonal entries 1
  • a scalar matrix \(kI\) commutes with every matrix
  • an upper triangular matrix has zeros below the main diagonal
  • idempotent matrices have eigenvalues only 0 or 1
  • an involutory matrix is its own inverse
Example 1.

Classify \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\) and \(B = \begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix}\).

  1. \(A\) is the identity matrix \(I\)
  2. \(B = 2I\) is a scalar matrix
  3. both are also diagonal matrices

Answer: \(A\) identity; \(B\) scalar matrix \(2I\)

Example 2.

Check that \(A = \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\) is idempotent.

  1. \(A^2 = \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. \(= \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. hence \(A^2 = A\)

Answer: \(A\) is idempotent

Common mistakes.
  • mixing up idempotent \((A^2 = A)\) with involutory \((A^2 = I)\)
  • assuming every triangular matrix is diagonal
  • treating the zero matrix and a nilpotent matrix as unrelated when \(A^k = O\) actually needs testing
Back to Index
Index / Algebra
Chapter 27 · Topic 11

Orthogonal Matrix

MatricesAlgebraTopic 10 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A square matrix \(A\) is orthogonal when \(A^TA = AA^T = I\), equivalently \(A^{-1} = A^T\); its rows and columns form orthonormal systems and its determinant is \(\pm 1\).
Key formula(s). \[ A^TA = AA^T = I,\qquad A^{-1} = A^T,\qquad |A| = \pm 1 \]
Key points.
  • orthogonality means the rows are unit vectors with pairwise zero dot products; likewise the columns
  • the product of two orthogonal matrices is orthogonal
  • \(|A| = \pm 1\) is a necessary (not sufficient) condition
  • orthogonal matrices preserve dot products, hence lengths of vectors
  • the inverse of an orthogonal matrix is simply its transpose
Example 1.

Show that \(A = \begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\) is orthogonal.

  1. \(A^T = \begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\)
  2. \(A^TA = \begin{pmatrix}\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta&-\cos\theta\sin\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta\\-\sin\theta\cos\theta+\cos\theta\sin\theta&\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta\end{pmatrix}\)
  3. \(= \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\)

Answer: \(A^TA = I\), so \(A\) is orthogonal with \(|A| = 1\)

Example 2.

Verify that \(A = \begin{pmatrix}\frac35&\frac45\\\frac45&-\frac35\end{pmatrix}\) satisfies \(A^{-1} = A^T\).

  1. \(|A| = -\frac{9}{25} - \frac{16}{25} = -1 \neq 0\), so the inverse exists
  2. \(A^TA = \begin{pmatrix}\frac{9+16}{25}&\frac{12-12}{25}\\\frac{12-12}{25}&\frac{16+9}{25}\end{pmatrix} = I\)
  3. therefore \(A^{-1} = A^T\)

Answer: \(A^TA = I\), hence \(A^{-1} = A^T\)

Common mistakes.
  • treating \(|A| = \pm 1\) as sufficient for orthogonality
  • checking only \(A^TA\) without the columns forming unit vectors when sizes permit
  • assuming the transpose of an orthogonal matrix stops being orthogonal
Back to Index

Chapter 28 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

Probability measures how likely an event \( is \) P(Event) \( = \) Favourable outcomes \( ÷ \) Total outcomes. It always lies between \( 0 \) and \( 1 \) If two events cannot happen together they are mutually exclusive and P(A or B) \( = \) P(A) \( + \) P(B).

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

Chapter 28 · Topic 2

Addition Theorem of Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 1 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The addition theorem states that for any two events \(A\) and \(B\), the probability of their union is \(P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)\), subtracting the doubly-counted overlap. For mutually exclusive events, \(P(A \cap B) = 0\) and the rule simplifies to \(P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B)\).
Key formula(s). \[ P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B), \qquad P(A \cup B \cup C) = P(A) + P(B) + P(C) - P(A \cap B) - P(B \cap C) - P(C \cap A) + P(A \cap B \cap C) \]
Key points.
  • mutually exclusive events: \(P(A \cap B) = 0\), so \(P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B)\)
  • exhaustive events satisfy \(P(A \cup B) = 1\)
  • complement rule: \(P(A^c) = 1 - P(A)\); use it when the complement is easier
  • inclusion-exclusion extends to three events by adding back \(P(A \cap B \cap C)\)
  • \(P(A \cup B) \le P(A) + P(B)\) is always true
  • if \(A \subseteq B\) then \(P(A) \le P(B)\)
  • the words "either ... or ..." usually signal a union \(A \cup B\)
Example 1.

A card is drawn from a well-shuffled pack of 52 cards. Find the probability that it is a king or a red card.

  1. Let \(A\) = event of a king and \(B\) = event of a red card.
  2. \(P(A) = \frac{4}{52}\), \(P(B) = \frac{26}{52}\), \(P(A \cap B) = P(\text{red king}) = \frac{2}{52}\)
  3. \(P(A \cup B) = \frac{4}{52} + \frac{26}{52} - \frac{2}{52} = \frac{28}{52}\)
  4. \(P(A \cup B) = \frac{7}{13}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{7}{13}\)

Example 2.

In a class, 40% passed Physics, 30% passed Chemistry and 15% passed both. Find the probability that a randomly chosen student passed in at least one subject.

  1. \(P(P) = 0.40\), \(P(C) = 0.30\), \(P(P \cap C) = 0.15\)
  2. \(P(P \cup C) = P(P) + P(C) - P(P \cap C)\)
  3. \(P(P \cup C) = 0.40 + 0.30 - 0.15 = 0.55\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{11}{20}\)

Common mistakes.
  • adding \(P(A) + P(B)\) without subtracting \(P(A \cap B)\) for overlapping events
  • treating overlapping events as if they were mutually exclusive
  • replacing \(P(A^c)\) by \(1 + P(A)\); the correct relation is \(1 - P(A)\)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 3

Conditional Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 2 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The conditional probability of \(A\) given \(B\), written \(P(A|B)\), is the probability that \(A\) occurs knowing that \(B\) has occurred, defined by \(P(A|B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}\) for \(P(B) > 0\). Conditionally, the sample space is shrunk to the set \(B\).
Key formula(s). \[ P(A | B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}, \qquad P(B | A) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)} \]
Key points.
  • defined only when the conditioning event has positive probability \(P(B) > 0\)
  • \(0 \le P(A|B) \le 1\)
  • rearranged multiplication form: \(P(A \cap B) = P(A|B)P(B)\)
  • \(P(A|B) + P(A^c|B) = 1\)
  • if \(A\) and \(B\) are independent then \(P(A|B) = P(A)\)
  • computed on the reduced sample space \(B\), counting outcomes in \(A \cap B\) out of \(B\)
  • "given that", "if it is known that" are the standard trigger phrases
Example 1.

A fair die is rolled. Given that the result is even, find the probability that it is a prime number.

  1. \(B = \{2, 4, 6\}\) so \(P(B) = \frac{3}{6}\); primes are \(\{2, 3, 5\}\)
  2. \(A \cap B = \{2\}\), so \(P(A \cap B) = \frac{1}{6}\)
  3. \(P(A|B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} = \frac{1/6}{3/6}\)
  4. \(P(A|B) = \frac{1}{3}\)

Answer: \(P(A|B) = \frac{1}{3}\)

Example 2.

The probability that a student passes Maths is \(\frac{2}{3}\), and the probability of passing both Maths and Physics is \(\frac{1}{2}\). Given that he passed Maths, find the probability that he passed Physics.

  1. \(P(M) = \frac{2}{3}\), \(P(M \cap P) = \frac{1}{2}\)
  2. \(P(P|M) = \frac{P(P \cap M)}{P(M)} = \frac{1/2}{2/3}\)
  3. \(P(P|M) = \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{3}{2} = \frac{3}{4}\)

Answer: \(P(P|M) = \frac{3}{4}\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(P(A|B)\) where the question actually asks for \(P(B|A)\)
  • dividing by zero, i.e. conditioning on an event of probability zero
  • confusing \(P(A \cap B)\) (both occur) with \(P(A|B)\) (B is known to occur)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 4

Probability Distributions

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 3 of 10

Explanation

Concept. A probability distribution of a discrete random variable \(X\) lists every value \(x_i\) with its probability \(p_i = P(X = x_i)\), the probability mass function, where each \(p_i \ge 0\) and \(\sum p_i = 1\). Its mean (expectation) and variance summarise the location and spread of \(X\).
Key formula(s). \[ E(X) = \sum_{i} x_i p_i, \qquad \text{Var}(X) = \sum_{i} (x_i - E(X))^2 p_i = \sum_{i} x_i^2 p_i - (E(X))^2 \]
Key points.
  • validity requires \(p_i \ge 0\) and \(\sum_{i} p_i = 1\)
  • \(E(X) = \sum x_i p_i\) is the probability-weighted average of the values
  • variance shortcut: \(\text{Var}(X) = \sum x_i^2 p_i - (E(X))^2\)
  • standard deviation is \(\sqrt{\text{Var}(X)}\)
  • \(E(aX + b) = aE(X) + b\) and \(\text{Var}(aX + b) = a^2\text{Var}(X)\)
  • \(P(X \le x)\) is the sum of all \(p_i\) with \(x_i \le x\)
  • binomial and Poisson mass functions are the standard discrete models
Example 1.

A random variable \(X\) takes the values \(1, 2, 3\) with probabilities \(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{6}\). Find \(E(X)\).

  1. validity check: \(\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{6} = 1\)
  2. \(E(X) = 1 \times \frac{1}{2} + 2 \times \frac{1}{3} + 3 \times \frac{1}{6}\)
  3. \(E(X) = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2}{3} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{5}{3}\)

Answer: \(E(X) = \frac{5}{3}\)

Example 2.

\(X\) counts the number of heads when two fair coins are tossed. Find \(E(X)\) and \(\text{Var}(X)\).

  1. \(P(X = 0) = \frac{1}{4}\), \(P(X = 1) = \frac{1}{2}\), \(P(X = 2) = \frac{1}{4}\)
  2. \(E(X) = 0 \times \frac{1}{4} + 1 \times \frac{1}{2} + 2 \times \frac{1}{4} = 1\)
  3. \(E(X^2) = 0 \times \frac{1}{4} + 1 \times \frac{1}{2} + 4 \times \frac{1}{4} = \frac{3}{2}\)
  4. \(\text{Var}(X) = \frac{3}{2} - 1^2 = \frac{1}{2}\)

Answer: \(E(X) = 1\), \(\text{Var}(X) = \frac{1}{2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting to verify that the probabilities sum to 1
  • assuming \(E(X^2) = (E(X))^2\), which holds only when the variance is zero
  • mixing up \(E(X^2)\) and \((E(X))^2\) when using the variance shortcut
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 5

Geometrical Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 4 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Geometrical probability applies when the sample space is a continuum, so probabilities become ratios of geometric measures: lengths, areas or volumes. The event and the sample space are two regions, and \(P\) equals favourable measure divided by total measure.
Key formula(s). \[ P(A) = \frac{\text{measure of the favourable region}}{\text{measure of the sample space region}} \]
Key points.
  • "measure" means length, area or volume according to the dimension
  • the total region must have finite measure for the ratio to make sense
  • curves and single lines have area zero, giving probability zero in planar problems
  • for two random numbers in \([0,1]\), plot conditions as regions in the unit square
  • convert meeting-point and inequality problems into area computations
  • uniform distribution inside a circle or square is the standard setup
Example 1.

A point is chosen at random inside a circle of radius \(R\). Find the probability that its distance from the centre is less than \(R/2\).

  1. total area \(= \pi R^2\); favourable area \(= \pi \left( \frac{R}{2} \right)^2 = \frac{\pi R^2}{4}\)
  2. \(P = \frac{\pi R^2 / 4}{\pi R^2}\)
  3. \(P = \frac{1}{4}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{1}{4}\)

Example 2.

Two numbers \(x\) and \(y\) are chosen independently at random from \([0, 1]\). Find the probability that \(x + y \le 1\).

  1. the sample space is the unit square of area \(1\)
  2. the condition \(x + y \le 1\) is the triangle bounded by \(y = 1 - x\) and the axes, of area \(\frac{1}{2}\)
  3. \(P = \frac{1/2}{1} = \frac{1}{2}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{1}{2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • counting discrete outcomes when the data mark a continuous region
  • computing the favourable area outside the total sample space region
  • applying the ratio when the region is unbounded (infinite total measure)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 6

Bayes' Theorem

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 5 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Bayes' theorem reverses conditional probabilities: it finds \(P(A_i | B)\), the posterior probability of a cause \(A_i\), from the prior \(P(A_i)\) and the likelihood \(P(B | A_i)\). It applies when the \(A_i\) are mutually exclusive and exhaustive events forming a partition of the sample space.
Key formula(s). \[ P(A_i | B) = \frac{P(A_i) P(B | A_i)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n} P(A_j) P(B | A_j)} \]
Key points.
  • requires the \(A_i\) to be mutually exclusive and exhaustive (a partition)
  • each numerator is \(P(A_i \cap B) = P(A_i) P(B | A_i)\) by the multiplication theorem
  • the denominator is exactly \(P(B)\) by the law of total probability
  • priors are often given as percentages of the population and \(P(B|A_i)\) as accuracy rates
  • tree diagrams organise the data: the \(A_i\) branches then the \(B\) evidence
  • the output is the posterior probability \(P(A_i|B)\), revising the prior in light of \(B\)
Example 1.

Bag I contains 3 red and 1 black ball; Bag II contains 2 red and 2 black balls. One bag is chosen at random and a red ball is drawn. Find the probability that the ball came from Bag I.

  1. \(P(I) = P(II) = \frac{1}{2}\), \(P(R|I) = \frac{3}{4}\), \(P(R|II) = \frac{1}{2}\)
  2. \(P(I|R) = \frac{P(I)P(R|I)}{P(I)P(R|I) + P(II)P(R|II)}\)
  3. \(P(I|R) = \frac{\frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{3}{4}}{\frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{3}{4} + \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{1}{2}} = \frac{3/8}{3/8 + 1/4}\)
  4. \(P(I|R) = \frac{3/8}{5/8} = \frac{3}{5}\)

Answer: \(P(I|R) = \frac{3}{5}\)

Example 2.

A diagnostic test detects a disease with 90% accuracy; 2% of the population actually has the disease. A person tests positive. Find the probability that he truly has the disease.

  1. \(P(D) = 0.02\), \(P(D^c) = 0.98\), \(P(+|D) = 0.9\), \(P(+|D^c) = 0.1\)
  2. \(P(D|+) = \frac{P(D)P(+|D)}{P(D)P(+|D) + P(D^c)P(+|D^c)}\)
  3. \(P(D|+) = \frac{0.02 \times 0.9}{0.02 \times 0.9 + 0.98 \times 0.1} = \frac{0.018}{0.116}\)
  4. \(P(D|+) = \frac{18}{116} = \frac{9}{58}\)

Answer: \(P(D|+) = \frac{9}{58} \approx 0.155\)

Common mistakes.
  • answering with \(P(B|A_i)\) when the question asks for \(P(A_i|B)\)
  • summing the posterior probabilities over non-partition events
  • omitting one of the denominator terms in \(\sum P(A_j)P(B|A_j)\)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 7

Dependent and Independent Events

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 6 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Two events \(A\) and \(B\) are independent when the occurrence of one does not change the probability of the other, i.e. \(P(A|B) = P(A)\); the defining test is \(P(A \cap B) = P(A)P(B)\). Dependent events fail this test.
Key formula(s). \[ P(A \cap B) = P(A) P(B) \iff A, B \text{ independent}, \qquad P(A | B) = P(A) \iff \text{independent} \]
Key points.
  • independence is symmetric: it also gives \(P(B|A) = P(B)\)
  • mutually exclusive events with \(P(A) > 0\), \(P(B) > 0\) are never independent
  • if \(A, B\) are independent, so are \(A, B^c\); \(A^c, B\); and \(A^c, B^c\)
  • independent events can both occur; mutually exclusive events cannot
  • three events are independent only if all pairwise products AND \(P(A \cap B \cap C) = P(A)P(B)P(C)\) hold
  • independence must be verified with the product condition, never assumed blindly
  • two independent trials: probabilities of combined outcomes multiply
Example 1.

Two events satisfy \(P(A) = \frac{1}{2}\), \(P(B) = \frac{1}{3}\), \(P(A \cup B) = \frac{2}{3}\). Determine whether \(A\) and \(B\) are independent.

  1. \(P(A \cap B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cup B)\)
  2. \(P(A \cap B) = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{3} - \frac{2}{3} = \frac{1}{6}\)
  3. \(P(A)P(B) = \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{6}\)
  4. since \(P(A \cap B) = P(A)P(B)\), the events are independent

Answer: Yes, \(A\) and \(B\) are independent.

Example 2.

A die is thrown twice. Are the events \(A\) = "first throw shows 2" and \(B\) = "sum of the throws is 7" independent?

  1. \(P(A) = \frac{1}{6}\); \(B = \{(1,6),(2,5),(3,4),(4,3),(5,2),(6,1)\}\), so \(P(B) = \frac{6}{36} = \frac{1}{6}\)
  2. \(A \cap B = \{(2,5)\}\), so \(P(A \cap B) = \frac{1}{36}\)
  3. \(P(A)P(B) = \frac{1}{6} \times \frac{1}{6} = \frac{1}{36} = P(A \cap B)\)

Answer: Yes, \(A\) and \(B\) are independent.

Common mistakes.
  • calling mutually exclusive events independent
  • verifying independence from symmetry, coincidental numerical agreement, without product test
  • assuming independence for dependent situations like draws without replacement
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 8

Multiplication Theorem

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 7 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The multiplication theorem gives the probability that both events occur: \(P(A \cap B) = P(A) \cdot P(B|A)\). It chains conditional factors for sequential experiments, reducing to \(P(A)P(B)\) when the events are independent.
Key formula(s). \[ P(A \cap B) = P(A) \cdot P(B | A) = P(B) \cdot P(A | B) \]
Key points.
  • extended form: \(P(A \cap B \cap C) = P(A) \cdot P(B|A) \cdot P(C|A \cap B)\)
  • choose whichever conditioning is easier to compute
  • for independent events it collapses to \(P(A)P(B)\)
  • the factor \(P(B|A)\) accounts for the reduced sample space after \(A\)
  • ideal for tree diagrams: multiply probabilities along a path
  • applies to "and" or "both" scenarios in consecutive draws
Example 1.

Two cards are drawn one after another without replacement from a pack of 52. Find the probability that both are kings.

  1. \(P(\text{1st king}) = \frac{4}{52}\)
  2. after a king is removed, \(P(\text{2nd king} | \text{1st king}) = \frac{3}{51}\)
  3. \(P(\text{both kings}) = \frac{4}{52} \times \frac{3}{51}\)
  4. \(P(\text{both kings}) = \frac{12}{2652}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{1}{221}\)

Example 2.

A bag contains 5 red and 3 black balls. Two balls are drawn without replacement. Find the probability that the first is red and the second is black.

  1. \(P(R_1) = \frac{5}{8}\)
  2. after one red is removed, \(P(B_2 | R_1) = \frac{3}{7}\)
  3. \(P(R_1 \cap B_2) = \frac{5}{8} \times \frac{3}{7} = \frac{15}{56}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{15}{56}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using \(P(B|A) = P(B)\) for draws without replacement, where the events are dependent
  • forgetting to shrink the sample space after the first draw
  • applying the theorem to "or" questions, which need the addition theorem
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 9

Law of Total Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 8 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The law of total probability computes \(P(B)\) by splitting the sample space into mutually exclusive, exhaustive parts \(A_1, \dots, A_n\) and summing the conditional probabilities \(P(B|A_i)\) weighted by \(P(A_i)\). It is the key tool for experiments that move through stages.
Key formula(s). \[ P(B) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} P(A_i) P(B | A_i) \]
Key points.
  • the \(A_i\) must form a partition of the sample space
  • the same expression is the denominator of Bayes' theorem
  • the weights \(P(A_i)\) sum to 1, guaranteeing a valid probability
  • with two parts the formula reads \(P(B) = P(A)P(B|A) + P(A^c)P(B|A^c)\)
  • tree diagrams show each branch as a product \(P(A_i)P(B|A_i)\)
  • used whenever selection happens in two stages: pick a group, then pick an outcome
Example 1.

Bag A contains 5 red and 5 white balls; Bag B contains 6 red and 4 white balls. A bag is picked at random and a ball is drawn. Find the probability that the ball is red.

  1. \(P(A) = P(B) = \frac{1}{2}\), \(P(R|A) = \frac{5}{10} = \frac{1}{2}\), \(P(R|B) = \frac{6}{10} = \frac{3}{5}\)
  2. \(P(R) = P(A)P(R|A) + P(B)P(R|B)\)
  3. \(P(R) = \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{3}{5} = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{3}{10}\)
  4. \(P(R) = \frac{5}{20} + \frac{6}{20} = \frac{11}{20}\)

Answer: \(P(R) = \frac{11}{20}\)

Example 2.

Machine M produces 40% of the bolts and machine N produces 60%; the defective rates are 4% and 2% respectively. Find the probability that a randomly chosen bolt is defective.

  1. \(P(M) = 0.4\), \(P(N) = 0.6\), \(P(D|M) = 0.04\), \(P(D|N) = 0.02\)
  2. \(P(D) = P(M)P(D|M) + P(N)P(D|N)\)
  3. \(P(D) = 0.4 \times 0.04 + 0.6 \times 0.02 = 0.016 + 0.012 = 0.028\)

Answer: \(P(D) = \frac{7}{250}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using parts that overlap or do not cover the whole sample space
  • forgetting to multiply \(P(B|A_i)\) by the weight \(P(A_i)\)
  • summing \(P(B|A_i)\) directly as if they were disjoint parts of \(P(B)\)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 10

Definition of Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 9 of 10

Explanation

Concept. The classical (a priori) definition of probability is the ratio of favourable equally likely outcomes to the total number of equally likely outcomes of an experiment: \(P(A) = \frac{n(A)}{n(S)}\). It rests on the fundamental assumption of equal likelihood.
Key formula(s). \[ P(A) = \frac{n(A)}{n(S)} = \frac{\text{number of favourable equally likely outcomes}}{\text{total number of equally likely outcomes}} \]
Key points.
  • valid only when all outcomes in the sample space are equally likely
  • \(0 \le P(A) \le 1\) always
  • \(P(S) = 1\) and \(P(\varnothing) = 0\)
  • complement identity: \(P(A) = 1 - P(A^c)\)
  • counting uses permutations or combinations, e.g. \(n(S) = \binom{n}{r}\)
  • axioms: \(P(A) \ge 0\), \(P(S) = 1\), and \(P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B)\) for disjoint \(A, B\)
Example 1.

Two dice are thrown together. Find the probability that the sum of the numbers shown is 9.

  1. \(n(S) = 36\)
  2. favourable outcomes: \((3,6), (4,5), (5,4), (6,3)\), so \(n(A) = 4\)
  3. \(P(A) = \frac{4}{36}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{1}{9}\)

Example 2.

A committee of 3 people is chosen at random from 5 men and 4 women. Find the probability that it has 2 men and 1 woman.

  1. \(n(S) = \binom{9}{3} = 84\)
  2. \(n(A) = \binom{5}{2} \binom{4}{1} = 10 \times 4 = 40\)
  3. \(P(A) = \frac{40}{84}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{10}{21}\)

Common mistakes.
  • applying the equal-likelihood ratio when outcomes are not equally likely
  • miscounting the sample space for draws with replacement versus without replacement
  • reporting \(n(A)/n(S)\) with \(n(A)\) and \(n(S)\) counted using different schemes
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 28 · Topic 11

Introduction to Probability

ProbabilityProbability & StatisticsTopic 10 of 10

Explanation

Concept. Probability quantifies the chance that an event occurs in a random experiment, whose set of all possible outcomes is the sample space \(S\); an event is any subset of \(S\). Probabilities always lie between 0 and 1, with 1 for the certain event and 0 for the impossible event.
Key formula(s). \[ P(S) = 1, \qquad 0 \le P(A) \le 1, \qquad P(\varnothing) = 0, \qquad P(A^c) = 1 - P(A) \]
Key points.
  • basic vocabulary: experiment, outcome, sample space \(S\), event, elementary events
  • a trial is deterministic in outcome only if it is not random; the sample space lists every possibility
  • the empty set is the impossible event; \(S\) itself is the certain event
  • under equal likelihood \(P(A) = \frac{n(A)}{n(S)}\)
  • the empirical definition uses relative frequency \(\frac{n(A)}{n}\) as repetitions \(n\) grow
  • problems solved by listing the sample space or by tree counting for sequential trials
  • mutually exclusive events cannot occur together; \(P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B)\) for them
Example 1.

A fair coin is tossed three times. Write the sample space and find the probability of getting exactly two heads.

  1. \(S = \{HHH, HHT, HTH, THH, HTT, THT, TTH, TTT\}\), so \(n(S) = 8\)
  2. favourable outcomes: \(HHT, HTH, THH\), so \(n(A) = 3\)
  3. \(P(A) = \frac{3}{8}\)

Answer: \(P(\text{exactly two heads}) = \frac{3}{8}\)

Example 2.

A die is rolled once. Find the probability of getting a number greater than 3.

  1. \(S = \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}\), so \(n(S) = 6\)
  2. \(A = \{4, 5, 6\}\), so \(n(A) = 3\)
  3. \(P(A) = \frac{3}{6}\)

Answer: \(P = \frac{1}{2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • omitting outcomes when writing the sample space of a sequential experiment
  • reporting probabilities outside the range \([0, 1]\)
  • confusing empirical relative frequency with the classical equal-likelihood probability
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

Statistics summarises data using averages and spread. The mean is the sum divided by the count. Variance is the average of squared deviations from the mean, and the standard deviation is its square root.

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Mean Deviation2. Mean3. Median4. Mode5. Variance and Standard Deviation
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 2

Mean Deviation

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsTopic 1 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The mean deviation of \(n\) observations is the average of the absolute deviations \(|x_i - A|\) from a central value \(A\), normally the mean or the median. Because it uses absolute values, positive and negative deviations cannot cancel out.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{MD} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} |x_i - A|}{n}, \qquad \text{MD (grouped)} = \frac{\sum f_i |x_i - A|}{\sum f_i} \]
Key points.
  • the absolute value bars make the deviation measure non-negative
  • mean deviation is minimum when measured about the median
  • about the mean, deviations always sum to zero, which is why absolute values are used
  • for grouped data the frequencies \(f_i\) are the weights and \(\sum f_i\) is the divisor
  • for continuous classes, \(x_i\) are the midpoints of the class intervals
  • mean deviation is less sensitive to outliers than standard deviation
  • combined mean deviation cannot be recovered from the individual MDs alone
Example 1.

Find the mean deviation about the mean of the data 3, 6, 8, 11, 12.

  1. \(\bar{x} = \frac{3 + 6 + 8 + 11 + 12}{5} = \frac{40}{5} = 8\)
  2. deviations: \(5, 2, 0, 3, 4\)
  3. \(\text{MD} = \frac{5 + 2 + 0 + 3 + 4}{5} = \frac{14}{5}\)

Answer: \(\text{MD} = 2.8\)

Example 2.

Find the mean deviation about the mean for the distribution \(x: 2, 4, 6, 8\) with frequencies \(4, 6, 8, 2\).

  1. \(\sum f x = 8 + 24 + 48 + 16 = 96\), \(\sum f = 20\), so \(\bar{x} = \frac{96}{20} = 4.8\)
  2. \(\sum f |x - \bar{x}| = 4(2.8) + 6(0.8) + 8(1.2) + 2(3.2) = 11.2 + 4.8 + 9.6 + 6.4 = 32\)
  3. \(\text{MD} = \frac{32}{20}\)

Answer: \(\text{MD} = 1.6\)

Common mistakes.
  • dropping the absolute value so that negative and positive deviations cancel
  • dividing by the number of classes instead of the total frequency \(\sum f_i\)
  • averaging raw class marks instead of first computing the mean about which deviations are taken
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 3

Mean

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsTopic 2 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The mean \(\bar{x}\) of \(n\) ungrouped observations is their arithmetic average; for grouped data it is the frequency-weighted average of the values or class marks. The assumed-mean trick centres the deviations to simplify arithmetic.
Key formula(s). \[ \bar{x} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i}{n}, \qquad \bar{x} = \frac{\sum f_i x_i}{\sum f_i} = A + \frac{\sum f_i d_i}{\sum f_i}, \quad d_i = x_i - A \]
Key points.
  • the sum of deviations about the mean is zero: \(\sum (x_i - \bar{x}) = 0\)
  • adding a constant \(c\) to every value shifts the mean by \(c\)
  • multiplying every value by \(k\) multiplies the mean by \(k\)
  • combined mean of two groups: \(\bar{x} = \frac{n_1 \bar{x}_1 + n_2 \bar{x}_2}{n_1 + n_2}\)
  • the mean always lies between the minimum and maximum observations
  • for continuous grouped data the class marks (midpoints) represent the classes
  • the assumed mean \(A\) may be any convenient value; it cancels out of the result
Example 1.

Find the mean of 12, 15, 18, 21, 24 using an assumed mean of 18.

  1. deviations: \(-6, -3, 0, 3, 6\); \(\sum d_i = 0\)
  2. \(\bar{x} = A + \frac{\sum d_i}{n} = 18 + \frac{0}{5}\)

Answer: \(\bar{x} = 18\)

Example 2.

The marks 40, 50, 60 and 70 are obtained by 5, 8, 7 and 4 students respectively. Find the mean mark.

  1. \(\sum w x = 5(40) + 8(50) + 7(60) + 4(70) = 200 + 400 + 420 + 280 = 1300\)
  2. \(\sum w = 5 + 8 + 7 + 4 = 24\)
  3. \(\bar{x} = \frac{1300}{24}\)

Answer: \(\bar{x} = \frac{325}{6} \approx 54.2\)

Common mistakes.
  • dividing by the number of classes rather than by the total frequency \(\sum f_i\)
  • combining two group means as the simple average, ignoring the group sizes
  • forgetting that scaling all values by \(k\) also scales the mean by \(k\)
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 4

Median

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsTopic 3 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The median is the middle observation of ordered data, splitting the data into two equal halves. For grouped data it is found from the class containing the \(\frac{n}{2}\)th observation by linear interpolation within that class.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{Median (ungrouped)} = \left( \frac{n+1}{2} \right)\text{th observation}; \qquad \text{Median (grouped)} = \ell + \frac{\frac{n}{2} - cf}{f} \times h \]
Key points.
  • sort the data ascending first; any ordering slip changes the answer
  • for even \(n\), average the two middle observations
  • \(\ell\) is the lower limit of the median class, \(f\) its frequency, \(cf\) the cumulative frequency just before it, \(h\) the class width
  • the median class is the first class whose cumulative frequency reaches or exceeds \(\frac{n}{2}\)
  • the median is robust: extreme values hardly affect it
  • for symmetric data, mean = median = mode
  • the median divides the frequency curve into two equal areas
Example 1.

Find the median of 13, 6, 9, 20, 15, 7, 12.

  1. arrange ascending: 6, 7, 9, 12, 13, 15, 20, so \(n = 7\)
  2. median is the \(\frac{7+1}{2} = 4\)th observation

Answer: Median = 12

Example 2.

Find the median of 5, 9, 2, 14, 7, 10.

  1. arrange ascending: 2, 5, 7, 9, 10, 14, so \(n = 6\)
  2. median is the mean of the 3rd and 4th observations
  3. Median = \(\frac{7 + 9}{2}\)

Answer: Median = 8

Common mistakes.
  • picking the middle value without sorting the data first
  • using the \(\frac{n}{2}\)th value for odd \(n\) instead of the \(\frac{n+1}{2}\)th
  • choosing the wrong class because cumulative frequencies were miscomputed
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 5

Mode

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsTopic 4 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The mode is the value with the highest frequency in the data, hence the most typical observation. For grouped data the modal class is the class with the greatest frequency and the mode is interpolated within it using the neighbouring frequencies.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{Mode} = \ell + \frac{f_1 - f_0}{2f_1 - f_0 - f_2} \times h \]
Key points.
  • for raw data: the value occurring the most number of times
  • for grouped data: \(\ell\) lower limit of modal class, \(f_1\) its frequency, \(f_0\) and \(f_2\) the frequencies of the preceding and following classes, \(h\) the class width
  • the modal class is the class with maximum frequency
  • a distribution may be bimodal or multimodal
  • the empirical relation \(\text{Mode} = 3 \times \text{Median} - 2 \times \text{Mean}\) holds approximately for moderately skewed data
  • the mode is unaffected by extreme values
Example 1.

Find the mode of 4, 7, 3, 7, 9, 7, 2.

  1. the value 7 occurs three times, more than any other value
  2. every other value occurs at most twice (3 occurs twice; 4, 9, 2 once each)

Answer: Mode = 7

Example 2.

For a continuous distribution the modal class is \(30{-}40\) with frequencies 12 (below), 28 (modal) and 18 (above). Find the mode.

  1. \(\ell = 30\), \(h = 10\), \(f_1 = 28\), \(f_0 = 12\), \(f_2 = 18\)
  2. \(\text{Mode} = 30 + \frac{28 - 12}{2(28) - 12 - 18} \times 10 = 30 + \frac{16}{26} \times 10\)
  3. \(\text{Mode} = 30 + \frac{160}{26} = 30 + \frac{80}{13}\)

Answer: \(\text{Mode} = \frac{470}{13} \approx 36.2\)

Common mistakes.
  • reporting the largest value instead of the most frequent value
  • quoting the modal class frequency as if it were the mode, without interpolation
  • using the empirical formula \(\text{Mode} = 3M - 2\bar{x}\) on strongly skewed or asymmetric data
Back to Index
Index / Probability & Statistics
Chapter 29 · Topic 6

Variance and Standard Deviation

StatisticsProbability & StatisticsTopic 5 of 5

Explanation

Concept. The variance \(\sigma^2\) is the average squared deviation of the observations from their mean, and the standard deviation \(\sigma\) is its square root, restoring the original units. Both measure the spread of the data about the mean.
Key formula(s). \[ \sigma^2 = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} (x_i - \bar{x})^2}{n} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i^2}{n} - \bar{x}^2, \qquad \sigma = \sqrt{\sigma^2} \]
Key points.
  • the standard deviation has the same units as the original data
  • shifting all values by a constant does not change the variance
  • scaling by \(a\) gives \(\text{Var}(aX + b) = a^2 \text{Var}(X)\), so \(\sigma(aX + b) = |a| \sigma(X)\)
  • grouped form: \(\sigma^2 = \frac{\sum f_i x_i^2}{\sum f_i} - \bar{x}^2\)
  • \(\sigma = 0\) exactly when all observations are equal
  • the JEE convention for a population of \(n\) observations divides by \(n\)
  • the coefficient of variation is \(\text{CV} = \frac{\sigma}{\bar{x}} \times 100\), used to compare data sets
Example 1.

Find the variance and standard deviation of 2, 4, 6, 8, 10.

  1. \(\bar{x} = 6\); \(\sum x^2 = 4 + 16 + 36 + 64 + 100 = 220\)
  2. \(\sigma^2 = \frac{220}{5} - 6^2 = 44 - 36 = 8\)
  3. \(\sigma = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2}\)

Answer: \(\sigma^2 = 8\), \(\sigma = 2\sqrt{2}\)

Example 2.

For 50 observations the mean is 20 and the sum of squares is 22000. Find the standard deviation.

  1. \(\sigma^2 = \frac{\sum x^2}{n} - \bar{x}^2 = \frac{22000}{50} - 20^2\)
  2. \(\sigma^2 = 440 - 400 = 40\)
  3. \(\sigma = \sqrt{40} = 2\sqrt{10}\)

Answer: \(\sigma = 2\sqrt{10}\)

Common mistakes.
  • subtracting \((E(X))^2\) with the wrong sign or using \(\sum x^2\) unweighted
  • using the denominator \(n-1\) where the JEE population convention uses \(n\)
  • thinking the variance changes when all values are shifted by a constant
  • reporting the variance when the standard deviation was asked, or vice versa
Back to Index

Chapter 30 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

Points in space have three coordinates (x, y, z). The distance between P and Q is \( \sqrt ((x_{2} - x_{1})^{2} + (y_{2} - y_{1})^{2} + (z_{2} - z_{1})^{2}) \) The midpoint is \( ((x_{1} + x_{2})/2, (y_{1} + y_{2})/2, (z_{1} + z_{2})/2) \) Direction ratios give the slopes along …

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

Chapter 30 · Topic 2

Equation of the Bisector Planes

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 1 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The two planes that bisect the angles between the planes \(a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1 z + d_1 = 0\) and \(a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2 z + d_2 = 0\) are the loci of points equally distant from the two planes. They are obtained by equating the normalised signed distances, which produces two planes differing by the ambiguous sign.
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1 z + d_1}{\sqrt{a_1^2 + b_1^2 + c_1^2}} = \pm \frac{a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2 z + d_2}{\sqrt{a_2^2 + b_2^2 + c_2^2}} \]
Key points.
  • there are two bisector planes, one from each sign, and they are mutually perpendicular
  • the bisector planes pass through the line of intersection of the two given planes
  • the bisector of the angle containing the origin is obtained by taking both constants with their given signs when the planes' constant terms have the same sign arrangement
  • to identify the acute-angle bisector, substitute a point of one plane into both candidates and compare the distances
  • if the two planes are parallel, the bisecting planes reduce to the single plane midway between them
  • the distance of any point on a bisector from the two original planes is equal
Example 1.

Find the bisector planes of the angles between \(x + 2y - 2z + 3 = 0\) and \(2x + y + 2z - 6 = 0\).

  1. normal lengths: \(\sqrt{1 + 4 + 4} = 3\) and \(\sqrt{4 + 1 + 4} = 3\)
  2. bisectors: \(\frac{x + 2y - 2z + 3}{3} = \pm \frac{2x + y + 2z - 6}{3}\)
  3. with \(+\): \(-x + y - 4z + 9 = 0\); with \(-\): \(3x + 3y - 3 = 0\)

Answer: \(x - y + 4z - 9 = 0\) and \(x + y - 1 = 0\)

Example 2.

Show that the origin lies on one of the bisector planes of the angles between \(x + 2y + 3z + 1 = 0\) and \(3x + 2y + z - 1 = 0\), and find that bisector.

  1. distances of the origin: \(1/\sqrt{14}\) and \(-1/\sqrt{14}\), equal in magnitude since both normal lengths are \(\sqrt{14}\)
  2. the signed distances have opposite signs, so the originlies on the minus-sign bisector
  3. \(\frac{x + 2y + 3z + 1}{\sqrt{14}} = -\frac{3x + 2y + z - 1}{\sqrt{14}}\)
  4. \(4x + 4y + 4z = 0\)

Answer: \(x + y + z = 0\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting to divide by the normal vector lengths before equating the distances
  • dropping the \(\pm\) and finding only one bisector plane
  • choosing the wrong sign for the angle containing the origin
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 30 · Topic 3

Equation of Line

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 2 of 6

Explanation

Concept. A line in space is fixed by a point \(A(x_1, y_1, z_1)\) and a direction; if \((l, m, n)\) is a set of direction ratios, the symmetric equation of the line is \(\frac{x - x_1}{l} = \frac{y - y_1}{m} = \frac{z - z_1}{n}\). The same line in vector form is \(\vec{r} = \vec{r}_1 + \lambda \vec{b}\).
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{x - x_1}{l} = \frac{y - y_1}{m} = \frac{z - z_1}{n} = \lambda \]
Key points.
  • through two points \(P(x_1,y_1,z_1)\), \(Q(x_2,y_2,z_2)\) the direction ratios are \((x_2 - x_1, y_2 - y_1, z_2 - z_1)\)
  • the symmetric form needs a non-zero direction ratio; if \(l = 0\), write \(x = x_1\) separately and use the other two ratios
  • any scalar multiple \((kl, km, kn)\) gives the same line
  • if \((l, m, n)\) are genuine direction cosines, the parameter \(\lambda\) measures distance along the line and \(l = \cos\alpha,\ m = \cos\beta,\ n = \cos\gamma\)
  • the line through two points in symmetric form: \(\frac{x - x_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{y - y_1}{y_2 - y_1} = \frac{z - z_1}{z_2 - z_1}\)
  • a line parallel to the plane \(ax + by + cz + d = 0\) must satisfy \(al + bm + cn = 0\)
Example 1.

Find the equation of the line through \(A(1, 2, 3)\) and \(B(3, 6, 5)\).

  1. direction ratios: \((3 - 1, 6 - 2, 5 - 3) = (2, 4, 2)\)
  2. reduce to the equivalent triple \((1, 2, 1)\)
  3. write through \(A\): \(\frac{x - 1}{1} = \frac{y - 2}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{1}\)

Answer: \(\frac{x - 1}{1} = \frac{y - 2}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{1}\)

Example 2.

Show that \(P(4, 0, 2)\) does not lie on the line \(\frac{x-1}{1} = \frac{y+2}{2} = \frac{z-3}{-1}\).

  1. parametrize with \(\lambda\): \(x = 1 + \lambda,\ y = -2 + 2\lambda,\ z = 3 - \lambda\)
  2. from \(x = 4\): \(\lambda = 3\)
  3. then \(y = -2 + 6 = 4 \ne 0\) and \(z = 0 \ne 2\), so the ratios are inconsistent

Answer: P is not on the line

Common mistakes.
  • using the reversed difference \((P - Q)\) in one coordinate and \((Q - P)\) in another
  • dividing by zero when a direction ratio is zero instead of using the split form \(x = x_1\)
  • treating \(\lambda\) as a distance when \((l, m, n)\) are not direction cosines
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 30 · Topic 4

Line and Plane

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 3 of 6

Explanation

Concept. A line and a plane in space either meet in a single point, are parallel, or the line lies entirely in the plane. The angle \(\theta\) between a line with direction ratios \((l, m, n)\) and the plane \(ax + by + cz + d = 0\) satisfies \(\sin\theta = \frac{|al + bm + cn|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}\sqrt{l^2 + m^2 + n^2}}\).
Key formula(s). \[ \sin\theta = \frac{|al + bm + cn|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}\sqrt{l^2 + m^2 + n^2}};\qquad \text{line in plane iff } al + bm + cn = 0 \text{ and the point lies on the plane} \]
Key points.
  • the line is parallel to the plane iff \(al + bm + cn = 0\); it lies in the plane if in addition its point satisfies the plane
  • the line is perpendicular to the plane iff \((l, m, n)\) is proportional to the normal \((a, b, c)\)
  • to find the intersection point, parametrise the line and substitute into the plane equation
  • the foot of the perpendicular from a point to a plane is found the same way, walking along the normal
  • the angle between a line and a plane complements the angle between the line and the plane's normal
  • the distance of a point \((x_1, y_1, z_1)\) from the plane is \(\frac{|a x_1 + b y_1 + c z_1 + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}\)
Example 1.

Show that the line \(\frac{x-1}{1} = \frac{y-2}{-2} = \frac{z-3}{1}\) is parallel to the plane \(x + 2y + 3z - 5 = 0\), and find the distance between them.

  1. direction ratios \((1, -2, 1)\), normal \((1, 2, 3)\)
  2. dot product \(= 1 - 4 + 3 = 0\), so the line is parallel to the plane
  3. pick the point \(A(1, 2, 3)\) on the line; distance \(= \frac{|1 + 4 + 9 - 5|}{\sqrt{14}} = \frac{9}{\sqrt{14}}\)

Answer: parallel; distance \(= \frac{9}{\sqrt{14}}\)

Example 2.

Find the point where the line \(x = 1 + \lambda,\ y = 2 + 2\lambda,\ z = 3\lambda\) meets the plane \(x - y + z = 2\).

  1. substitute: \((1 + \lambda) - (2 + 2\lambda) + 3\lambda = 2\)
  2. \(1 - 2 + \lambda - 2\lambda + 3\lambda = 2 \Rightarrow -1 + 2\lambda = 2\)
  3. \(\lambda = 3/2\): point \((1 + 3/2,\ 2 + 3,\ 9/2)\)

Answer: \(\left(\frac{5}{2},\ 5,\ \frac{9}{2}\right)\)

Common mistakes.
  • using a cosine relation instead of \(\sin\theta\) for a line-plane angle
  • treating "parallel to the plane" as automatically "in the plane"
  • substituting the plane equation into the line instead of vice versa
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 30 · Topic 5

Family of Planes

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 4 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Every plane passing through the line of intersection of two planes \(P_1 = a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1 z + d_1 = 0\) and \(P_2 = a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2 z + d_2 = 0\) belongs to the family \(P_1 + \lambda P_2 = 0\). Choosing the parameter \(\lambda\) to meet an additional condition such as passage through a point or perpendicularity selects the required plane.
Key formula(s). \[ P_1 + \lambda P_2 = 0 \]
Key points.
  • the family is linear in \(x, y, z\) for each fixed \(\lambda\), hence always a plane
  • the plane \(P_2 = 0\) itself is recovered only in the limit \(\lambda \to \infty\)
  • choose \(\lambda\) so that the plane passes through a prescribed point not on the intersection line
  • to make the member perpendicular to another plane, impose the zero dot product of its normal with that plane's normal
  • planes parallel to \(ax + by + cz + d = 0\) form the family \(ax + by + cz + \lambda = 0\)
  • a line given in symmetric form can be written as the intersection of two planes, then extended to a family through it
Example 1.

Find the plane through the origin and the line of intersection of \(x + y + z - 3 = 0\) and \(x - y + 2z - 1 = 0\).

  1. family: \((x + y + z - 3) + \lambda(x - y + 2z - 1) = 0\)
  2. pass through the origin: \(-3 + \lambda(-1) = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -3\)
  3. \((x + y + z - 3) - 3(x - y + 2z - 1) = 0\)
  4. \(-2x + 4y - 5z = 0\)

Answer: \(2x - 4y + 5z = 0\)

Example 2.

Find the plane through the line of intersection of \(x + y + z = 1\) and \(2x + 3y - z = 4\) that is perpendicular to \(x - y + z = 4\).

  1. family: \((x + y + z - 1) + \lambda(2x + 3y - z - 4) = 0\), normal \((1 + 2\lambda, 1 + 3\lambda, 1 - \lambda)\)
  2. perpendicularity to \(x - y + z = 4\): \((1 + 2\lambda) - (1 + 3\lambda) + (1 - \lambda) = 0\)
  3. \(1 - 2\lambda = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1/2\)
  4. plane: \(2x + \frac{5}{2}y + \frac{1}{2}z - 3 = 0\)

Answer: \(4x + 5y + z = 6\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting that \(P_2 = 0\) cannot be reached with a finite \(\lambda\)
  • imposing the through-point condition without carrying the constant terms \(d_1, d_2\)
  • confusing the family of planes through a line with the family of planes parallel to a plane
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 30 · Topic 6

Introduction to 3D-Geometry

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 5 of 6

Explanation

Concept. Three-dimensional coordinate geometry describes points by ordered triples \((x, y, z)\) measured from three mutually perpendicular coordinate planes through the origin. The distance between \(A(x_1, y_1, z_1)\) and \(B(x_2, y_2, z_2)\) is \(\sqrt{(x_2-x_1)^2 + (y_2-y_1)^2 + (z_2-z_1)^2}\), and the section formula generalises coordinate-wise from two dimensions.
Key formula(s). \[ AB^2 = (x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2 + (z_2 - z_1)^2;\qquad \text{internal division } m:n:\ x = \frac{m x_2 + n x_1}{m + n} \]
Key points.
  • the coordinate planes are \(x = 0\) (yz-plane), \(y = 0\), and \(z = 0\), meeting at the origin \((0,0,0)\)
  • the midpoint of \(AB\) is \(\left(\frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}, \frac{z_1 + z_2}{2}\right)\)
  • the distance of a point from the origin is \(\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}\)
  • internal division of the segment \(AB\) in the ratio \(m : n\) uses \(\frac{m x_2 + n x_1}{m + n}\), and the external form uses \(m - n\) in the denominator
  • the centroid of a triangle is the average of its three vertex coordinates
  • collinearity can be verified by checking that one distance equals the sum of the other two, or that the parameters of section agree
  • the signs of \((x, y, z)\) fix the octant; all positive gives the first octant
Example 1.

Find the distance between the points \((1, -2, 4)\) and \((3, 3, -1)\).

  1. differences: \(dx = 2,\ dy = 5,\ dz = -5\)
  2. \(AB^2 = 2^2 + 5^2 + (-5)^2 = 4 + 25 + 25 = 54\)
  3. \(AB = \sqrt{54}\)

Answer: \(3\sqrt{6}\)

Example 2.

Find the point dividing the segment from \((2, 3, 4)\) to \((6, 9, 14)\) internally in the ratio \(1 : 2\).

  1. \(x = \frac{1 \cdot 6 + 2 \cdot 2}{1 + 2} = \frac{10}{3}\)
  2. \(y = \frac{1 \cdot 9 + 2 \cdot 3}{3} = \frac{15}{3} = 5\)
  3. \(z = \frac{1 \cdot 14 + 2 \cdot 4}{3} = \frac{22}{3}\)

Answer: \(\left(\frac{10}{3},\ 5,\ \frac{22}{3}\right)\)

Common mistakes.
  • subtracting coordinates in wrong pairs in the distance formula, e.g. \(x_1 - x_2\) in one place and \(x_2 - x_1\) in another (the squares hide the error but mixed pairs lose terms)
  • dividing by \(m - n\) for internal division instead of \(m + n\)
  • omitting the final square root in the distance formula
Back to Index
Index / Coordinate Geometry
Chapter 30 · Topic 7

Direction Ratio and Direction Cosines

3D Coordinate GeometryCoordinate GeometryTopic 6 of 6

Explanation

Concept. The direction cosines of a line are the cosines \(l, m, n\) of the angles it makes with the positive coordinate axes, satisfying \(l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1\). Direction ratios are any triple \((a, b, c)\) proportional to \((l, m, n)\); the actual cosines are recovered by dividing each ratio by \(\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}\).
Key formula(s). \[ l = \frac{a}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}},\quad m = \frac{b}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}},\quad n = \frac{c}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}} \]
Key points.
  • always \(l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1\)
  • direction ratios are not unique; \((ka, kb, kc)\) describe the same line for any \(k \ne 0\)
  • the angle between two lines with direction cosines \((l_1, m_1, n_1)\) and \((l_2, m_2, n_2)\): \(\cos\theta = |l_1 l_2 + m_1 m_2 + n_1 n_2|\)
  • perpendicular lines: \(l_1 l_2 + m_1 m_2 + n_1 n_2 = 0\); parallel lines: ratios proportional
  • direction ratios of the line joining two points are the coordinate differences
  • the projection of a segment of length \(L\) on an axis is \(L\) times the corresponding direction cosine
  • direction cosines are also written as \((\cos\alpha, \cos\beta, \cos\gamma)\)
Example 1.

Find the direction cosines of the line whose direction ratios are \((2, -1, 2)\).

  1. \(\sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 4} = 3\)
  2. \(l = 2/3,\ m = -1/3,\ n = 2/3\)
  3. check: \(l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 4/9 + 1/9 + 4/9 = 1\)

Answer: \(\left(\frac{2}{3}, -\frac{1}{3}, \frac{2}{3}\right)\)

Example 2.

Find the cosine of the angle between the lines whose direction ratios are \((1, 2, 2)\) and \((1, 1, 1)\).

  1. normalising first line: \((1/3, 2/3, 2/3)\); second line: \((1/\sqrt{3}, 1/\sqrt{3}, 1/\sqrt{3})\)
  2. \(\cos\theta = \frac{1}{3\sqrt{3}} + \frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}} + \frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}\)
  3. \(\cos\theta = \frac{5}{3\sqrt{3}}\)

Answer: \(\frac{5}{3\sqrt{3}}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using unnormalised direction ratios in place of direction cosines in the dot-product formula
  • dropping the absolute value and reporting the obtuse angle systematically
  • confusing the direction cosines of a line with the coordinates of a point on it
Back to Index
Index / Calculus
Chapter 31 · Topic 1

Introduction & Instructions

The area under a curve y \( = \) f(x) from x \( = \) a to b is the definite integral \( ∫_{a}^{b} \) f(x) dx. Area is always positive, so if the curve dips below the x-axis, take the absolute value of each separate region and add.
