Chapter 35 · Topic 2

Inradius

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 1 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The inradius \(r\) of a triangle is the radius of the incircle that touches all three sides; its centre is the incenter, the intersection of the angle bisectors. The basic identity is \(r = \frac{\Delta}{s}\), where \(\Delta\) is the area and \(s\) the semiperimeter.
Key formula(s). \[ r = \frac{\Delta}{s} = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2},\qquad r = (s-c)\tan\frac{C}{2} \]
Key points.
  • incenter is the intersection point of the three internal angle bisectors
  • definition: \(r = \frac{\Delta}{s}\), equivalently area \(\Delta = rs\)
  • \(r = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}\)
  • tangent length from vertex \(C\) is \(s-c\), giving \(r = (s-c)\tan\frac{C}{2}\)
  • in a right triangle with legs \(a, b\) and hypotenuse \(c\): \(r = \frac{a+b-c}{2}\)
  • distances from the incenter to the three sides are all equal to \(r\)
  • \(\tan\frac{A}{2} = \frac{r}{s-a}\) connects inradius and half-angle formulas
Example 1.

Find the inradius of the triangle with sides \(13, 14, 15\).

  1. \(s = \frac{13+14+15}{2} = 21\), so \(s-a = 8,\ s-b = 7,\ s-c = 6\)
  2. Heron: \(\Delta = \sqrt{21\cdot8\cdot7\cdot6} = 84\)
  3. \(r = \frac{\Delta}{s} = \frac{84}{21}\)

Answer: \(r = 4\)

Example 2.

Find the inradius of a right triangle with legs \(6\) and \(8\).

  1. hypotenuse \(c = \sqrt{6^2+8^2} = 10\)
  2. \(r = \frac{a+b-c}{2} = \frac{6+8-10}{2}\)
  3. \(r = \frac{4}{2}\)

Answer: \(r = 2\)

Common mistakes.
  • using \(s = a+b+c\) instead of \(s = \frac{a+b+c}{2}\) in \(\Delta = rs\)
  • applying \(r = \frac{a+b-c}{2}\) to a triangle that is not right-angled
  • confusing incenter (angle-bisector intersection) with circumcenter
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 3

Circumradius

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 2 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The circumradius \(R\) is the radius of the circumcircle passing through the three vertices of the triangle; its centre is the circumcenter, the intersection of the perpendicular bisectors of the sides. The extended sine rule fixes \(R\) from one side and its opposite angle.
Key formula(s). \[ R = \frac{abc}{4\Delta} = \frac{a}{2\sin A} = \frac{b}{2\sin B} = \frac{c}{2\sin C},\qquad a = 2R\sin A \]
Key points.
  • circumcenter is the intersection of perpendicular bisectors of the sides
  • extended sine rule: \(\frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B} = \frac{c}{\sin C} = 2R\)
  • \(R = \frac{abc}{4\Delta}\) is the standard area-based formula
  • in a right triangle with hypotenuse \(c\), \(R = \frac{c}{2}\)
  • for an obtuse triangle the circumcenter lies outside, but the formulas still hold
  • a larger side subtends a larger angle: \(\sin A \propto a\)
  • combining \(R\) with the cosine rule solves mixed side-angle data
Example 1.

Find the circumradius of the triangle with sides \(13, 14, 15\).

  1. \(\Delta = 84\) from Heron's formula, and \(abc = 13\times14\times15 = 2730\)
  2. \(R = \frac{abc}{4\Delta} = \frac{2730}{4\times84}\)
  3. \(R = \frac{2730}{336} = \frac{65}{8}\)

Answer: \(R = \frac{65}{8}\)

Example 2.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(a = 2, b = 3, C = 60^\circ\). Find \(R\).

  1. \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab\cos C = 4 + 9 - 6 = 7\), so \(c = \sqrt{7}\)
  2. \(\Delta = \frac{1}{2}ab\sin C = \frac{1}{2}\times2\times3\times\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\)
  3. \(R = \frac{abc}{4\Delta} = \frac{2\times3\times\sqrt{7}}{4\times\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}} = \frac{6\sqrt{7}}{6\sqrt{3}}\)

Answer: \(R = \sqrt{\frac{7}{3}}\)

Common mistakes.
  • pairing a side with a non-opposite angle in \(a = 2R\sin A\)
  • forgetting the factor \(4\) in \(R = \frac{abc}{4\Delta}\)
  • writing \(R = \frac{c}{2}\) in a triangle that is not right-angled
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 4

Exradius

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 3 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The exradii \(r_a, r_b, r_c\) are the radii of the three excircles, each tangent to one side and to the extensions of the other two sides; the corresponding excenter is opposite one vertex. The formula is \(r_a = \frac{\Delta}{s-a}\).
Key formula(s). \[ r_a = \frac{\Delta}{s-a},\quad r_b = \frac{\Delta}{s-b},\quad r_c = \frac{\Delta}{s-c},\qquad \frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{r_a} + \frac{1}{r_b} + \frac{1}{r_c} \]
Key points.
  • the excircle opposite \(A\) is tangent to side \(BC\) and the extensions of \(AB, AC\)
  • \(r_a = \frac{\Delta}{s-a}\) with cyclic analogues for \(r_b, r_c\)
  • excenter opposite \(A\) lies on the internal bisector of \(A\) and external bisectors of \(B, C\)
  • \(r_a = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}\)
  • \(\frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{r_a} + \frac{1}{r_b} + \frac{1}{r_c}\), useful for sums of reciprocals
  • products like \(r_ar_br_c\) combine with Heron's formula, but most problems reduce to \(\Delta = rs\) type relations
  • the excenter lies outside the triangle, unlike the incenter
Example 1.

Triangle with sides \(13, 14, 15\) has \(\Delta = 84, s = 21\). Find \(r_a\).

  1. \(s - a = 21 - 13 = 8\)
  2. \(r_a = \frac{\Delta}{s-a} = \frac{84}{8}\)
  3. \(r_a = \frac{21}{2}\)

Answer: \(r_a = \frac{21}{2}\)

Example 2.

For the same triangle verify \(\frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{r_a} + \frac{1}{r_b} + \frac{1}{r_c}\).

  1. \(r = 4\), and \(r_a = \frac{21}{2}, r_b = \frac{84}{7} = 12, r_c = \frac{84}{6} = 14\)
  2. sum of reciprocals = \(\frac{2}{21} + \frac{1}{12} + \frac{1}{14}\)
  3. common denominator 84: \(\frac{8+7+6}{84} = \frac{21}{84} = \frac{1}{4}\)

Answer: \(\frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{r_a}+\frac{1}{r_b}+\frac{1}{r_c}\) holds

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(r_a = \frac{\Delta}{s+a}\) instead of \(\frac{\Delta}{s-a}\)
  • placing the excenter inside the triangle
  • confusing the excircle opposite a vertex with the side it touches
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 5

Length of Angle Bisectors, Medians and Altitudes

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 4 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The standard cevians have lengths expressible in terms of the sides: medians \(m_a\), internal angle bisectors \(l_a\), and altitudes \(h_a\). These formulas appear constantly in JEE solutions of triangles.
Key formula(s). \[ m_a = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2b^2 + 2c^2 - a^2},\qquad l_a = \frac{2bc\cos\frac{A}{2}}{b+c},\qquad h_a = \frac{2\Delta}{a} = c\sin B \]
Key points.
  • median divides the opposite side in half and splits the triangle into two equal-area triangles
  • \(m_a^2 = \frac{2b^2 + 2c^2 - a^2}{4}\), with cyclic versions for \(m_b, m_c\)
  • the internal angle bisector of \(A\) divides side \(BC\) in the ratio \(c : b\)
  • angle bisector length: \(l_a = \frac{2bc\cos\frac{A}{2}}{b+c} = \sqrt{bc\Big(1 - \frac{a^2}{(b+c)^2}\Big)}\)
  • altitude: \(h_a = \frac{2\Delta}{a} = b\sin C = c\sin B\)
  • the centroid divides each median internally in the ratio \(2:1\)
  • in a right triangle, an altitude can be found by the area product \(h = \frac{ab}{c}\)
Example 1.

In a triangle with sides \(a = 6, b = 7, c = 8\), find the median to side \(a\).

  1. \(m_a = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2b^2 + 2c^2 - a^2}\)
  2. \(m_a = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2(49) + 2(64) - 36}\)
  3. \(m_a = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{98 + 128 - 36} = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{190}\)

Answer: \(m_a = \frac{\sqrt{190}}{2}\)

Example 2.

Triangle with sides \(a = 13, b = 14, c = 15\) has \(\Delta = 84\). Find the altitude \(h_a\).

  1. \(h_a = \frac{2\Delta}{a}\)
  2. \(h_a = \frac{2\times84}{13}\)
  3. \(h_a = \frac{168}{13}\)

Answer: \(h_a = \frac{168}{13}\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting the factor \(\frac{1}{2}\) in the median formula
  • putting \(a^2\) instead of \(bc\) in the angle bisector length
  • writing \(h_a = \frac{\Delta}{a}\) and missing the factor 2
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 6

Orthocentre and Distance between Special Points

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 5 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The orthocentre \(H\) is the intersection of the three altitudes; combined with the circumcentre \(O\), centroid \(G\), and incentre \(I\), it satisfies Euler-type distance formulas such as \(OH^2 = 9R^2 - (a^2+b^2+c^2)\) and \(OI^2 = R^2 - 2Rr\).
Key formula(s). \[ AH = 2R\cos A,\qquad OH^2 = 9R^2 - (a^2+b^2+c^2),\qquad OI^2 = R^2 - 2Rr \]
Key points.
  • the orthocentre lies inside an acute triangle, at the right-angle vertex of a right triangle, and outside an obtuse triangle
  • \(AH = 2R\cos A,\ BH = 2R\cos B,\ CH = 2R\cos C\)
  • Euler line: \(O, G, H\) are collinear with \(OG : GH = 1 : 2\)
  • \(OH^2 = 9R^2 - (a^2 + b^2 + c^2)\), the Euler distance formula
  • \(OI^2 = R^2 - 2Rr\), the Euler formula for the incenter-circumcenter distance
  • reflections of \(H\) across the sides lie on the circumcircle
  • the circumcircle of the pedal triangle is the nine-point circle with radius \(\frac{R}{2}\)
Example 1.

For the triangle with sides \(13, 14, 15\), given \(R = \frac{65}{8}\), find \(OH\).

  1. \(a^2+b^2+c^2 = 169 + 196 + 225 = 590\) and \(9R^2 = 9\times\frac{4225}{64} = \frac{38025}{64}\)
  2. \(OH^2 = \frac{38025}{64} - 590 = \frac{38025-37760}{64} = \frac{265}{64}\)
  3. \(OH = \sqrt{\frac{265}{64}}\)

Answer: \(OH = \frac{\sqrt{265}}{8}\)

Example 2.

Find \(OI\) for the right triangle with legs \(6, 8\) and hypotenuse \(10\).

  1. \(R = \frac{10}{2} = 5\) and \(r = \frac{6+8-10}{2} = 2\)
  2. Euler: \(OI^2 = R^2 - 2Rr = 25 - 2(5)(2)\)
  3. \(OI^2 = 25 - 20 = 5\)

Answer: \(OI = \sqrt{5}\)

Common mistakes.
  • computing \(AH = 2R\cos A\) with the wrong angle
  • thinking the orthocentre always lies inside the triangle
  • sign errors in \(OH^2\) (the squares of the sides are subtracted, not added to \(9R^2\))
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 7

Solution of Triangle and Ambiguous Case

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 6 of 16

Explanation

Concept. In the ambiguous (SSA) case, two sides and an angle not included between them are given; the sine rule returns \(\sin B\), and depending on its value there may be zero, one, or two distinct triangles.
Key formula(s). \[ \sin B = \frac{b\sin A}{a}:\quad b\sin A > a \Rightarrow 0\ \text{triangles};\quad b\sin A = a \Rightarrow 1;\quad b\sin A < a < b \Rightarrow 2;\quad a \ge b \Rightarrow 1 \]
Key points.
  • SSA data is the only case that can be ambiguous
  • compute \(\sin B = \frac{b\sin A}{a}\); if the value exceeds \(1\), no triangle exists
  • if two values \(B\) and \(180^\circ - B\) both satisfy \(A + B < 180^\circ\), two triangles exist
  • two solutions require \(b\sin A < a < b\) (the side opposite the given angle is shorter)
  • exactly one solution when \(a \ge b\) or \(b\sin A = a\) (right triangle at \(B\))
  • SSS, SAS, and ASA data always determine a unique triangle
  • always finish by checking \(A + B < 180^\circ\) for each candidate \(B\)
Example 1.

Given \(A = 30^\circ\), \(a = 4\), \(b = 8\). How many triangles satisfy the data?

  1. \(\sin B = \frac{b\sin A}{a} = \frac{8\times\frac{1}{2}}{4}\)
  2. \(\sin B = 1\), so \(B = 90^\circ\)
  3. this is the right-triangle limiting case, giving a unique triangle

Answer: exactly one triangle

Example 2.

Given \(A = 30^\circ\), \(a = 5\), \(b = 8\). How many triangles satisfy the data?

  1. \(\sin B = \frac{8\sin30^\circ}{5} = \frac{4}{5} < 1\)
  2. candidates \(B = \sin^{-1}\frac{4}{5} \approx 53^\circ\) and \(B = 127^\circ\)
  3. \(A + B = 83^\circ\) and \(157^\circ\) are both \(< 180^\circ\), and \(5 < 8\)
  4. the ambiguous case is active, giving two triangles

Answer: two triangles

Common mistakes.
  • assuming every SSA datum gives two triangles (only \(b\sin A < a < b\) does)
  • accepting \(B = 180^\circ - \sin^{-1}x\) without checking \(A + B < 180^\circ\)
  • ignoring the impossible case \(\sin B > 1\)
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 8

Orthic Triangle (Pedal)

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 7 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The orthic (pedal) triangle is formed by the feet of the altitudes of an acute triangle \(ABC\). Its sides, angles, and circumradius are all expressible in terms of the original triangle.
Key formula(s). \[ EF = a\cos A,\ FD = b\cos B,\ DE = c\cos C;\qquad \text{angles } \pi-2A,\ \pi-2B,\ \pi-2C \]
Key points.
  • let \(D, E, F\) be the feet of the altitudes from \(A, B, C\) onto \(BC, CA, AB\)
  • side lengths \(DE = c\cos C, EF = a\cos A, FD = b\cos B\)
  • the angles of the orthic triangle are \(\pi - 2A,\ \pi - 2B,\ \pi - 2C\)
  • its circumcircle is the nine-point circle, so its circumradius is \(\frac{R}{2}\)
  • the altitudes of \(ABC\) bisect the angles of the orthic triangle
  • the orthocentre \(H\) of \(ABC\) is the incenter of the orthic triangle
  • area of the orthic triangle is \(2\Delta\cos A\cos B\cos C\)
  • results require an acute triangle so the feet lie on the sides
Example 1.

In an acute triangle with \(A = 60^\circ, B = 45^\circ, C = 75^\circ\), find the angles of the orthic triangle.

  1. the angles are \(\pi - 2A,\ \pi - 2B,\ \pi - 2C\)
  2. \(\pi - 2(60^\circ) = 60^\circ\)
  3. \(\pi - 2(45^\circ) = 90^\circ\), and \(\pi - 2(75^\circ) = 30^\circ\)

Answer: angles are \(60^\circ, 90^\circ, 30^\circ\)

Example 2.

Show that the circumradius of the orthic triangle is \(\frac{R}{2}\).

  1. the circumcircle of the orthic triangle is the nine-point circle of \(ABC\)
  2. the nine-point circle passes through the three altitude feet and the three side midpoints
  3. it is the image of the circumcircle under a homothety of ratio \(\frac{1}{2}\) centred at the orthocentre
  4. hence its radius is half the circumradius

Answer: circumradius of the orthic triangle = \(\frac{R}{2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • applying orthic-triangle formulas to obtuse triangles where the feet fall outside the sides
  • writing the orthic angles as \(2A\) instead of \(\pi - 2A\)
  • confusing the altitude feet with the side midpoints
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 9

Regular Polygon

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 8 of 16

Explanation

Concept. A regular polygon with \(n\) sides inscribed in a circle of radius \(R\) has each side subtending the central angle \(\frac{2\pi}{n}\); its side, inradius (apothem), and area are simple functions of \(n\).
Key formula(s). \[ a = 2R\sin\frac{\pi}{n},\qquad r = R\cos\frac{\pi}{n},\qquad \Delta = \frac{1}{2}nar = \frac{n}{4}a^2\cot\frac{\pi}{n} = \frac{nR^2}{2}\sin\frac{2\pi}{n} \]
Key points.
  • each side subtends the central angle \(\frac{2\pi}{n}\), half of which gives \(a = 2R\sin\frac{\pi}{n}\)
  • the apothem (inradius, distance from centre to a side) is \(r = R\cos\frac{\pi}{n}\)
  • every interior angle equals \(\frac{(n-2)\pi}{n}\)
  • area is \(n\) times the area of one isoceles triangle: \(\frac{nR^2}{2}\sin\frac{2\pi}{n}\)
  • area also equals \(\frac{1}{2}\times n \times a \times r\)
  • perimeter \(= n a\); sum of interior angles \(= (n-2)\pi\)
Example 1.

Find the side of a regular hexagon inscribed in a circle of radius \(4\).

  1. \(n = 6\) and \(R = 4\), with central half-angle \(\frac{\pi}{6}\)
  2. \(a = 2R\sin\frac{\pi}{n} = 2(4)\sin\frac{\pi}{6}\)
  3. \(a = 8\times\frac{1}{2}\)

Answer: \(a = 4\)

Example 2.

Find the area of a regular octagon inscribed in a circle of radius \(\sqrt{2}\).

  1. the octagon splits into 8 triangles, each with two sides \(R\) and vertex angle \(45^\circ\)
  2. \(\Delta = \frac{nR^2}{2}\sin\frac{2\pi}{n} = \frac{8(\sqrt{2})^2}{2}\sin\frac{\pi}{4}\)
  3. \(\Delta = 8\times\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\)

Answer: \(\Delta = 4\sqrt{2}\)

Common mistakes.
  • using the full central angle \(\frac{2\pi}{n}\) in \(a = 2R\sin(...)\) instead of \(\frac{\pi}{n}\)
  • confusing circumradius with the apothem in the area formula
  • mixing degrees and radians in \(\sin\frac{\pi}{n}\)
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 10

Cyclic Quadrilateral

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 9 of 16

Explanation

Concept. A cyclic quadrilateral has all four vertices on a circle; its opposite angles sum to \(\pi\), its diagonals satisfy Ptolemy's theorem, and its area is given by Brahmagupta's generalization of Heron's formula.
Key formula(s). \[ A+C = B+D = \pi,\qquad AC\cdot BD = AB\cdot CD + BC\cdot DA,\qquad \Delta = \sqrt{(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d)} \]
Key points.
  • a quadrilateral is cyclic if and only if opposite angles are supplementary
  • Ptolemy's theorem: \(AC\cdot BD = AB\cdot CD + BC\cdot DA\)
  • Brahmagupta's formula: \(\Delta = \sqrt{(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d)}\) with \(s = \frac{a+b+c+d}{2}\)
  • a diagonal is a chord subtending equal angles: \(AC = 2R\sin\angle ABC = 2R\sin\angle ADC\)
  • sides as chords: \(AB = 2R\sin\angle ACB\) etc.
  • angle between diagonal \(AC\) and side \(AB\) is \(\angle CAB\), which faces side \(BC\)
  • a rectangle is cyclic with circumradius \(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{a^2+b^2}\)
Example 1.

In a cyclic quadrilateral \(ABCD\), \(AB = 6, BC = 8, CD = 10, DA = 12\). Find \(AC\cdot BD\).

  1. Ptolemy: \(AC\cdot BD = AB\cdot CD + BC\cdot DA\)
  2. \(AC\cdot BD = 6\times10 + 8\times12\)
  3. \(AC\cdot BD = 60 + 96\)

Answer: \(AC\cdot BD = 156\)

Example 2.

Find the area of a cyclic quadrilateral with sides \(6, 8, 10, 12\).

  1. \(s = \frac{6+8+10+12}{2} = 18\)
  2. Brahmagupta: \(\Delta = \sqrt{(s-6)(s-8)(s-10)(s-12)}\)
  3. \(\Delta = \sqrt{12\times10\times8\times6} = \sqrt{5760}\)

Answer: \(\Delta = 24\sqrt{10}\)

Common mistakes.
  • applying Brahmagupta's formula to quadrilaterals that are not cyclic
  • writing Ptolemy with the wrong pairing of opposite sides
  • assuming every parallelogram is cyclic (only rectangles are)
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 11

Quadrilateral

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 10 of 16

Explanation

Concept. For a general quadrilateral, solutions proceed by splitting it along a diagonal into two triangles and applying the sine and cosine rules in each; the total area is the sum of the two triangle areas, and Bretschneider's formula gives the area directly from the sides and one pair of opposite angles.
Key formula(s). \[ \Delta = \frac{1}{2}AB\cdot AD\sin A + \frac{1}{2}BC\cdot CD\sin C,\qquad \Delta^2 = (s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d) - abcd\cos^2\frac{A+C}{2} \]
Key points.
  • a quadrilateral has \(4\) sides and \(4\) angles, with sum of angles \(2\pi\)
  • one diagonal splits it into two triangles; the diagonal length follows from the cosine rule
  • area = sum of areas of the two triangles sharing that diagonal
  • Bretschneider's formula: \(\Delta^2 = (s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d) - abcd\cos^2\frac{A+C}{2}\)
  • when \(A+C = \pi\) (cyclic), Bretschneider reduces to Brahmagupta's formula
  • a tangential quadrilateral (one with an incircle) satisfies \(a+c = b+d\)
  • cyclic and tangential conditions are independent: a bicentric quadrilateral satisfies both
Example 1.

A quadrilateral has \(AB = 5, AD = 6\) with \(A = 60^\circ\). Find the diagonal \(BD\).

  1. in \(\triangle ABD\), the included angle between \(AB\) and \(AD\) is \(A = 60^\circ\)
  2. \(BD^2 = AB^2 + AD^2 - 2(AB)(AD)\cos A\)
  3. \(BD^2 = 25 + 36 - 2(5)(6)\frac{1}{2} = 61 - 30 = 31\)

Answer: \(BD = \sqrt{31}\)

Example 2.

A tangential quadrilateral has \(AB = 3, BC = 4, CD = 5\). Find \(AD\).

  1. for a tangential quadrilateral the sums of opposite sides are equal
  2. \(AB + CD = BC + AD\)
  3. \(3 + 5 = 4 + AD\)

Answer: \(AD = 4\)

Common mistakes.
  • using cyclic quadrilateral formulas for a quadrilateral not known to be cyclic
  • forgetting the \(\cos^2\frac{A+C}{2}\) correction term in Bretschneider's formula
  • splitting the quadrilateral by the wrong diagonal and mixing up the triangles
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 12

M-N Theorem

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 11 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The m-n theorem connects the angle a cevian makes with a side to the tangents of the base angles when the side is divided in the ratio \(m : n\), providing a quick route for cevian problems without computing lengths.
Key formula(s). \[ \text{if } D\in BC,\ BD = m,\ DC = n,\ \angle ADC = \theta:\qquad (m+n)\cot\theta = n\cot B - m\cot C \]
Key points.
  • \(D\) lies on side \(BC\) and \(AD\) is the cevian from \(A\)
  • the theorem uses the cotangents of the two base angles \(B\) and \(C\)
  • with \(m = n\) (the median case) it reduces to \(\cot\theta = \frac{\cot B - \cot C}{2}\)
  • for the internal angle bisector, \(m : n = c : b\) from the angle bisector theorem
  • the theorem applies only when \(D\) divides the side internally
  • a negative value of \(\cot\theta\) simply signals an obtuse cevian angle
  • the altitude case \(AD \perp BC\) gives \(\theta = 90^\circ\), i.e. \(\cot\theta = 0\), so \(m\cot C = n\cot B\)
Example 1.

A median \(AD\) of \(\triangle ABC\) makes angle \(\theta\) with \(BC\). If \(B = 45^\circ\) and \(C = 60^\circ\), find \(\cot\theta\).

  1. for a median \(m = n\), so \((m+n)\cot\theta = n\cot B - m\cot C\)
  2. \(\cot\theta = \frac{\cot B - \cot C}{2} = \frac{1 - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{2}\)
  3. \(\cot\theta = \frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{2\sqrt{3}}\)

Answer: \(\cot\theta = \frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{2\sqrt{3}}\)

Example 2.

On side \(BC\) of \(\triangle ABC\), \(D\) is such that \(BD = 2, DC = 5\), and \(B = 30^\circ, C = 60^\circ\). Find \(\cot\theta\), where \(\theta = \angle ADC\).

  1. m-n theorem: \((2+5)\cot\theta = 5\cot C - 2\cot B\)
  2. \(\cot C = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}},\ \cot B = \sqrt{3}\)
  3. \(7\cot\theta = \frac{5}{\sqrt{3}} - 2\sqrt{3} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\)

Answer: \(\cot\theta = -\frac{1}{7\sqrt{3}}\)

Common mistakes.
  • swapping \(m\) and \(n\) in the two subtracted terms
  • matching the cotangent of the wrong base angle to the wrong segment
  • applying the theorem when \(D\) lies on the extension of the side rather than on it
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 13

Half-Angle Formulas

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 12 of 16

Explanation

Concept. Half-angle formulas express \(\sin\frac{A}{2}, \cos\frac{A}{2}, \tan\frac{A}{2}\) in terms of the sides through the semiperimeter \(s\); all the square roots are positive because each half-angle lies in \((0, 90^\circ)\).
Key formula(s). \[ \sin\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{bc}},\qquad \cos\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{s(s-a)}{bc}},\qquad \tan\frac{A}{2} = \frac{\Delta}{s(s-a)} = \frac{r}{s-a} \]
Key points.
  • semiperimeter \(s = \frac{a+b+c}{2}\)
  • since \(A < 180^\circ\), we have \(\frac{A}{2}\in(0,90^\circ)\), so all half-angle roots under \(\sqrt{}\) are positive
  • \(\sin\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{bc}}\) with cyclic analogues
  • \(\cos\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{s(s-a)}{bc}}\)
  • \(\tan\frac{A}{2} = \frac{r}{s-a}\), connecting with the inradius
  • multiplying the \(\sin\) and \(\cos\) forms gives \(\sin A = \frac{2\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}}{bc} = \frac{2\Delta}{bc}\)
  • \(\tan\frac{A}{2}\tan\frac{B}{2} + \tan\frac{B}{2}\tan\frac{C}{2} + \tan\frac{C}{2}\tan\frac{A}{2} = 1\) in any triangle
Example 1.

Triangle with sides \(a = 13, b = 14, c = 15\). Find \(\tan\frac{A}{2}\).

  1. \(s = \frac{13+14+15}{2} = 21\)
  2. \(\tan\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{s(s-a)}} = \sqrt{\frac{(21-14)(21-15)}{21(21-13)}}\)
  3. \(\tan\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{7\times6}{21\times8}} = \sqrt{\frac{42}{168}}\)

Answer: \(\tan\frac{A}{2} = \frac{1}{2}\)

Example 2.

In the same triangle \((s = 21, b = 14, c = 15)\), find \(\sin\frac{A}{2}\).

  1. \(\sin\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{bc}}\)
  2. \(\sin\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{7\times6}{14\times15}} = \sqrt{\frac{42}{210}}\)
  3. \(\sin\frac{A}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{5}}\)

Answer: \(\sin\frac{A}{2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\)

Common mistakes.
  • forgetting that \(s-a, s-b, s-c\) are positive only because of the triangle inequality
  • misplacing which factor belongs in the numerator of each formula
  • taking a negative root when half-angles must be acute
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 14

Area, Napier's and Projection

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 13 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The area functional \(\Delta\), Napier's analogies (tangent half-sum formulas), and the projection formula relate sides and angles; together with Heron's formula they form the working toolkit for solving triangles.
Key formula(s). \[ \Delta = \frac{1}{2}bc\sin A = \sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)} = rs = \frac{abc}{4R},\qquad a = b\cos C + c\cos B,\qquad \tan\frac{A-B}{2} = \frac{a-b}{a+b}\cot\frac{C}{2} \]
Key points.
  • area forms: \(\frac{1}{2}bc\sin A\), Heron \(\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}\), \(\Delta = rs\), \(\Delta = \frac{abc}{4R}\)
  • projection formula: \(a = b\cos C + c\cos B\), i.e. a side is the sum of the projections of the other two
  • Napier's analogies: \(\tan\frac{A-B}{2} = \frac{a-b}{a+b}\cot\frac{C}{2}\), with cyclic versions
  • use \(\Delta = \frac{1}{2}ab\sin C\) when two sides and the included angle are known
  • Heron's formula needs only the three sides
  • \(\tan\frac{A+B}{2} = \cot\frac{C}{2}\) follows from \(A+B = \pi - C\)
Example 1.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(b = 8, c = 5, A = 60^\circ\). Find the area.

  1. \(\Delta = \frac{1}{2}bc\sin A = \frac{1}{2}\times8\times5\times\sin60^\circ\)
  2. \(\Delta = 20\times\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\)
  3. \(\Delta = 10\sqrt{3}\)

Answer: \(\Delta = 10\sqrt{3}\)

Example 2.

Find the area of the triangle with sides \(13, 14, 15\).

  1. \(s = \frac{13+14+15}{2} = 21\)
  2. Heron: \(\Delta = \sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)} = \sqrt{21\times8\times7\times6}\)
  3. \(\Delta = \sqrt{21\times336} = \sqrt{7056}\)

Answer: \(\Delta = 84\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(\Delta = \frac{1}{2}bc\sin A\) but using an angle not included between \(b\) and \(c\)
  • sign errors in the projection formula \(a = b\cos C + c\cos B\)
  • ordering the factors wrongly inside Heron's square root
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 15

Introduction

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 14 of 16

Explanation

Concept. Solving a triangle means determining all sides and angles from the given data; the angle sum \(A+B+C = \pi\), the sine rule, and the cosine rule together recover every missing element of the triangle.
Key formula(s). \[ A+B+C = \pi,\qquad \frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B} = \frac{c}{\sin C} = 2R,\qquad a^2 = b^2 + c^2 - 2bc\cos A \]
Key points.
  • the six elements are sides \(a, b, c\) and opposite angles \(A, B, C\)
  • \(A + B + C = \pi\) always supplies one relation
  • three independent pieces of data determine a triangle
  • standard cases: SAS, ASA/AAS, SSS give unique triangles; SSA can be ambiguous
  • in a right triangle at \(C\): \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\), \(\sin A = \frac{a}{c}\), \(\cos A = \frac{b}{c}\)
  • complementary identities: \(\sin A = \sin(B+C)\), \(\cos A = -\cos(B+C)\)
  • use the sine rule when a side and its opposite angle are known, the cosine rule for two sides and an included angle
Example 1.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(A = 45^\circ, B = 60^\circ, b = 4\). Find \(C\) and \(a\).

  1. \(C = 180^\circ - 45^\circ - 60^\circ = 75^\circ\)
  2. sine rule: \(\frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B}\)
  3. \(a = 4\times\frac{\sin45^\circ}{\sin60^\circ} = 4\times\frac{\sqrt{2}/2}{\sqrt{3}/2}\)
  4. \(a = \frac{4\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{4\sqrt{6}}{3}\)

Answer: \(C = 75^\circ,\ a = \frac{4\sqrt{6}}{3}\)

Example 2.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(b = 2, c = 3, A = 60^\circ\). Find \(a\) by the cosine rule.

  1. \(a^2 = b^2 + c^2 - 2bc\cos A\)
  2. \(a^2 = 4 + 9 - 2(2)(3)\cos60^\circ\)
  3. \(a^2 = 13 - 6 = 7\)

Answer: \(a = \sqrt{7}\)

Common mistakes.
  • pairing a side in the sine rule with a non-opposite angle
  • forgetting \(A + B + C = \pi\), so the third angle is not found
  • using the cosine rule with the wrong included angle
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 16

Sine Rule

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 15 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The sine rule states that each side of a triangle is proportional to the sine of the angle opposite it, and the common ratio is twice the circumradius \(R\).
Key formula(s). \[ \frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B} = \frac{c}{\sin C} = 2R,\qquad a = 2R\sin A,\ b = 2R\sin B,\ c = 2R\sin C \]
Key points.
  • valid in every triangle: acute, right, or obtuse
  • sides are in the same ratio as opposite angles: \(a : b : c = \sin A : \sin B : \sin C\)
  • equal angles imply equal opposite sides, and vice versa
  • use it when two angles and one side (AAS/ASA) or two sides and one angle (SSA) are given
  • \(\frac{a+b}{c} = \frac{\sin A + \sin B}{\sin C}\), handy for ratio-based results
  • the extended form \(= 2R\) gives the circumradius from one side and its opposite angle
  • in one period, \(\sin(180^\circ - A) = \sin A\), so the sine rule never distinguishes the obtuse case by itself
Example 1.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(A = 30^\circ, B = 45^\circ, a = 2\). Find \(b\).

  1. \(\frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B}\)
  2. \(b = 2\times\frac{\sin45^\circ}{\sin30^\circ} = 2\times\frac{\sqrt{2}/2}{1/2}\)
  3. \(b = 2\sqrt{2}\)

Answer: \(b = 2\sqrt{2}\)

Example 2.

Prove \(\frac{a+b}{c} = \frac{\cos\frac{A-B}{2}}{\sin\frac{C}{2}}\).

  1. by the sine rule, \(\frac{a+b}{c} = \frac{\sin A + \sin B}{\sin C}\)
  2. \(\sin A + \sin B = 2\sin\frac{A+B}{2}\cos\frac{A-B}{2}\)
  3. \(\frac{A+B}{2} = \frac{\pi-C}{2}\), so \(\sin\frac{A+B}{2} = \cos\frac{C}{2}\), and \(\sin C = 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}\)
  4. \(\frac{a+b}{c} = \frac{2\cos\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A-B}{2}}{2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}}\)

Answer: \(\frac{a+b}{c} = \frac{\cos\frac{A-B}{2}}{\sin\frac{C}{2}}\)

Common mistakes.
  • matching a side with a non-opposite angle in the ratio
  • using the sine rule with SAS data (the cosine rule is required)
  • forgetting that \(\sin A = \sin(180^\circ - A)\) creates the ambiguous case
Back to Index
Index / Trigonometry
Chapter 35 · Topic 17

Cosine Rule

Solution of TrianglesTrigonometryTopic 16 of 16

Explanation

Concept. The cosine rule relates any side of a triangle to the other two sides and the included angle, generalizing Pythagoras' theorem to arbitrary angles.
Key formula(s). \[ a^2 = b^2 + c^2 - 2bc\cos A,\qquad \cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc} \]
Key points.
  • use the cosine rule with SAS data (two sides, included angle) or SSS data (three sides)
  • rearranged form: \(\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc}\), which recovers an angle from the sides
  • if \(A = 90^\circ\), the rule reduces to \(a^2 = b^2 + c^2\) (Pythagoras)
  • the opposite side is largest: \(a^2 > b^2 + c^2\) means \(A\) is obtuse
  • \(\cos A\) positive \(\Rightarrow\) acute angle, zero \(\Rightarrow\) right, negative \(\Rightarrow\) obtuse
  • the circumradius and area can both follow from the cosine rule result
  • cyclic versions apply for each vertex
Example 1.

Given sides \(a = 13, b = 14, c = 15\), find \(\cos A\).

  1. \(\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc}\)
  2. \(\cos A = \frac{14^2 + 15^2 - 13^2}{2\times14\times15}\)
  3. \(\cos A = \frac{196 + 225 - 169}{420} = \frac{252}{420}\)

Answer: \(\cos A = \frac{3}{5}\)

Example 2.

In \(\triangle ABC\), \(a = 5, b = 7, C = 60^\circ\). Find the side \(c\).

  1. \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab\cos C\)
  2. \(c^2 = 25 + 49 - 2(5)(7)\cos60^\circ\)
  3. \(c^2 = 74 - 70\times\frac{1}{2} = 74 - 35 = 39\)

Answer: \(c = \sqrt{39}\)

Common mistakes.
  • writing \(\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2ab}\) (the denominator uses the two sides enclosing \(A\))
  • applying the rule with the angle opposite a different side
  • forgetting the square root when solving for a side
